## Decisions and Dispositions

### Lopez v. Monterey County, 519 U.S. 9 (1996)

Argued October 8, 1996 | Decided November 6, 1996

From: a three-judge District Court order directing Monterey County to conduct judicial elections under the countywide, at-large arrangement while California’s claim that later state law controlled the arrangement remained unresolved.

Questions presented: whether a covered county must preclear a postbaseline voting change mandated by a noncovered State; whether the consolidation, designation, and laches defenses permit use of the countywide arrangement; and what remedial authority a § 5 enforcement court possesses while preclearance remains absent.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia* applies § 5 functionally to legally operative electoral administration and permits prospective nonuse of an unprecleared change, but does not decide whether local discretion is required.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

##### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The order authorizing the March 1996 countywide, at-large election is vacated. Monterey County may not administer that system unless every post-1968 change necessary to its operation has received § 5 preclearance.

The District Court shall determine the exact scope of California’s 1983 submission, identify the remaining unprecleared changes, and require expeditious submission through the forums Congress designated. It may not decide whether the governmental plan is substantively entitled to preclearance.

If no lawful arrangement can operate in time and an independent source of remedial authority permits temporary judicial action, the court may formulate a necessary, temporary plan of its own. The plan must comply with the Constitution and the Voting Rights Act, displace state law no further than necessary, and terminate when a lawful precleared arrangement becomes available.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full; Kennedy and Thomas as to the covered-change, designation, laches, enforcement-court, and remedial propositions | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for seven Justices; partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Kennedy | Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Declines to decide the pure no-local-discretion question; relies on the governmental source of the ordered plan and the enforcement court’s limited authority |

#### Decision of the Court

##### State-mandated voting changes

Holding: a covered political subdivision seeks to administer a voting change when its officials give that change operative effect within the subdivision. Section 5 therefore requires preclearance before Monterey County implements a postbaseline judicial-election change mandated by California, even if County officials possess no independent discretion over the policy.

Operative rule: the obligation turns on administration in the covered jurisdiction, not on whether the covered jurisdiction authored or freely selected the change. The noncovered State need not preclear its enactment in the abstract.

Reasoning: § 5 separately reaches a jurisdiction that enacts a change and one that seeks to administer a change. Administration concerns execution and operation. Adding a local-discretion condition would permit a noncovered State to bypass § 5 throughout its covered subdivisions even though affected voters experience the same electoral change.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not determine discriminatory purpose, retrogressive effect, or entitlement to preclearance. It imposes no statewide preclearance obligation on California and does not reach laws having no operative effect in a covered jurisdiction.

Legal basis: Voting Rights Act §§ 4, 5, and 14(c)(1).

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion, joined on this question by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a seven-Justice majority.

##### Covered change, designation, and laches

Holding: replacing nine district electorates with one countywide electorate is a change affecting voting. Monterey County’s published coverage designation remains operative unless the County obtains statutory bailout, and laches does not authorize future use of an unprecleared change.

Operative rule: a covered jurisdiction may not collaterally relitigate its operative § 4 designation in an enforcement action when Congress supplied bailout as the route out of coverage. Delay may affect retrospective relief, reliance interests, or transition timing, but it cannot convert an unprecleared change into a lawful prospective practice.

Reasoning: the consolidation changed district boundaries, the constituency entitled to elect each judge, and the method by which votes select officeholders. California’s 1983 submission may have reached the final ordinance, but it did not identify the preceding consolidation sequence and therefore cannot be treated as blanket preclearance.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide damages, the treatment of completed elections, a factual bailout claim, or the precise administrative effect of the 1983 submission.

Authority of the proposition: all nine Justices support these propositions.

##### Enforcement-court authority and temporary remedies

Holding: a § 5 enforcement court may not authorize elections under an unprecleared County or State plan. A court order does not transform a governmental policy choice into an exempt judicial plan.

Operative rule: a plan formulated wholly by the federal court may operate without separate § 5 preclearance only when independent remedial authority makes temporary judicial action necessary. The exception does not apply when the court carries forward material policy choices proposed, required, or previously selected by the County or State.

Reasoning: the March order reinstated the same countywide, at-large arrangement Monterey County had adopted and provided ordinary six-year judicial terms. It was therefore a governmental plan placed into operation by judicial order, not a confined judicial stopgap.

Limits and questions left open: any court-developed plan remains subject to all other constitutional and statutory constraints. The Court neither selects an interim system nor expresses a view on substantive preclearance.

Authority of the proposition: all nine Justices support these propositions.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s holdings that nondiscretionary administration is covered, the consolidation remains subject to § 5, prospective nonuse cannot be defeated by laches, and the enforcement court may employ only a genuinely judicial and independently authorized temporary remedy.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia* is extended from state-authorized electoral administration to nondiscretionary implementation of a voting change mandated by a noncovered State. Its prospective nonuse rule is clarified to prevent an enforcement court from placing an unprecleared governmental choice into operation. The designated preclearance forums’ substantive authority remains unchanged.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical 1996 Court also reversed unanimously, but it confined the decision to the District Court’s remedial error and reserved the state-mandate, designation, laches, and covered-change questions. The simulation resolves those preserved issues because the grant is broader and the supplied record permits legal decision without additional factfinding. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

The simulated no-discretion holding anticipates the real-world 1999 decision, which was joined by O’Connor, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Here Stone supplies a seventh vote. Kennedy and Thomas join the narrower reversal because the ordered arrangement remained an unprecleared governmental plan, without accepting the pure no-discretion proposition. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/525bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the historical 1996 disposition, the consolidation record, and the later real-world treatment of state-mandated changes. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### Tyler Murray v. Montrose County School District RE-1J, 51 F.3d 921

Argued October 7, 1996 | Decided December 2, 1996

From: the Tenth Circuit’s affirmance of judgment for the school district. The later November 1993 IEP continued Tyler’s placement at Olathe, mooting the factual dispute over the superseded proposed transfer.

Question presented: whether IDEA’s least-restrictive-environment requirement creates an independent rebuttable presumption that the child must be educated in the neighborhood school.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Florence County School District Four v. Carter* governs reimbursement after denial of a free appropriate public education but supplies no geographic placement presumption.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Scope of the live controversy

The old dispute over Tyler’s proposed transfer and the request for additional evidence concerning that superseded IEP are moot. The annually recurring placement process, however, presents a reasonable prospect that Tyler will again face the same preserved legal question before ordinary review can be completed. The Court therefore reaches only the asserted neighborhood-school presumption.

##### Neighborhood-school placement under IDEA

Holding: IDEA creates no independent rebuttable presumption that the least restrictive environment is the child’s neighborhood school.

Operative rule: education with nondisabled children to the maximum extent appropriate remains the controlling statutory command. The responsible team must consider proximity, place the child as close to home as practicable, and ordinarily use the school the child would attend if nondisabled when the IEP can be implemented there. Those requirements are mandatory placement considerations, not a burden-shifting rule overriding an individualized IEP that requires another arrangement.

Reasoning: the statute identifies removal from regular education with nondisabled children as the form of restriction Congress sought to reduce. It does not equate every transfer between public-school buildings with greater educational restriction. The regulations expressly preserve a different placement when the IEP requires it.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not permit convenience-based transfers, make travel distance irrelevant, diminish the duty to consider supplementary aids before removal from regular classes, or determine whether Northside or Olathe was educationally appropriate under the old IEP.

Legal basis: IDEA and its then-effective placement regulations.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground. He joins the distinction between IDEA’s integration command and the regulations’ mandatory but nonpresumptive treatment of geographic proximity.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision adds the first controlling simulated rule concerning IDEA neighborhood-school placement. *Florence County* and its reimbursement rule remain unchanged.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulation affirms the Tenth Circuit’s central legal conclusion while expressly preserving the independent force of the proximity regulations. The lower court likewise treated the later Olathe IEP as eliminating the need to resolve the old placement’s factual adequacy. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/51/921/618583/))

Research sources: the Tenth Circuit opinion for Tyler’s placement history, the issue preserved, the regulatory treatment, and mootness. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/51/921/618583/))

### In re Askin, 47 F.3d 100

Argued October 8, 1996 | Decided December 9, 1996

From: the Fourth Circuit’s affirmance of a civil-contempt judgment against a trial witness who refused questions he believed were derived from warrantless monitoring of his own telephone conversations.

Questions presented: whether former Title III covered acquisition of the cordless radio segment; whether a noncordless caller automatically loses Fourth Amendment protection because the other participant uses a vulnerable cordless handset; and whether directly derived questions may support civil confinement for contempt.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Landgraf v. USI Film Products* requires use of the statutory definitions in force when the interception occurred. *United States v. Pinson* and *United States v. Wellons* reject categorical technological and derivative-loss rules in materially different settings but do not control telephone interception.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Former Title III construction | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The former cordless-radio exclusion governs the completed interception. |
| Categorical Fourth Amendment ruling | Reversed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Kennedy | The lower court may not treat the other participant’s handset as automatically eliminating Askin’s own privacy interest. |
| Civil-contempt judgment | Vacated and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Knowledge, derivation, and lawful exceptions must be determined question by question. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full; Scalia and Thomas as to former Title III and the Fourth Amendment; O’Connor and Kennedy as to former Title III | Supports vacatur and remand | Varying majorities as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Opposes vacatur of contempt | Joins the statutory and Fourth Amendment holdings but rejects the constitutional just-cause remedy |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Kennedy | Opposes the Fourth Amendment and contempt rulings | Joins only the former-Title-III holding |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Former Title III coverage

Holding: former Title III excluded the cordless radio segment acquired here, including both speakers’ words. Congress’s later deletion of that exclusion may not be applied retroactively to completed surveillance.

Reasoning: acquisition occurred at the radio segment between the cordless handset and its base unit. Statutory noncoverage of that segment does not itself authorize acquisition at another point or answer the Fourth Amendment question.

Limits and questions left open: the holding concerns only the superseded statutory text and does not govern interceptions occurring after the 1994 amendment.

Legal basis: former 18 U.S.C. §§ 2510 and 2511; *Landgraf*.

Authority of the proposition: all nine Justices.

##### Fourth Amendment protection of the noncordless caller

Holding: a person using a landline or cellular telephone does not lose Fourth Amendment protection merely because the other participant, without that caller’s knowledge, uses a vulnerable cordless handset.

Operative rule: participant consent, a willing informant, or knowing exposure presents a different case. Absent those conditions, independent government interception of the caller’s own words through an unknown technological vulnerability is not justified solely by the signal’s technical accessibility.

Reasoning: *White* assigns a speaker the risk that an interlocutor will reveal or voluntarily transmit what was said. It does not establish voluntary exposure when neither participant cooperates with the Government and the claimant did not know that the other endpoint retransmitted the conversation by readily intercepted radio. Fourth Amendment interests remain personal rather than merging into a unitary conversation owned by the least-protected participant.

Application: the record does not establish whether Askin knew that Brumbaugh was using a cordless handset. The categorical ruling against him therefore cannot stand.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide Brumbaugh’s own privacy interest, protect a caller who knowingly accepts the cordless retransmission, or disturb participant-consent cases.

Authority of the proposition: Souter’s opinion is joined on this question by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing a seven-Justice majority.

##### Just cause and civil contempt

Holding: when trial questions are direct fruits of an unlawful interception of the witness’s own calls, and no independent source, attenuation, objectively reasonable reliance, or other lawful basis permits the questions, refusal constitutes just cause under § 1826(a).

Operative rule: derivation must be determined question by question. The rule does not bar independently sourced questioning and does not create general testimonial immunity.

Reasoning: in this setting the Government seeks to imprison the intercepted speaker for refusing questions obtained directly through the unconstitutional acquisition of his own communications. The direct connection between the violation and the coercive use supplies a substantial remedial justification, while ordinary causation limits and good-faith principles prevent an unbounded exclusionary rule.

Limits and questions left open: the decision is confined to direct-fruit use against the intercepted speaker at trial. It does not decide the treatment of an ordinary grand-jury witness, remote derivative evidence, or questions supported independently of the surveillance.

Authority of the proposition: Souter’s opinion, joined on this issue by Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a five-Justice majority.

##### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The contempt judgment is vacated. The lower court shall determine whether Askin knew of or knowingly exposed the cordless retransmission, whether each refused question derived from the interception, and whether an independent source, attenuation, objectively reasonable reliance, or another lawful ground permits the question.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: vacate the civil-contempt judgment and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s separation of former statutory noncoverage from constitutional permission, its individualized protection of the unaware noncordless caller, and its confined direct-fruit rule under § 1826(a).

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Landgraf* is applied without alteration. *Pinson* and *Wellons* are applied by analogy but not enlarged beyond their existing subjects. The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for interception of a caller’s communication through another participant’s vulnerable endpoint and the first direct-fruit just-cause rule in this trial-contempt setting.

#### Historical Comparison

The Fourth Circuit affirmed contempt after treating the conversation as a single unit and the cordless radio signal as defeating both parties’ privacy claims. The simulation affirms its former-Title-III construction but rejects that categorical Fourth Amendment treatment and returns the unresolved knowledge and derivation questions for lawful findings. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/47/100/472728/))

Research sources: the Fourth Circuit opinion for the interception method, statutory construction, trial testimony, questions refused, and contempt judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/47/100/472728/))

### Caterpillar Inc. v. Lewis, 519 U.S. 61 (1996)

Argued November 12, 1996 | Decided December 10, 1996

From: the Sixth Circuit’s decision vacating a federal jury judgment because complete diversity did not exist when Caterpillar removed and the District Court erroneously denied Lewis’s timely remand motion.

Question presented: whether that initial removal defect requires destruction of the completed judgment when complete diversity arose lawfully before trial and remained through judgment.

Controlling simulated law before decision: no simulated holding directly determines the effect of a removal defect cured before trial. Entering law distinguishes the statutory duty to remand while a defect exists from the constitutional and statutory jurisdiction supporting a later merits judgment.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded with instructions to reinstate the District Court’s judgment, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Justice Ginsburg delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Removal defect cured before trial

Holding: an erroneous refusal to remand does not invalidate a later diversity judgment when complete diversity arises lawfully and noncollusively before trial and continues through final judgment.

Operative rule: the District Court must remand while the jurisdictional defect remains. Once complete diversity has arisen before the merits trial, however, a completed judgment supported throughout the merits proceeding by § 1332 is not destroyed solely to sanction the earlier removal violation.

Reasoning: Lewis timely sought remand and the District Court’s initial ruling was wrong. Before trial, Whayne left the case through an ordinary settlement, complete diversity arose, and the federal court thereafter possessed adjudicatory authority. Vacatur would not enforce a jurisdictional limitation present at trial or judgment; it would impose a judgment-destroying consequence Congress did not attach to the earlier error.

Limits and questions left open: finality cannot cure absent subject-matter jurisdiction, collusive manufacture of diversity, or improper manipulation. Any independently authorized fee or sanction remedy for the wrongful removal remains separate.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. §§ 1332, 1441, 1446, and 1447.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and direct reinstatement of the federal judgment.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the distinction between the mandatory remand duty while the defect existed and the validity of a merits trial conducted only after complete diversity had arisen.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule governing a removal defect cured before trial. It does not modify the rule that a judgment entered without subject-matter jurisdiction cannot stand.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, and controlling rule match the historical decision. The official volume records a unanimous reversal and remand in an opinion by Justice Ginsburg. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the removal chronology, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### M.L.B. v. S.L.J., 519 U.S. 102 (1996)

Argued October 7, 1996 | Decided December 16, 1996

From: dismissal of an indigent parent’s first appeal from a decree permanently terminating parental rights because she could not prepay approximately $2,352 for preparation of the record.

Question presented: whether due process and equal protection permit a State to offer appellate review of permanent termination while making the record indispensable to that review unavailable solely because of indigency.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *A. St. P. C. v. B. C.* and *Baby Richard v. Kirchner* recognize heightened procedural protection in termination-like family-status proceedings but do not establish a general appellate fee-waiver rule.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

Mississippi shall permit M.L.B.’s first appeal without prepayment and shall provide a record of sufficient completeness or another adequate equivalent. The state courts retain responsibility for deciding the termination appeal.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Kennedy | None | Supports reversal and remand | Rests on the procedural protection required before permanent destruction of parental status |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would affirm | Rejects extension of the indigent-access cases beyond their established settings |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Indigent access to the first termination appeal

Holding: a State violates due process and equal protection when it denies an indigent parent meaningful first appellate review of a decree permanently terminating parental rights solely because the parent cannot prepay the cost of the record necessary for that review.

Operative rule: a State need not create a civil appeal, subsidize ordinary private litigation, or provide every requested transcript. Once it creates a first appeal from a judgment permanently and exclusively altering the legal parent-child relationship, it must furnish a transcript, settled narrative, agreed statement, or another adequate equivalent when indigency makes the necessary record otherwise unavailable.

Reasoning: termination is an irreversible exercise of state power over a legal status the State defines and enforces. The underlying proof burden is elevated, the consequence is permanent, and the appellate court cannot test the sparse decree without an adequate account of the evidence and rulings. The accommodation is confined to making the State’s own first-level error-correction process meaningful in this exceptional class of civil judgments.

Limits and questions left open: the decision creates no general right to a civil appeal, free discovery, appointed appellate counsel, or publicly financed litigation concerning money, property, or ordinary custody scheduling. It expresses no view on parental fitness or the merits of the decree.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process and Equal Protection Clauses.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s narrow rule for meaningful first appellate review of permanent termination decrees.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision adds an appellate-access rule without changing the proof requirement established in *A. St. P. C.* or the transition-process rule established in *Baby Richard*. Those decisions supply context for the gravity of the family-status deprivation but not the fee-waiver mechanics.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical judgment was 6-3. In the simulation, Stone joins Justice Ginsburg’s opinion rather than reproducing the historical Chief Justice’s dissent, producing a 7-2 judgment. Kennedy remains a concurrence in the judgment, and Scalia and Thomas remain in dissent. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the termination decree, record cost, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### United States v. Sahhar, 56 F.3d 1026

Argued October 9, 1996 | Decided December 16, 1996

From: the Ninth Circuit’s reversal of an order releasing Sahhar after his federal confinement exceeded the five-year maximum sentence for the dismissed charge.

Question presented: whether § 4246 permits continued civil commitment based on current mental illness and dangerousness beyond that hypothetical maximum sentence, and what continuing review, release, and state-placement safeguards are required.

Controlling simulated law before decision: no simulated holding directly governs § 4246. Existing due-process decisions require the nature and duration of civil custody to remain tied to its current lawful predicate.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The dismissal of the indictment remains undisturbed. The District Court shall conduct a contemporaneous inquiry into the statutory predicates and Arizona’s response to renewed placement efforts.

Justice Breyer delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Duration and safeguards under § 4246

Holding: § 4246 permits civil commitment beyond the maximum possible sentence for the dismissed charge because continued confinement rests on current mental disease or defect causing current dangerousness, not on punishment for the charged offense.

Operative rule: continued federal custody requires clear and convincing proof of the statutory illness-and-dangerousness predicate, meaningful periodic review, practical routes to conditional or unconditional release, and release when the predicate ends. A prior or stale finding is insufficient.

Reasoning: the duration of a hypothetical criminal sentence does not measure recovery from a present mental disorder. At the same time, incompetence alone cannot authorize indefinite detention. Sections 4246 and 4247 preserve counsel, neutral adjudication, an elevated proof requirement, recurring review, and release when illness-caused danger no longer exists.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not permit confinement based on incompetence alone, dangerousness without qualifying mental disease, nominal review, or hypothetical future risk.

##### State-placement obligation

Holding: the Attorney General must make reasonable efforts, renewed periodically, to transfer responsibility to an eligible State. Transfer becomes mandatory when a State accepts responsibility for custody, care, and treatment.

Application: Arizona has not been asked to reassess responsibility in light of the final dismissal of the federal charge. Its present position must therefore be determined before continued federal retention is ordered.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not direct Arizona to accept custody or authorize federal retention after a lawful state acceptance.

##### Equal protection and preserved scope

Holding: the custody-defined federal class and the interests in safely concluding lawful federal custody, treatment, and public protection are sufficiently related even under intermediate scrutiny. The Court does not select the ultimate tier because the statute satisfies the more demanding standard considered.

Question not reached: the separately asserted enumerated-power objection is not decided because preservation is not established.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. §§ 4246 and 4247; Fifth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s current-predicate rule, its continuing state-transfer obligation, and its refusal to use the hypothetical maximum sentence as a civil-commitment cap.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for continued federal civil commitment under § 4246. It preserves the broader due-process principle that civil confinement must remain tied to a current lawful predicate and establishes no freestanding federal power to commit persons outside the statute’s custody-defined class.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulation affirms the Ninth Circuit’s central conclusion that continuing illness and dangerousness, rather than the dismissed charge’s maximum sentence, govern duration. It adds an express contemporaneous-review direction and gives operative force to the statute’s repeated state-placement requirement. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/56/1026/623920/))

Research sources: the Ninth Circuit opinion for the custody history, statutory scheme, sentence issue, and state-placement posture. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/56/1026/623920/))

### DeAngelis v. El Paso Municipal Police Officers’ Association, 51 F.3d 591

Argued October 15, 1996 | Decided January 6, 1997

From: the Fifth Circuit’s reversal of jury verdicts for hostile environment and retaliation arising from repeated sex-derogatory columns in the police association’s widely distributed official newsletter.

Questions presented: whether the evidence was statutorily sufficient under *Harris*; whether Title VII damages based on the Association’s own publication would violate the First Amendment; and whether the complaint-related publications caused a cognizable retaliatory consequence.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Harris v. Forklift Systems* requires a severe or pervasive environment under the complete circumstances and makes no single consideration indispensable. *Hurley* protects private control over an expressive presentation but does not immunize independently unlawful employment conduct.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Hostile-environment verdict | Affirmed in favor of the Association, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Judgment for the Association remains in force. |
| Retaliation verdict | Affirmed in favor of the Association, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | Judgment for the Association remains in force. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment and Opinion of the Court in part | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas as to hostile-environment insufficiency; every Justice as to retaliation | Supports affirmance | Four-Justice plurality on hostile environment; unanimous Court opinion on retaliation |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone | None | Supplies the fifth vote to affirm the hostile-environment component | Agrees the evidence is statutorily sufficient but would bar this publication-only damages judgment under the First Amendment; joins the retaliation holding |
| Dissent in part | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Would reinstate the hostile-environment verdict | Agrees with Stone on statutory sufficiency but rejects his constitutional ground; joins the retaliation holding |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Majority-supported statutory proposition

Five Justices agree that a rational jury could find the Association’s conduct severe or pervasive under *Harris*. Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer rely on the recurring distribution to more than 700 workplace colleagues, the duration and sex-based content, the references to DeAngelis, reported humiliation and loss of professional authority, promotion deterrence, and the workplace reaction.

Those five Justices also agree that physical threat, sexual advances, supervisory status, measurable performance loss, or psychological injury is not an indispensable requirement under *Harris*.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s concurrence and Stevens’s dissent, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, support the same statutory proposition at the same operative level of generality. It is an actual majority proposition, although it does not supply the judgment for the Association.

##### Hostile-environment judgment

No controlling rationale under *Marks*: O’Connor’s four-Justice plurality would hold the evidence statutorily insufficient. Stone concludes that the evidence is sufficient but that damages resting solely on protected editorial opinion or satire, without an unprotected category or independently discriminatory employment conduct, are constitutionally impermissible. Neither rationale is a logical subset of the other.

The judgment for the Association therefore binds the parties, but the decision establishes no controlling First Amendment rule for publication-based hostile-environment liability.

##### Retaliation

Holding: hostile references to a discrimination complaint and a conditional statement that the Association might sue if the complaint proved groundless do not establish Title VII retaliation when the claimant suffers no material employment or membership disadvantage.

Reasoning: the record shows no loss of pay, promotion, assignment, authority, representation, membership, or another cognizable employment or associational benefit. Opposition and litigation rhetoric alone do not complete the statutory claim.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not confine retaliation to ultimate employment decisions or foreclose liability for threats, harassment, or public shaming that materially deter protected activity on a stronger record.

Authority of the proposition: unanimous.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm both judgment components.

Public role: joins the Court’s retaliation holding and concurs in the judgment on hostile environment.

Announced ground: Stone states that the jury had a sufficient basis under *Harris*, but concludes that this particular damages judgment punishes the viewpoint of the Association’s own protected opinion and satire without a true threat, discriminatory directive, denial of an employment benefit, or other independently unlawful employment conduct. That constitutional proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor’s plurality would resolve the hostile-environment claim solely through statutory insufficiency. Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, would reinstate the verdict because the evidence permits liability under *Harris* and the First Amendment does not categorically exempt expression forming the discriminatory workplace environment. Neither proposition commands the judgment-supporting majority.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Harris* is applied without alteration. Its totality inquiry and rejection of indispensable factors are reaffirmed by five Justices. The decision creates no controlling First Amendment exception to Title VII because Stone’s constitutional rationale attracts no additional vote.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Fifth Circuit also entered judgment for the Association, but it treated the evidence as insufficient and avoided the constitutional question. In the simulation, five Justices reject that statutory analysis; the same result survives only because Stone supplies the fifth affirming vote on a distinct and noncontrolling First Amendment ground. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/51/591/618202/))

Research sources: the Fifth Circuit opinion for the newsletter’s content and distribution, workplace response, asserted effects, statutory analysis, and preserved constitutional concern. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/51/591/618202/))

### United States v. Watts; United States v. Putra, 519 U.S. 148 (1997)

Submitted without oral argument | Decided January 6, 1997

From: separate Ninth Circuit judgments vacating sentences enhanced through conduct underlying counts on which the juries returned acquittals.

Question presented: whether the Double Jeopardy Clause, Due Process Clause, and jury-trial guarantee categorically prohibit Guidelines findings based on acquitted conduct.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *United States v. Gaudin* reserves offense elements to the jury and requires proof beyond a reasonable doubt, but no simulated holding categorically prevents consideration of acquitted conduct within the punishment authorized by the conviction.

#### Judgment

| Docket | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *United States v. Watts* | Reversed and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens, Kennedy | Resentencing under ordinary Guidelines, Rule 32, and evidentiary requirements |
| *United States v. Putra* | Reversed and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens, Kennedy | Resentencing under ordinary Guidelines, Rule 32, and evidentiary requirements |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Per curiam Opinion of the Court | The Court | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal in both cases | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | None | Supports reversal | Emphasizes the distinction between conviction elements and facts considered within the conviction-authorized punishment |
| Concurrence | Breyer | None | Supports reversal | Notes that the Sentencing Commission remains free to alter its treatment of acquitted conduct |
| Dissent | Stevens | None | Would affirm the Ninth Circuit | Treats use of acquitted offenses to determine the mandatory range as inconsistent with the verdict and statutory design |
| Dissent | Kennedy | None | Opposes summary reversal | Concludes that the issue warrants full briefing and argument and expresses concern about undercutting acquittals |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Acquitted conduct at Guidelines sentencing

Holding: a general acquittal does not categorically prevent a sentencing court from considering the underlying conduct when reliable evidence proves that conduct by a preponderance and the resulting sentence remains within the punishment legally authorized by the conviction.

Operative rule: the sentencing court must comply with the Guidelines, Rule 32, ordinary reliability requirements, and the governing burden of proof. An accusation is not evidence. The rule does not permit the judge to remove an offense element from the jury or exceed the punishment authorized by the conviction.

Reasoning: a general verdict establishes that the prosecution failed to prove the charged offense beyond a reasonable doubt. It does not identify the element on which proof failed or establish that every underlying fact was false. The same evidence may carry a different consequence under a lower burden and a distinct legal inquiry. Section 3661 and the Guidelines do not create a special acquittal-preclusion rule.

Application: the Ninth Circuit treated acquittal as a categorical factual determination. Each sentence must instead be reconsidered under ordinary sentencing procedures, without this categorical bar.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide any unresolved reliability objection, authorize punishment beyond the conviction-authorized range, require special verdicts, or excuse noncompliance with Rule 32.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. § 3661, the then-mandatory Sentencing Guidelines, and the constitutional distinction between elements and sentencing facts.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand in both cases.

Public role: joins the per curiam Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground. He joins the distinction between failure of proof beyond a reasonable doubt and sentencing proof under the lower burden within the punishment authorized by the conviction.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia emphasizes that the decision depends on remaining within the punishment authorized by the conviction. Breyer notes that the Commission may revise the Guidelines if it concludes that acquitted conduct should receive different treatment. Stevens rejects use of the acquitted offenses to determine the mandatory range. Kennedy’s dissent addresses both the institutional impropriety of summary disposition and the unresolved tension with the jury’s verdict.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Gaudin* is distinguished, not narrowed. Elements remain for the jury and require proof beyond a reasonable doubt. The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for acquitted conduct used to determine a Guidelines range.

#### Historical Comparison

The supplied brief listed the historical disposition as 8-1. The official volume records separate dissents by Justices Stevens and Kennedy, making the historical disposition 7-2. The simulation reproduces that 7-2 division, with Stone occupying the supporting position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the per curiam disposition, the concurrences, and both dissents. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### Old Chief v. United States, 519 U.S. 172 (1997)

Argued October 16, 1996 | Decided January 7, 1997

From: affirmance of a felon-in-possession conviction after the District Court rejected Old Chief’s unconditional admission to the complete status element and admitted a judgment identifying his prior offense as assault causing serious bodily injury.

Question presented: whether Rule 403 required comparative consideration of the admission and exclusion of the prior judgment’s name and nature.

Controlling simulated law before decision: no simulated holding directly governs Rule 403 treatment of an offered admission to a formal status element.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded for a new trial, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia and Thomas | Would affirm | Would preserve the prosecution’s ordinary authority to prove each element through evidence of its own choosing |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Rule 403 and a conceded status element

Holding: when a defendant offers an unconditional admission to the complete prior-felony status element and the Government identifies no other permissible purpose, Rule 403 requires exclusion of the prior judgment’s name and nature when their incremental probative value is substantially outweighed by the danger of unfair propensity prejudice.

Operative rule: evidentiary alternatives enter the Rule 403 comparison when they prove exactly the same discrete legal-status proposition. The Government ordinarily controls the narrative of its case, but that principle has little application when the admission conclusively supplies a formal status element unrelated to the narrative sequence of the charged conduct.

Reasoning: the relevant comparison is between two methods of proving the same point, not between evidence and no evidence. Old Chief’s admission would establish felony status without dispute. The assault judgment added vivid character information while doing no additional legitimate work concerning possession.

Application: the District Court exercised discretion under an erroneous conception of probative value. Given the prior offense’s violent nature and its potential interaction with the charged firearm conduct, the Government has not shown the error harmless.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not permit a defendant to stipulate away the narrative of the charged event, compel acceptance of a qualified admission, or exclude prior acts offered for a genuinely disputed nonpropensity purpose.

Legal basis: Federal Rules of Evidence 401 and 403.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand for a new trial.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s comparative Rule 403 treatment of equally conclusive proof of a formal status element.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule requiring consideration of an equally conclusive evidentiary alternative under Rule 403 in the confined context of a conceded prior-felony status element.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical decision was 5-4. Stone joins Justice Souter’s majority rather than reproducing the historical Chief Justice’s dissent, producing a 6-3 judgment. The remaining opinion alignment is unchanged. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the evidentiary record, historical rule, judgment, and lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### Walmer v. Department of Defense, 52 F.3d 851

Argued October 16, 1996 | Decided January 13, 1997

From: affirmance of the denial of a preliminary injunction against discharge proceedings under an Army regulation requiring separation for homosexual acts.

Questions presented: what equal-protection standard governs the classification, how professional military judgment operates within that standard, and whether the executive military regulation is a bill of attainder.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Kelley v. Board of Trustees* reserved the scrutiny applicable to sexual-orientation classifications. *Romer v. Evans* invalidated a broad disability imposed on gay persons but did not assign heightened scrutiny. *United States v. Virginia* supplies the received intermediate-scrutiny form for intentional classifications requiring an actual important objective and a substantial, nonstereotyped fit.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Equal-protection denial of preliminary relief | Vacated and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Apply intermediate scrutiny and the ordinary preliminary-injunction factors on a developed record. |
| Bill of Attainder claim | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | Judgment for the Department remains in force. |

The Court does not itself order preliminary relief or finally adjudicate the validity of AR 635-100.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Breyer as to equal protection; every Justice as to the Bill of Attainder claim | Supports vacatur in part, affirmance in part, and remand | Five-Justice equal-protection holding; unanimous Bill of Attainder holding |
| Dissent in part | Kennedy | O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas | Would affirm the equal-protection component | Would apply rational-basis review with substantial military deference |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Equal protection and the military discharge rule

Holding: a military rule mandating separation specifically for homosexual acts intentionally classifies by sexual orientation and is governed by intermediate scrutiny.

Operative rule: the Government must establish that the classification serves an actual important military objective and is substantially related to that objective through a genuine, nonstereotyped fit. Professional military judgments and reasonable contemporaneous predictions concerning discipline, cohesion, readiness, security, and command receive substantial evidentiary weight. Deference does not lower the governing standard or satisfy the Government’s burden through assertion alone.

Reasoning: selecting homosexual acts as an automatic ground for separation uses the conduct category as the operative proxy for sexual orientation. Moral disapproval, private bias, and stereotype cannot supply the required fit. The lower courts instead applied rational-basis review and placed the practical burden on Walmer to disprove the policy. The incomplete preliminary-injunction record does not permit this Court to determine whether the Government can satisfy the proper standard.

Application: the equal-protection component is returned for development of the military record and application of the ordinary preliminary-injunction factors.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not invalidate every military regulation of sexual conduct, recognize a fundamental right to serve, overrule *Bowers v. Hardwick*, or substitute unsupported judicial prediction for professional military judgment.

Legal basis: Fifth Amendment equal protection.

Authority of the proposition: Ginsburg’s opinion, joined on this question by Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Breyer, supplies a five-Justice majority.

##### Bill of Attainder Clause

Holding: the Bill of Attainder Clause does not govern an executive military regulation.

Reasoning: Article I prohibits legislative identification and punishment without judicial trial. AR 635-100 is an executive regulation. Any constitutional or statutory defect remains reviewable through equal protection, due process, administrative law, or another applicable source, but not through the branch-specific Bill of Attainder prohibition.

Limits and questions left open: because the institutional requirement is absent, the Court does not decide whether the regulation otherwise inflicts punishment in the constitutional sense.

Authority of the proposition: unanimous.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: vacate and remand the equal-protection component; affirm the Bill of Attainder judgment.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground. He joins the assignment of intermediate scrutiny, the substantial but nonconclusive weight given to professional military judgment, and the institutional limit of the Bill of Attainder Clause.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas, concludes that *Romer* did not assign heightened scrutiny, that the regulation classifies prohibited conduct rather than orientation as such, and that the preliminary record and traditional military deference require affirmance.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision resolves the scrutiny question expressly reserved in *Kelley* for a military rule mandating separation on the basis of homosexual acts. The received intermediate-scrutiny formulation from *United States v. Virginia* is applied to that classification, with professional military judgment operating as weighty evidence rather than a replacement standard.

*Romer* remains unchanged at its own scope. *Bowers* remains controlling on the asserted fundamental-conduct claim and is not extended to determine the separate equality standard.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Tenth Circuit affirmed denial of preliminary relief under rational-basis precedent and rejected the Bill of Attainder claim because the regulation was executive. The simulation preserves the latter ruling but vacates the equal-protection component because the controlling simulated Court assigns intermediate scrutiny and requires a developed military showing under that standard. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/52/851/573233/))

Research sources: the Tenth Circuit opinion for the preliminary-injunction posture, governing regulation, lower-court scrutiny, and Bill of Attainder treatment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/52/851/573233/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Atherton v. Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, 519 U.S. 213 (1997)

Argued November 4, 1996 | Decided January 14, 1997

From: the Third Circuit’s application of a federal common-law ordinary-negligence standard to claims brought by the federal receiver of a federally chartered savings institution against former officers and directors.

Question presented: whether state law or a federal common-law negligence rule supplies the governing standard, and whether 12 U.S.C. § 1821(k) establishes a liability floor or a ceiling.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *O’Melveny & Myers v. FDIC* requires state law to govern an FDIC receiver’s state-law claims unless enacted federal law or a specific and significant conflict with an identifiable federal policy requires displacement. The official record confirms the receiver’s negligence and fiduciary-duty claims, the statutory text, and the historical disposition. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The court of appeals shall direct application of New Jersey law, subject to § 1821(k)’s federal gross-negligence minimum. Breach, causation, defenses, and available relief remain for the lower courts.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg in full; O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas except the legislative-history discussion | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion for six Justices; all portions except legislative history for three |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | O’Connor | Scalia and Thomas | Supports vacatur and remand | Relies on statutory text and structure without legislative history |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Governing law and § 1821(k)

Holding: state law ordinarily governs the duty of care owed by officers and directors of a federally chartered savings association. Section 1821(k) establishes gross negligence as a minimum federal basis for liability and preserves stricter applicable state standards.

Operative rule: a State may impose liability for simple negligence or another standard more demanding than gross negligence. State law may not insulate officers and directors from the federal gross-negligence minimum. Federal chartering, insurance, supervision, or receivership convenience does not by itself authorize a federal common-law ordinary-negligence rule.

Reasoning: the asserted duties concern lending judgment, fiduciary administration, and corporate governance, subjects traditionally governed by state law. The FDIC identifies no operational conflict requiring displacement. Section 1821(k)’s preservation clause would perform little work if the statute were treated as a ceiling, while reading it as a floor gives effect to both the gross-negligence language and the preservation of rights under other applicable law.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not determine New Jersey’s business-judgment rule, whether Atherton breached any duty, or the standard governing a distinct federal enforcement action based on duties created by federal statute or regulation.

Legal basis: 12 U.S.C. § 1821(k), federal-common-law principles, and applicable state corporate law.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: vacate and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s application of *O’Melveny*, its rejection of a judge-made national negligence standard, and its construction of § 1821(k) as a federal floor preserving stricter state law.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*O’Melveny & Myers v. FDIC* is applied and clarified. Its state-law presumption governs traditional corporate duties even when the failed institution was federally chartered. Section 1821(k) supplies a specific federal minimum without displacing stricter state standards.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, and controlling rule match the historical decision. The same three Justices decline to join the portions relying on legislative history. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the receiver’s claims, statutory text, judgment, opinion lineup, and historical comparison. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### Elizabeth G. Curtis v. School Committee of Falmouth, 420 Mass. 749, 652 N.E.2d 580

Argued November 4, 1996 | Decided January 21, 1997

From: the Supreme Judicial Court of Massachusetts’ affirmance of summary judgment for the school committee. The challenged program made condoms voluntarily available to students in grades seven through twelve, without parental opt-out or notice. Junior-high students obtained them from a nurse after counseling; high-school students could use a nurse or restroom vending machines. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/massachusetts/supreme-court/volumes/420/420mass749.html))

Questions presented: whether voluntary availability infringes parental liberty or familial privacy, and whether it substantially burdens religious exercise.

Controlling simulated law before decision: the family-status protections recognized in *A. St. P. C. v. B. C.* and *Baby Richard v. Kirchner* do not establish parental control over every optional public-school service. *Swanner* and *Church of Scientology v. Clearwater* require a substantial governmental burden before religious-objection claims receive further review.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Parental liberty and familial privacy

Holding: a voluntary public-school condom-availability program does not violate parental liberty or familial privacy merely because it contains no parental opt-out or notification requirement.

Operative rule: parents retain primary authority to instruct their children on morality, religion, and sexual conduct. The State does not constitutionally displace that authority by making a nonprescription preventive item available when students need not obtain it, receive counseling, read materials, affirm a message, or suffer any consequence for refusing.

Reasoning: the program changes what is available at school, not the family’s legal relationship or the parents’ authority to instruct their children. Compulsory attendance does not make every optional school resource compulsory. A parent’s instruction may become more difficult when the school presents an option the parent rejects, but difficulty and offense are not coercive governmental interference.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide confidential medical treatment, prescription medication, compelled sex education, distribution to a child legally unable to consent, or a program that penalizes adherence to parental instruction.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment.

##### Free exercise

Holding: the program imposes no substantial burden on religious exercise because neither students nor parents are required to approve, obtain, or use condoms, and no educational benefit or disciplinary consequence depends on participation.

Reasoning: the Constitution protects against coerced religiously prohibited conduct and targeted disability. It does not require removal of every voluntary public program inconsistent with a person’s faith.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not govern religious targeting, compelled participation, or denial of an exemption from a mandatory practice.

Legal basis: Free Exercise Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the distinction between voluntary availability and governmental coercion.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule concerning voluntary school access to nonprescription preventive items. *A. St. P. C.* and *Baby Richard* remain confined to state action altering or implementing legal family status.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulated Court reaches the merits and unanimously adopts the central reasoning of the unanimous Massachusetts judgment, which treated the program as voluntary and noncoercive. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/massachusetts/supreme-court/volumes/420/420mass749.html))

Research sources: the Massachusetts opinion for the program’s operation, requested relief, constitutional theories, and lower-court judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/massachusetts/supreme-court/volumes/420/420mass749.html))

### Ingalls Shipbuilding, Inc. v. Director, Office of Workers’ Compensation Programs, 519 U.S. 248 (1997)

Argued November 12, 1996 | Decided February 18, 1997

From: the Fifth Circuit’s affirmance of a Benefits Review Board ruling that Mrs. Yates did not forfeit later death benefits by entering third-party settlements before her husband’s death, and that the Director of OWCP could appear as respondent.

Questions presented: whether a spouse is a “person entitled to compensation” under LHWCA § 33(g) before the worker’s death, and whether the Department of Labor may appear through the Director as respondent under Federal Rule of Appellate Procedure 15(a). The official record confirms that the spouse’s status at the time of settlement and the Department’s appellate representation were the two issues decided. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Predeath settlement and § 33(g) forfeiture | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | Mrs. Yates’s later death-benefit claim is not barred by the predeath settlements. |
| Director as respondent | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The Director may remain as the Department’s designated representative. |

The amount of benefits, work-related causation, and any other defenses remain for the agency process.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Every Justice as to the compensation issue; Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer as to Director participation | Supports affirmance | Unanimous on compensation; seven-Justice Court opinion on participation |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Supports the compensation judgment and opposes the participation ruling | Rejects the Director’s status as Rule 15(a) respondent |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Predeath spouse and § 33(g)

Holding: an injured worker’s spouse is not a “person entitled to compensation” for LHWCA death benefits before the worker dies from a qualifying cause. A predeath third-party settlement therefore does not trigger § 33(g)’s approval forfeiture for the later death-benefit claim.

Operative rule: entitlement is assessed when the settlement is made. A contingent possibility of future widowhood is not present legal entitlement, because the spouse may predecease or divorce the worker, or the worker may die from a nonqualifying cause.

Reasoning: the statute uses entitlement, not expectancy. The phrase “would be entitled” compares settlement value with compensation after the legal prerequisites for entitlement exist; it does not convert every potential future beneficiary into a present claimant. General concern about double recovery cannot add a forfeiture Congress did not enact.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not govern settlements entered after the worker’s death, vested disability benefits, or offsets expressly authorized elsewhere.

Legal basis: 33 U.S.C. §§ 909 and 933.

Authority of the proposition: unanimous.

##### Director as respondent

Holding: the Director of OWCP, acting as the Secretary’s designated representative of the Department of Labor, is a proper respondent under Rule 15(a) when a private party has invoked appellate jurisdiction to review a Benefits Review Board order.

Reasoning: the Board, administrative law judges, and Director perform different functions within the Department. The Board’s order is the Department’s order, and the Secretary may designate the Department’s appellate representative. Respondent status permits the Director to state the Department’s position, including disagreement with the Board, without giving the Director an independent right to initiate an appeal.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not reconsider *Newport News* or decide whether the Director alone can preserve an Article III controversy after all private adversity ends.

Legal basis: Federal Rule of Appellate Procedure 15(a), 33 U.S.C. § 921a, and Department regulations.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion, joined on this issue by Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a seven-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm both judgment components.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the temporal meaning of statutory entitlement and the distinction between initiating review and representing the Department once review exists.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Director, OWCP v. Newport News Shipbuilding & Dry Dock Co.* is distinguished. Its restriction on the Director’s initiation of review does not prevent respondent participation after a private party invokes appellate jurisdiction.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, author, and issue-specific votes match the historical decision. The compensation question is unanimous; Scalia and Thomas dissent only from the Director-participation ruling. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the settlement chronology, statutory holding, appellate-representation structure, judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### Uhlrig v. Harder, 64 F.3d 567

Argued November 5, 1996 | Decided February 18, 1997

From: the Tenth Circuit’s affirmance of summary judgment for state mental-health administrators in an individual-capacity § 1983 action arising from therapist Stephanie Uhlrig’s murder by a patient after closure of a secure forensic unit.

Question presented: whether the named administrators affirmatively created or intensified a specific danger to Uhlrig, and whether qualified immunity independently bars damages.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Pinder v. Johnson* requires a specific imminent threat, affirmative inducement and reliance, deliberate official use of authority materially increasing vulnerability, deliberate indifference, and causation. The lower-court record shows that clinical teams made the patient-placement decisions, Uhlrig received general and specific warnings, had access to the patient’s records, and agreed to work with him. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/64/567/631313/))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Constitutional merits and judgment |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Would resolve the damages action on qualified immunity without extending substantive due process |

#### Decision of the Court

##### State-created danger

Holding: the record does not establish the affirmative inducement, material increase in vulnerability, deliberate indifference, and defendant-specific causation required by *Pinder*.

Reasoning: closing the secure unit and delegating reassignment were affirmative policy decisions, but the named senior officials did not give Uhlrig a concrete assurance of safety, induce her to abandon a protective course, conceal Waddell’s history, or personally direct his placement into her care. The warnings, access to records, and delegated clinical process prevent the policy decision from becoming the particular affirmative sequence recognized in *Pinder*.

Application: summary judgment is proper because no reasonable factfinder could attribute the constitutionally required inducement and causal sequence to the named defendants. The Court therefore does not reach qualified immunity as to the seven-Justice merits coalition.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not protect an official who conceals a known imminent threat, promises safety and then directs an employee into the identified danger, or personally overrides safeguards with deliberate indifference.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment and 42 U.S.C. § 1983.

Authority of the proposition: Breyer’s opinion, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg, supplies a seven-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the application of *Pinder* and announces no additional ground.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would assume without deciding that the danger-creation theory is available and would affirm because no clearly established law in 1991 imposed personal damages on the senior administrators for the institutional decisions shown by this record.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Pinder v. Johnson* is applied without alteration. The decision confirms that an affirmative institutional policy change is not enough by itself; the claimant must prove the defendant-specific sequence of inducement, increased vulnerability, culpability, and causation required by *Pinder*.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulated Court affirms the historical Tenth Circuit judgment, but the seven-Justice majority uses the narrower controlling rule adopted in simulated *Pinder* rather than the Tenth Circuit’s freestanding multi-factor formulation. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/64/567/631313/))

Research sources: the Tenth Circuit opinion for the unit closure, placement process, warnings, patient history, summary-judgment record, and historical disposition. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/64/567/631313/))
