### Auer v. Robbins, 519 U.S. 452 (1997)

Argued December 10, 1996 | Decided February 19, 1997

From: the Eighth Circuit’s holding that St. Louis police sergeants and a lieutenant were salaried executive employees exempt from FLSA overtime requirements.

Questions presented: whether the salary-basis regulation validly applies to public employees, what “subject to reduction” means, and what weight an agency interpretation of its own genuinely ambiguous regulation receives.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala* independently construed the regulation there and expressly left own-regulation deference unresolved. The official *Auer* record presents a broad disciplinary manual, no general practice of deductions, one relevant deduction, the Secretary’s actual-practice or significant-likelihood construction, and a correction mechanism. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The officers remain classified as exempt executives, subject to reimbursement and prospective compliance required by the correction provision.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full; Stone as to the regulation’s public-sector validity and the duties determination | Supports affirmance | Eight-Justice Court opinion on own-regulation deference; unanimous result on validity and exemption |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone | None | Supports affirmance | Independently construes the regulation and rejects automatic controlling deference |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Validity of the salary-basis regulation

Holding: the salary-basis condition is a permissible implementation of the FLSA executive exemption as applied to public employees.

Reasoning: Congress gave the Secretary broad authority to define and delimit the exemption. A rule distinguishing fixed salary from compensation varying with work quantity or quality remains reasonably related to executive status despite the disciplinary needs of public-safety organizations.

Limits and questions left open: any procedural objection to the Secretary’s failure to amend the regulation after public employees became subject to the FLSA must be presented through the appropriate rulemaking process.

Legal basis: 29 U.S.C. § 213(a)(1) and the then-effective regulations.

Authority of the proposition: unanimous.

##### Interpretation of an agency’s own regulation

Holding: when ordinary interpretation leaves an agency regulation genuinely ambiguous, the agency’s authoritative interpretation controls unless it is plainly erroneous or inconsistent with the regulation, provided it reflects the agency’s fair and considered judgment rather than a convenient post hoc rationalization.

Operative rule: an interpretation may receive controlling weight even when presented in an amicus brief if it states the agency’s established, authoritative position and is not manufactured to defend the particular litigation. Deference cannot make the regulatory text bear a meaning it cannot reasonably carry.

Reasoning: “subject to reduction” comfortably permits a practical-exposure interpretation. The Secretary’s position reflects the agency’s administration of its own salary-basis rule and does not arise as an unexplained litigation invention.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not authorize deference to an inconsistent, secret, plainly erroneous, or merely convenient interpretation.

Legal basis: the regulation and the received *Seminole Rock* principle.

Authority of the proposition: Scalia’s opinion, joined on this question by Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies an eight-Justice majority. Stone does not join this proposition.

##### Salary status, correction, and duties

Holding: an employee is “subject to reduction” when an actual practice of inconsistent deductions exists or a clear and particularized policy creates a significant practical likelihood of such deductions. The general St. Louis manual and isolated deduction do not defeat salary status here.

Operative rule: an employer may preserve exempt status by reimbursing an improper deduction and committing to future compliance when the deduction was inadvertent or was made for a reason other than lack of work.

Reasoning: theoretical disciplinary authority is not enough. The manual did not communicate a recurring compensation practice specific to these officers, and the record showed no pattern of inconsistent reductions. Their undisputed supervisory responsibilities satisfy the executive-duties requirement.

Limits and questions left open: a recurring deduction practice or a clear policy specifically threatening salary reductions for ordinary work variations would present a different case. The separately asserted evidentiary-burden argument is not reached because it was not adequately preserved.

Authority of the proposition: Scalia’s opinion and Stone’s concurrence support the practical-exposure construction, correction mechanism, and case-specific exemption at the same operative level. Those propositions are unanimous, although the rationale concerning agency deference is not.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the public-sector-validity and duties portions of the Opinion of the Court and concurs in the judgment on regulatory interpretation and application.

Announced ground: Stone independently construes the regulation. He gives the Secretary’s authoritative, considered, adequately explained, fairly noticed, and expert interpretation special persuasive weight, but does not treat it as automatically controlling. Independent review reaches the same practical-exposure construction and the same judgment.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The question reserved in *Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala* is resolved. Eight Justices adopt controlling own-regulation deference when genuine ambiguity remains and the interpretation satisfies the limits stated above. Stone’s earlier persuasive-weight position remains noncontrolling. The salary-basis construction and correction rule are separately supported by all nine Justices.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed unanimously in a single opinion by Justice Scalia. The simulation preserves the unanimous judgment and substantive FLSA application, but Stone’s controlled position and his prior simulated *Thomas Jefferson University* concurrence produce an eight-Justice deference holding and a separate Stone concurrence. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the regulatory text, practical-exposure construction, correction provision, historical unanimity, and opinion authorship. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### Lynce v. Mathis, 519 U.S. 433 (1997)

Argued November 4, 1996 | Decided February 19, 1997

From: dismissal of a habeas petition challenging Florida’s retroactive cancellation of overcrowding release credits that had already been awarded and used to release Lynce, followed by his rearrest and return to custody.

Question presented: whether the cancellation increased the punishment attached to Lynce’s offense despite the credits’ administrative overcrowding purpose.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *California Department of Corrections v. Morales* requires a sufficient risk of increased punishment and rejects merely speculative or attenuated possibilities. The official record establishes 1,860 provisional credits, actual release, retroactive cancellation, and reincarceration. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The district court shall determine the amount of constitutionally protected credit under the correct temporal analysis and order release if Lynce’s lawful sentence has expired.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Thomas | Scalia | Supports reversal and remand | Relies on the narrower ground that the State canceled credits already accrued and used |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Retroactive cancellation of release credits

Holding: Florida violates the Ex Post Facto Clause when it retrospectively cancels release credits already awarded and used to release an inmate, and the cancellation lengthens actual confinement.

Operative rule: whether a retrospective change increases punishment turns on its objective operation, not on the administrative or penological motive for originally creating the credit. A credit that determines the lawful release date is part of the effective measure of confinement.

Reasoning: unlike the procedural change in *Morales*, the increase in Lynce’s punishment is neither speculative nor attenuated. Florida changed his release calculation by years and returned him to prison. Calling the credits an overcrowding device does not eliminate the punitive consequence of renewed confinement.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not protect credits never awarded or unavailable under the law governing the offense, and the exact amount attributable to materially different post-offense statutory provisions remains for remand.

Legal basis: Article I, § 10, Ex Post Facto Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s objective-operation analysis and its distinction of *Morales*.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Morales* is distinguished and clarified. Its sufficient-risk rule remains controlling, but actual reincarceration after retroactive cancellation of accrued release credits satisfies that rule directly.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, and opinion alignment match the historical decision. Thomas and Scalia rely on the narrower accrued-and-used-credit ground. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the credit statutes, release and rearrest, holding, remand issue, and separate writing. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### Maryland v. Wilson, 519 U.S. 408 (1997)

Argued December 11, 1996 | Decided February 19, 1997

From: the Maryland Court of Special Appeals’ affirmance of suppression after a trooper ordered Wilson, a passenger, out of a lawfully stopped vehicle and cocaine fell as Wilson exited.

Question presented: whether *Pennsylvania v. Mimms* authorizes an officer to order every passenger out as a matter of course, without a case-specific safety or investigative reason. Maryland did not preserve the actual movements, nervousness, and delayed stop as an independent justification. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Whren v. United States* makes objective legal support controlling for the initial traffic stop but does not decide passenger-exit commands.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Stevens, and Kennedy.

The suppression judgment is vacated, and the Maryland courts shall proceed under the categorical passenger-exit rule.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Stone and Stevens | Would affirm | Requires an objective circumstance connected to safety, a lawful search, or the conduct of the stop |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Passenger-exit authority

Holding: during a lawful traffic stop, an officer may order passengers to exit the vehicle as a matter of course pending completion of the stop.

Operative rule: no individualized suspicion that the particular passenger is armed, dangerous, or involved in criminal activity is required for the exit order itself.

Reasoning: traffic stops present a weighty officer-safety interest, which may increase when multiple occupants are present. Although the passenger ordinarily committed no traffic offense, the lawful stop has already interrupted the passenger’s travel. Requiring the passenger to step outside imposes a comparatively limited additional intrusion while allowing the officer to control the immediate roadside setting.

Limits and questions left open: the rule does not authorize an unlawful initial stop, a search of the passenger, detention extending beyond the lawful traffic mission, or other investigative action requiring independent justification.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm the Maryland Court of Special Appeals.

Public role: joins Kennedy’s dissent.

Announced ground: Stone joins the proposition that an exit command requires at least one objective circumstance making it reasonable for officer safety, a lawful search, or conduct of the stop. The necessary showing would be modest, but Maryland preserved only the categorical rule, so Stone would not decide whether the actual movements and nervousness were sufficient.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy, joined by Stone and Stevens, would require a reasoned, objective basis connected to the actual stop. The dissent does not require probable cause or a formal reasonable-suspicion finding and reserves the distinct question whether an officer may direct passengers to remain in the vehicle.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for passenger-exit orders. *Whren* remains the rule governing the objective validity of the initial stop; *Wilson* adds categorical authority to control passenger location while that lawful stop remains in progress.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 7-2 in an opinion by the Chief Justice, with Stevens and Kennedy dissenting separately. In the simulation, Stone rejects the historical Chief Justice’s position, reducing the majority to 6-3. O’Connor authors the Court opinion, while Kennedy’s objective-circumstance formulation supplies the common dissenting ground for Stone and Stevens. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the stop, suppression posture, categorical rule, historical lineup, preservation issue, and Kennedy’s narrower alternative. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York, 519 U.S. 357 (1997)

Argued October 16, 1996 | Decided February 19, 1997

From: the en banc Second Circuit’s affirmance of preliminary-injunction provisions creating fixed 15-foot zones around clinic entrances and driveways, floating 15-foot zones around persons and vehicles, and a cease-and-desist condition for sidewalk counselors.

Question presented: whether each restriction satisfies the provision-specific First Amendment standard governing a content-neutral, party-specific, contempt-backed injunction.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Madsen v. Women’s Health Center* requires each provision to be connected to demonstrated misconduct and to burden no more speech than necessary. The record documents blockades, trespass, crowding, physical interference, pursuit, and ineffective ordinary enforcement. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fixed entrance and driveway zones, including the fixed-zone counseling condition | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | These provisions may remain in force. |
| Person and vehicle floating zones, including the automatic 15-foot retreat outside fixed zones | Reversed, 8-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Breyer | These provisions shall be struck. |
| Narrow post-refusal disengagement provision on remand | Authorized, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The district court may require the enjoined parties to stop following and maintain a modest, precisely defined separation after an objectively clear refusal. |

#### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The judgment is affirmed in part and reversed in part. The district court may retain the fixed zones and their limited counseling exception. It shall remove the unconditional floating zones and the automatic 15-foot retreat outside the fixed zones.

A replacement provision may permit an initial peaceful approach but, after an objectively clear verbal or nonverbal refusal, require the enjoined defendants to stop following and maintain a modest, precisely defined separation supported by the record. The provision may not restrict stationary advocacy, speech addressed generally, willing conversation, or nonparties.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, and Ginsburg in full; Breyer as to the fixed zones, fixed-zone condition, and remand authority; Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas as to invalidation of the floating zones | Supports the mixed judgment | Different issue coalitions as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Kennedy and Thomas | Supports invalidation of floating zones; opposes the provisions left or authorized | Rejects fixed zones and listener-triggered separation |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | None | Supports fixed zones and remand authority; would affirm the floating zones | Treats the existing injunction as sufficiently tied to the local record |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Fixed zones and the counseling exception

Holding: the fixed 15-foot zones around clinic doorways, driveways, and parking entrances, together with the cease-and-desist condition governing the two counselors permitted inside those zones, satisfy the First Amendment on this record.

Reasoning: the fixed geography corresponds to the places where obstruction and crowding repeatedly impaired access. The stable corridor leaves visible and audible advocacy immediately outside. The counseling condition governs only an exception that allows two speakers inside an otherwise valid clear zone; it prevents that exception from recreating the close pursuit and crowding the zone remedies.

Limits and questions left open: the holding does not create a general right to avoid unwelcome speech on public sidewalks or validate a larger unsupported fixed zone.

Legal basis: First Amendment and the injunction-specific rule adopted in *Madsen*.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion, joined on this issue by Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a six-Justice majority.

##### Floating zones

Holding: the unconditional 15-foot floating zones around persons and vehicles, and the linked automatic retreat outside the fixed zones, burden more speech than necessary.

Reasoning: a moving 15-foot boundary is uncertain in operation, prevents ordinary conversational distance and leaflet transfer, and burdens peaceful speakers far beyond the access points. Vehicle zones also restrict speakers who stand at the curb holding signs or addressing traffic without obstructing an entrance.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not foreclose direct rules against obstruction, touching, threats, trespass, or persistent pursuit.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion is joined on this issue by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg, producing an eight-Justice majority.

##### Narrow post-refusal remedy

Holding: this record permits a narrower provision requiring the enjoined defendants, after an objectively clear refusal, to stop following the person addressed and maintain a modest, specifically defined separation.

Operative rule: refusal may terminate targeted pursuit by the adjudicated parties, but it may not silence stationary advocacy, speech addressed generally, a peaceful initial approach, or willing conversation. The distance must be precisely stated and supported by the record rather than fixed automatically at 15 feet.

Reasoning: direct regulation of post-refusal following addresses the proved pattern of close pursuit without converting every listener into a moving speech-restriction zone.

Limits and questions left open: the holding creates no general listener’s veto or undefined entitlement to personal space.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion, joined on this issue by Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a six-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm the fixed zones and fixed-zone counseling condition; reverse the floating zones and automatic retreat; authorize the narrower post-refusal remedy.

Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone applies the provision-specific fit rule adopted in simulated *Madsen*, distinguishing stable access corridors from uncertain mobile speech restrictions and from a precise conduct rule addressing persistent pursuit.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, agrees that the floating zones cannot stand but rejects both the fixed restrictions and any listener-triggered separation rule. Breyer would uphold the existing floating zones but joins the Court’s remaining disposition and narrower remand authority.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Madsen* is applied and clarified. Fixed geographic relief may be sustained by location-specific obstruction findings. Mobile zones require separate precision and support. The decision also recognizes a narrower conduct-based remedy for persistent post-refusal pursuit by adjudicated parties, without creating a general listener’s veto.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated votes on the fixed and floating zones match the historical 6-3 and 8-1 divisions. The simulation adds a controlling remand rule permitting a precisely defined post-refusal disengagement provision, replacing the automatic 15-foot retreat that accompanies the invalid floating zones. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the injunction text, misconduct record, fixed and floating zone analysis, historical opinion structure, and Breyer’s separate position. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/519bv.pdf))

### Brown v. Hot, Sexy & Safer Productions, Inc., 68 F.3d 525

Argued November 6, 1996 | Decided February 24, 1997

From: the First Circuit’s affirmance of dismissal under Rule 12(b)(6). Two fifteen-year-old students were required to attend a school-wide AIDS-awareness assembly containing allegedly graphic speech and simulated sexual conduct. The plaintiffs were not selected for the demonstrations or personally addressed. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/68/525/537667/))

Questions presented: whether the complaint states claims under parental liberty, student privacy, procedural due process, free exercise, or Title IX.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Settle v. Dickson County School Board* recognizes public-school authority to impose genuine curricular requirements reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical objectives. Same-term *Curtis* holds that voluntary condom availability creates no coercive burden. *Curtis* does not resolve compulsory attendance and is therefore incorporated only for its distinction between exposure, participation, and compelled conduct.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Parental liberty and student privacy

Holding: parental liberty does not confer a constitutional right to prescribe or opt out of each public-school curricular presentation reasonably related to a legitimate pedagogical objective. Compulsory exposure to ideas, without compelled participation, affirmation, disclosure, or bodily intrusion, does not violate student privacy or substantive due process.

Operative rule: parents retain authority to choose among lawful educational paths and to instruct their children outside school. Once a child attends public school, the Constitution does not give each parent a line-item veto over curricular content.

Reasoning: the students had to attend and listen, but they were not required to perform a demonstration, state agreement, disclose private information, or undergo treatment. The alleged vulgarity and the school’s possible violation of local policy may support criticism or state-law relief, but they do not identify a federal deprivation.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not approve compelled profession, personally targeted sexual conduct, bodily intrusion, religious instruction, pretextual curricular action, or sex-based humiliation.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment.

##### Procedural due process

Holding: assuming without deciding that the written positive-subscription policy created a protected state-law interest, the federal procedural claim fails because the plaintiffs did not preserve an argument that available Massachusetts postdeprivation remedies were inadequate.

Reasoning: a state policy violation is not itself a federal due-process violation. The Court does not decide whether the departure from the policy was random and unauthorized because the necessary challenge to the available state remedial process was not preserved.

Limits and questions left open: state-law enforcement of the policy remains outside the granted federal questions.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment procedural due process.

##### Free exercise

Holding: compulsory attendance at the assembly does not violate free exercise when the students are not required to engage in religiously forbidden conduct, affirm the presentation’s message, or surrender a benefit because of religious objection.

Reasoning: the attendance requirement was neutral and generally applicable. Exposure to speech inconsistent with religious belief is not the same as compelled observance or conduct.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not govern religious targeting, compelled affirmation, or a mandatory activity itself forbidden by faith.

Legal basis: Free Exercise Clause.

##### Title IX

Holding: the complaint fails under Title IX because it alleges no discrimination, exclusion, or hostile educational condition imposed on the plaintiffs because of sex.

Reasoning: sexual subject matter is not itself sex discrimination. The presentation was given to the student audience generally, and the named plaintiffs were not selected for the demonstrations. The missing because-of-sex element makes it unnecessary to decide severity or pervasiveness.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not govern sex-specific humiliation, school-authorized sexual targeting, or deliberate indifference to known sex-based harassment.

Legal basis: Title IX.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm dismissal of the federal claims.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction among compulsory attendance, compelled affirmation, personal participation, and bodily or religiously prohibited conduct.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Same-term *Curtis* is distinguished and clarified. A voluntary resource creates no burden merely by being available. *Brown* adds that mandatory attendance at a curricular presentation, without compelled affirmation, participation, disclosure, bodily intrusion, or religious conduct, likewise creates no parental line-item veto or free-exercise violation. *Settle* is applied without altering its protection for genuine curricular requirements.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulated Court unanimously affirms the historical First Circuit judgment but uses same-term *Curtis* to distinguish voluntary availability from compulsory exposure while reaching the same result. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/68/525/537667/))

Research sources: the First Circuit opinion for the complaint, dismissal posture, assembly allegations, positive-subscription policy, constitutional theories, and Title IX claim. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/68/525/537667/))

### Warner-Jenkinson Co. v. Hilton Davis Chemical Co., 520 U.S. 17 (1997)

Argued October 15, 1996 | Decided March 3, 1997

From: the en banc Federal Circuit’s affirmance of a jury verdict finding infringement under the doctrine of equivalents. Hilton Davis’s patent claimed ultrafiltration at approximately pH 6.0 to 9.0; Warner-Jenkinson used pH 5.0. The record does not establish why the lower pH limit was added during prosecution. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/520bv.pdf))

Questions presented: whether the doctrine of equivalents survived the 1952 Patent Act, whether it must be applied element by element, and what consequence follows from an unexplained narrowing amendment.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Markman v. Westview Instruments* assigns claim construction to the court while leaving ordinary infringement facts for the jury.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Federal Circuit shall determine the legal effect of the pH amendment. If the asserted equivalent remains legally available, disputed factual equivalence shall proceed under ordinary infringement procedures. The Court does not decide infringement finally.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | Kennedy | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes fair notice in applying the unexplained-amendment burden |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Survival and element-specific operation of equivalents

Holding: the doctrine of equivalents survived the 1952 Patent Act, but it must be applied to each claim element rather than to the accused product or process as a whole.

Operative rule: nonliteral infringement may be found only when each claimed element is present literally or through a legally permissible equivalent. The doctrine may not erase or vitiate a material claim limitation.

Reasoning: the doctrine prevents trivial substitutions from defeating a lawful patent, while element-specific application preserves the statutory claim’s notice and boundary functions. The 1952 Act did not clearly displace the doctrine recognized in *Graver Tank*.

Limits and questions left open: the Court adopts no single verbal formula for equivalence and leaves case-specific refinement to the Federal Circuit.

Legal basis: Patent Act of 1952 and surviving patent precedent.

##### Prosecution-history estoppel and unexplained amendments

Holding: subject matter surrendered during patent prosecution may not be recaptured through equivalents. When the record does not disclose why a narrowing amendment was made, the patentee bears the burden of establishing a reason that does not estop the asserted equivalent.

Operative rule: the court determines the legal availability of the asserted equivalent under the claim and prosecution history. If the patentee cannot supply a nonestopping explanation for an unexplained narrowing amendment, the disputed territory is presumed surrendered.

Reasoning: the public is entitled to rely on the claim and the prosecution record. The upper pH limit was added to distinguish prior art, but the reason for the lower 6.0 limit is unresolved. The existing infringement judgment therefore cannot stand without determining the legal effect of that amendment.

Limits and questions left open: the decision creates no categorical bar for every foreseeable but unclaimed substitute absent a narrowing amendment. It does not decide a broader intent-based theory or remove disputed factual equivalence from the jury after the court resolves the legal limits.

Legal basis: Patent Act, claim construction, and prosecution-history estoppel.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the doctrine’s survival, element-specific operation, and the patentee’s burden to explain the narrowing pH amendment before reclaiming the disputed territory.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Markman v. Westview Instruments* is applied without alteration. Courts decide claim meaning and the legal effect of prosecution history; genuine factual equivalence remains subject to ordinary infringement procedures when the asserted equivalent survives those legal limits.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, and separate-writing lineup match the historical decision. The simulation confines the decision to the element-specific and prosecution-history questions necessary to remand and does not resolve the broader judge-jury issue. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/520bv.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the patent claim, pH amendment, doctrine-of-equivalents analysis, prosecution-history burden, remand, judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/520bv.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Wright v. MetroHealth Medical Center, 58 F.3d 1130

Argued November 12, 1996 | Decided March 3, 1997

From: the Sixth Circuit’s affirmance of summary judgment for MetroHealth. Its policy barred immediate family members from positions requiring close interaction, offered Martha Wright a comparable transfer within the hospital, and led MetroHealth to ask its helicopter contractor to reassign John Wright after Martha declined. Neither spouse was discharged, and the policy imposed no legal bar on their marriage. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/58/1130/574869/))

Question presented: whether applying that policy to the LifeFlight unit directly and substantially interfered with the fundamental right to marry, or instead imposed an incidental public-employment consequence governed by rational-basis review.

Controlling simulated law before decision: no simulated holding specifically addresses public-employer anti-nepotism rules. Surviving marriage precedent distinguishes direct and substantial legal impediments from incidental consequences that neither prevent marriage nor make it practically unavailable.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The federal constitutional judgment remains in force. The Court makes no separate ruling on the merits of the pendent state-law theories.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Anti-nepotism policy and the right to marry

Holding: a public employer does not directly and substantially interfere with marriage when it requires one spouse to leave a work unit in which the spouses necessarily interact, provides its employee a comparable transfer without loss of pay, and neither prohibits the marriage nor conditions continued marital status on loss of public employment.

Reasoning: the policy regulates simultaneous assignment within one operational unit, not eligibility to marry or remain married. The Wrights married despite the policy, and Martha remained eligible for comparable employment throughout MetroHealth’s system. The burden is real but indirect, of the kind that *Jobst* distinguished from the legal and practical barriers invalidated in *Loving* and *Zablocki*.

Under rational-basis review, MetroHealth may address actual and perceived conflicts of interest, divided loyalty, crew coordination, and confidence in assignment and safety decisions within a small emergency-flight team. It need not prove that every married pair will compromise operations. Applying the rule only where family members work in close and recurring proximity is rational line drawing, not evidence that the policy is arbitrary.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not approve a policy intended to deter marriage, automatic discharge of one spouse, sex-based administration, enforcement resting on animus, or a transfer materially inferior in pay or responsibility. It does not decide a completed discharge claim because no such discharge occurred.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process and Equal Protection Clauses.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between regulation of a particular work assignment and direct or substantial interference with the decision to marry.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The received distinction between direct legal impediments to marriage and incidental economic or employment consequences is applied without alteration. The decision supplies the first controlling simulated application of that distinction to a public-employer anti-nepotism policy.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulated Court unanimously adopts the Sixth Circuit’s central constitutional conclusion that the policy did not directly and substantially interfere with marriage and was rationally related to workplace-conflict and morale concerns. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/58/1130/574869/))

Research sources: the Sixth Circuit opinion for the LifeFlight arrangement, policy text, offered transfer, constitutional analysis, state-law posture, and historical lower-court judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/58/1130/574869/))

### Brown v. Polk County, 61 F.3d 650

Argued November 13, 1996 | Decided March 17, 1997

From: the en banc Eighth Circuit’s judgment concerning Polk County’s directions that information-services director Isaiah Brown cease anything that could be considered religious proselytizing, witnessing, or counseling, remove all religious items from his office, and refrain from religious use of public resources. Brown had supervised about fifty employees. The record included use of a secretary for Bible-study notes, voluntary prayer before work and at some department meetings, and testimony that the religion-related reprimand was a factor in his later discharge. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/61/650/492754/))

Questions presented: which activities Title VII required Polk County to accommodate; whether the religion-specific blanket directives violated the First Amendment; and whether the completed trial record permitted judgment that religion was an impermissible factor in Brown’s discharge.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Beadle v. City of Tampa* applies the received more-than-de-minimis undue-hardship rule. *Waters v. Churchill* and *National Treasury Employees Union* govern public-employer factual judgments and broad prospective restraints. *Capitol Square v. Pinette* and *Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller* distinguish private religious expression from government speech and public sponsorship.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Religion-specific workplace directives and removal order | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The directives may not be enforced as blanket prohibitions. The remand may preserve neutral and tailored workplace rules. |
| Title VII religious-discrimination judgment concerning discharge | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The remand for appropriate statutory relief continues; the County may not reopen the completed trial record to supply a same-decision defense it failed to prove. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full; Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas as to accommodation boundaries and the First Amendment judgment | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for six Justices; partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Kennedy | Scalia and Thomas | Supports the First Amendment judgment but opposes final resolution of the same-decision defense | Would remand the mixed-motive question for district-court findings |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Title VII accommodation and public resources

Holding: Title VII requires reasonable accommodation of a known sincere religious practice unless the employer establishes a real, more-than-de-minimis operational burden. It does not require diversion of public employees or resources to private religious work, official worship through supervisory authority, coercive religious counseling, harassment, or unequal personal use of the workplace.

Operative rule: genuinely voluntary employee-initiated prayer, private religious conversation, and personal religious items must be distinguished from expression attributable to the government or dependent on public resources. Generalized discomfort, offense, speculative polarization, and unsupported fear of favoritism do not establish undue hardship.

Application: Polk County could prevent Brown from directing a secretary to prepare Bible-study notes. It could also regulate prayer or religious instruction delivered as an official supervisory act. The record did not establish a real operational burden from every private conversation, voluntary prayer, or personal religious item swept into the County’s categorical orders.

Limits and questions left open: comparable secular personal use may be restricted through a neutral rule. Title VII does not authorize favoritism, compulsory prayer, harassment, or religious use of subordinates.

Legal basis: Title VII, 42 U.S.C. §§ 2000e(j) and 2000e-2.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion is joined on these accommodation propositions by all nine Justices.

##### Religion as a factor in discharge

Holding: under the law governing the pre-1991 conduct, Brown produced direct evidence that religion was a motivating factor in his discharge, and Polk County failed on the completed trial record to prove that it would have made the same decision without considering the protected religious practices.

Reasoning: the administrator testified that the religion-based reprimand was a factor in the discharge. That admission activated the received mixed-motive framework. The County had a full trial at which to establish its asserted budgetary and supervisory grounds, but its decisionmaker could not say that Brown would have been discharged absent the religious factor, and the record supplied no other proof carrying the County’s burden. A second evidentiary opportunity is not required merely because the district court initially applied the wrong framework. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/61/650/492754/))

Limits and questions left open: the holding does not prevent discharge for independently established poor performance, misuse of public resources, or coercive supervision. It does not apply the Civil Rights Act of 1991 retroactively.

Legal basis: Title VII and the pre-1991 mixed-motive rule.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion, joined on this question by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a six-Justice majority.

##### Religion-specific directives and attribution

Holding: a public employer violates the First Amendment when it singles out religious exercise and viewpoint through an undifferentiated prohibition on everything that could be considered witnessing, counseling, or proselytizing and orders removal of every religious item without tying those restrictions to official duties, coercion, harassment, resource use, or demonstrated disruption.

Operative rule: government may control speech and worship delivered as its own, restrict coercive supervision, and enforce neutral rules governing time, duties, resources, and workplace conduct. It may not treat the religious character of otherwise private expression as the workplace harm.

Reasoning: the directives reached far beyond the activities that could lawfully be regulated. They covered a personal Bible kept in a desk, private conversation, and any expression someone might characterize as religious. Avoiding an objectively grounded Establishment Clause violation is a legitimate public-employer interest, especially where a supervisor leads worship in a mandatory meeting. Abstract fear of establishment or offense does not justify suppressing private religious expression. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/61/650/492754/))

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not require identical treatment of government-sponsored worship and private employee expression, or make a statutory accommodation violation automatically establish a constitutional violation.

Legal basis: Free Speech and Free Exercise Clauses, informed by the Establishment Clause’s attribution boundary.

Authority of the proposition: unanimous.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm both judgment components.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s separation of private religious practice from official, coercive, or resource-dependent conduct; its rejection of the blanket religion-specific directives; and its conclusion that Polk County failed to prove the same-decision defense on the completed record.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas, agrees that the County’s blanket directives cannot stand and joins the Court’s accommodation boundaries. He would return the same-decision question to the district court rather than treat the County’s trial proof as legally insufficient.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Beadle v. City of Tampa* is applied and clarified. Its real-burden requirement governs private religious practices, while public resource use, official worship, coercion, and harassment remain independently regulable. *Waters*, *National Treasury Employees Union*, *Pinette*, and *Chabad-Lubavitch* are applied through the distinction between private expression and speech attributable to government.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulation largely affirms the divided en banc Eighth Circuit judgment but narrows its broader language by expressly preserving neutral restrictions on government resources, official worship, and supervisory coercion. The 6-3 division over the completed same-decision record corresponds to the principal disagreement within the historical en banc court. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/61/650/492754/))

Research sources: the en banc opinion and rehearing materials for the County’s directives, the classified activities, the accommodation evidence, the mixed-motive record, and the historical disagreement. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/61/650/492754/))

### Young v. Harper, 520 U.S. 143 (1997)

Argued December 9, 1996 | Decided March 18, 1997

From: the Tenth Circuit’s grant of federal habeas relief after Oklahoma summarily returned Harper from its Preparole Conditional Supervision Program to prison. Harper had spent five uneventful months living at his own residence, working, and reporting to a parole officer before the Governor denied formal parole and an officer directed him to return immediately. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520143/usrep520143.pdf))

Question presented: whether preparole conferred parole-like community liberty requiring the revocation procedures prescribed in *Morrissey v. Brewer*.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Morrissey* governs revocation of parole-like conditional liberty. Simulated *Sandin v. Conner* addresses prison discipline but does not displace the more specific rule governing release into the community.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Oklahoma may continue Harper’s custody only through a lawful revocation determination or another independent legal basis. The Court does not order unconditional release.

Justice Thomas delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Parole-like community release

Holding: a State must provide the procedures required by *Morrissey* before terminating a conditional-release program that releases a prisoner from the institution to live and work in the community under restrictions materially equivalent to parole.

Operative rule: legal labels do not control. The inquiry concerns the liberty actually conferred, including residence outside the institution, employment, ordinary community life, and supervision resembling parole. The rule does not create an entitlement to initial release.

Reasoning: Harper maintained his own residence and employment and lived generally free of the incidents of imprisonment. Reporting, travel, and behavioral conditions did not make that community liberty institutional confinement. Oklahoma could treat the Governor’s denial of parole as relevant to whether preparole should continue, but it could not extinguish the liberty already conferred without notice, an opportunity to contest the asserted basis for return, and a neutral decision under the appropriate revocation procedures.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not govern short furloughs, work details, or release arrangements that remain functionally institutional. Immediate temporary custody remains possible in a genuine safety emergency if prompt effective process follows.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s functional treatment of the conditional community liberty conferred by preparole.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Morrissey* is applied and clarified. Its protections extend to a release program equivalent to parole in operation, notwithstanding a different program label. Simulated *Sandin* remains confined to claimed liberty interests within institutional confinement.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, and controlling rule match the historical decision. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520143/usrep520143.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the program structure, Harper’s community life, the summary return, historical judgment, and opinion authorship. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520143/usrep520143.pdf))

### Lambert v. Wicklund, 520 U.S. 292 (1997)

Submitted without oral argument | Decided March 31, 1997

From: the Ninth Circuit’s affirmance of a facial injunction against Montana’s parental-notification law. The statute required a confidential and expedited judicial bypass, counsel on request, action within forty-eight hours, automatic waiver upon judicial inaction, and waiver upon maturity, abuse, or a finding that notification was not in the minor’s best interests.

Question presented: whether the notification-not-in-best-interests formulation provides the constitutionally required best-interests bypass.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Planned Parenthood v. Miller* requires an independent, confidential, and expeditious decisionmaker capable of authorizing abortion without notice under maturity and best-interests criteria.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The injunction is vacated insofar as it rests on the asserted defect in the best-interests formulation. Any preserved issue not resolved by this judgment remains for further proceedings.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Per curiam Opinion of the Court | The Court | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | Ginsburg and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Agrees the Montana route is constitutionally sufficient while reserving whether an abortion-best-interests finding is also necessary |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Best-interests bypass for parental notification

Holding: a confidential and expeditious judicial bypass satisfies the best-interests component of the parental-notification rule when it requires waiver upon a finding that notifying the parent is not in the minor’s best interests.

Operative rule: constitutional sufficiency turns on whether the decisionmaker can authorize the abortion without notice when parental involvement would be contrary to the minor’s interests, not on use of a particular verbal formula.

Reasoning: Montana’s language is materially equivalent to the Ohio formulation previously sustained. Read in context, a finding that notification is not in the minor’s best interests directs the court to authorize abortion without notification. The maturity route, confidentiality, appointed counsel, deadline, and automatic-waiver provision reinforce the availability of effective independent review. The Ninth Circuit could not facially invalidate the statute by separating two formulations that produce the same legal authorization. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/292/))

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide the validity of clear-and-convincing proof in every application, the adequacy of the emergency provisions, or whether implementation could make the nominally expedited process unavailable.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: the six-Justice per curiam opinion supplies the controlling rationale. All nine Justices agree that the statutory best-interests route is constitutionally sufficient.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the per curiam Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the functional sufficiency of Montana’s best-interests route.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg and Breyer, agrees that the statute is constitutional under the controlling parental-notification precedents. He would reserve whether a minor must additionally demonstrate that the abortion itself is in her best interests, emphasizing that the statute’s text makes nonnotification alone a sufficient basis for waiver. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/292/))

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Planned Parenthood v. Miller* is applied and clarified. Its best-interests requirement concerns effective authority to dispense with notice, not a mandatory phrasing of the judicial inquiry.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated public form, judgment, per curiam rationale, and Stevens concurrence joined by Ginsburg and Breyer match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/292/))

Research sources: official opinion text for the statutory bypass, the per curiam disposition, and the separate concurrence. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/292/))

### Robinson v. City of Edmond, 68 F.3d 1226

Argued December 2, 1996 | Decided March 31, 1997

From: the Tenth Circuit’s judgment that Edmond violated the Establishment Clause by placing a Christian cross in one quadrant of its official seal and using the seal on city signs, flags, uniforms, vehicles, property labels, utility bills, stationery, newsletters, and in the council chambers. The plaintiffs were non-Christian residents and workers seeking prospective municipal relief. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/68/1226/537980/))

Questions presented: whether the plaintiffs had Article III standing based on regular exposure to the City’s own official symbol, and whether the seal’s composition, historical explanation, and pervasive official use impermissibly adopted or endorsed Christianity.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Capitol Square v. Pinette* and *Chabad-Lubavitch* protect clearly private religious expression in neutral forums. They do not govern a symbol selected, owned, and used by government as its own official identifier.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Article III standing | Standing sustained, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The Court reaches the municipal Establishment Clause claim. |
| Establishment Clause judgment | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Edmond shall cease using the cross-bearing seal as its official imprimatur within a reasonable implementation period set on remand. |

The district court may permit archival preservation and genuinely historical displays with accurate contextual treatment. Individual damages and immunity remain separate from the municipal prospective relief.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full; Kennedy as to standing | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for six Justices; standing portion joined by seven |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Kennedy | None | Supports standing but would reverse on the merits | Would require coercion, legal preference, or another more concrete governmental religious disability |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would dismiss for lack of standing and, alternatively, reverse on the merits | Rejects direct-exposure standing and treats the seal as permissible historical civic symbolism |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Standing to challenge the official seal

Holding: residents and workers who are regularly confronted with a municipality’s own official seal in ordinary civic transactions suffer a direct and continuing injury sufficient to seek prospective relief from the seal’s allegedly unconstitutional governmental message.

Operative rule: mere knowledge that government has engaged in disputed conduct, or abstract ideological disagreement by a person lacking direct contact, remains insufficient. Regular exposure to the government’s own official identifier through bills, uniforms, vehicles, signs, and civic proceedings is not the remote observation rejected in *Valley Forge*.

Reasoning: the plaintiffs live or work under a municipal government that repeatedly presents the challenged seal as its authenticating mark. Their asserted injury therefore arises from direct recurring governmental communication, not simply from awareness of conduct occurring elsewhere.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not create standing whenever a person learns of an allegedly unconstitutional symbol or disagrees with a historical display encountered only by choice.

Legal basis: Article III.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion, joined on standing by Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a seven-Justice majority.

##### Christian cross in the official city seal

Holding: a municipality violates the Establishment Clause when it incorporates an unmistakable Christian cross into its official seal and uses that seal pervasively as the government’s own identifying and authenticating mark.

Operative rule: attribution, governmental authorship, control, placement, and use determine the constitutional character of the message. Secular images elsewhere in a seal and a historical explanation do not neutralize a sectarian symbol that the government presents as part of its official self-description.

Reasoning: Edmond selected and owns the seal, controls every official use, and places it on the instruments through which the City speaks and acts. The cross therefore is not the message of a private speaker in an open forum. Its placement among the City’s chosen civic emblems identifies Christianity as part of the government’s official identity. The engine, oil derrick, tower, and covered wagon do not transform the cross into a religiously neutral graphic, and longevity does not cure governmental adoption of a faith’s unmistakable symbol. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/68/1226/537980/))

Limits and questions left open: the holding does not invalidate the City’s name, private religious displays in neutral forums, archival retention, a contextual historical exhibit, or ceremonial references that do not adopt a religion as the government’s own.

Legal basis: Establishment Clause.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion, joined on the merits by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a six-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: sustain standing and affirm the Establishment Clause judgment.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s direct-exposure standing rule and its treatment of the pervasively used municipal seal as government speech adopting the cross as part of the City’s official identity.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy agrees that the plaintiffs have standing but would require coercion, legal preference, or another more concrete governmental religious disability before invalidating a longstanding civic seal.

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would hold that offense arising from observation of the seal is insufficient for standing. On the merits, they would treat the seal’s historical composition and surrounding secular images as constitutionally adequate context.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Capitol Square v. Pinette* and *Chabad-Lubavitch* are distinguished. Their attribution rules protect private religious expression in neutral forums; they do not insulate a religious symbol chosen and pervasively used by government as its own official mark. The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for a sectarian symbol incorporated into a municipal seal.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Supreme Court denied certiorari 6-3 on May 13, 1996. Chief Justice Rehnquist, joined by Scalia and Thomas, dissented from denial, citing both the circuit conflict and the standing question. The simulation reaches the merits. Stone rejects the position associated with the historical Chief Justice, while Scalia and Thomas preserve their objections and Kennedy separately dissents on the merits. ([The Free Speech Center](https://firstamendment.mtsu.edu/article/city-of-edmond-v-robinson/))

Research sources: the Tenth Circuit opinion for the seal’s composition, official uses, plaintiffs, lower-court judgment, and Establishment Clause analysis; the historical denial instrument for its date and separate writing. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/68/1226/537980/))

### Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. Federal Communications Commission, 520 U.S. 180 (1997)

Argued October 7, 1996 | Decided March 31, 1997

From: direct appeal from a three-judge District Court judgment sustaining the Cable Act’s must-carry provisions after the eighteen-month evidentiary remand ordered in simulated *Turner I*. The developed record addressed cable bottleneck power, vertical integration, actual and predicted noncarriage of local broadcasters, channel capacity, and displacement of cable programming. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/180/))

Questions presented: whether the expanded record supports Congress’s predictive judgment that must-carry materially advances the important interests recognized in simulated *Turner I*, and whether the provisions burden substantially more cable editorial discretion than necessary.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC*, 512 U.S. 622, classifies must-carry as content neutral and requires evidence-based intermediate scrutiny, including independent review of editorial burden and fit.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

The must-carry provisions remain in force. Properly supported system-specific as-applied challenges remain available.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment and Opinion of the Court except in part | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, and Souter in full; Breyer except insofar as the opinion relies on an anticompetitive rationale | Supports affirmance | Court opinion on preservation of free local broadcasting, multiplicity of sources, record support, burden, and fit |
| Concurrence | Stevens | None | Supports affirmance | Emphasizes the legitimacy of Congress’s supported judgment concerning the communications structure |
| Concurrence in part | Breyer | None | Supports affirmance | Relies on preserving free local broadcasting and a multiplicity of information sources, not the anticompetitive rationale |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas, and Ginsburg | Would reverse | Rejects the record showing and maintains the classification and editorial-discretion objections stated in simulated *Turner I* |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Record support for the governmental interests

Holding: the expanded record supports Congress’s reasonable predictive judgment that, without must-carry, cable bottleneck control would threaten carriage and economic viability of local broadcast stations, thereby impairing free over-the-air service and the dissemination of information from multiple sources.

Reasoning: Congress could rely on evidence of growing cable market power, vertical integration, incentives to drop or disadvantage unaffiliated broadcasters, prior noncarriage, and the relationship among carriage, audience reach, advertising revenue, and continued broadcast operation. Intermediate scrutiny does not require proof that the entire broadcasting industry would collapse or that every individual station faced imminent failure. Courts independently review constitutional fit while according proper weight to legislative predictions supported by substantial evidence. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/180/))

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not apply broadcast-scarcity doctrine to cable or treat legislative assertion without supporting evidence as conclusive.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s opinion is joined on the free-broadcasting and multiplicity rationales by Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Breyer, supplying a five-Justice majority. The additional anticompetitive rationale is supported by Kennedy, Stone, Stevens, and Souter only and is not independently controlling.

##### Editorial burden and fit

Holding: the must-carry provisions do not burden substantially more cable editorial discretion than necessary to advance the majority-supported interests.

Reasoning: the obligations are limited by system capacity, count qualifying stations already carried voluntarily, and leave operators control over the substantial remainder of their lineups. The developed evidence showed that most systems used otherwise available capacity and that relatively few existing cable services were displaced. Congress was not required to select a less effective alternative merely because one could be imagined; intermediate scrutiny requires reasonable fit, not least-restrictive-means proof.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not immunize a materially more burdensome future rule or foreclose an as-applied challenge supported by disproportionate actual displacement.

Legal basis: First Amendment and 47 U.S.C. §§ 534 and 535.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s opinion, joined on this question by Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Breyer, supplies a five-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins Kennedy’s opinion in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the complete record-support, legislative-prediction, and reasonable-fit analysis, including the additional fair-competition rationale that does not itself command five votes.

#### Material Separate Writings

Breyer agrees that must-carry satisfies intermediate scrutiny but relies only on preserving free over-the-air broadcasting and promoting a multiplicity of information sources. His partial join supplies the fifth vote for those interests and for the fit holding.

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Thomas, and Ginsburg, concludes that the evidence remains insufficient and that compelled carriage intrudes too deeply on cable operators’ editorial choices.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Turner I* is applied and completed. Its content-neutral classification, intermediate-scrutiny standard, burden allocation, and allowance for supported legislative prediction remain unchanged. The evidentiary gap that required the 1994 remand is now closed.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, 5-4 vote, author, partial join, concurrences, and dissent match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the supporting position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/180/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for the remand record, questions presented, historical judgment, opinion topology, and separate writings. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/180/))

### Young v. Fordice, 520 U.S. 273 (1997)

Argued January 6, 1997 | Decided March 31, 1997

From: direct appeal from a three-judge District Court judgment sustaining Mississippi’s post-February 10, 1995 voter-registration system. Mississippi briefly used a provisional unitary system, abandoned it when the needed state legislation failed, and then retained its old state-registration rules while implementing different NVRA procedures for federal elections through new instructions, forms, agency designations, reporting duties, and voter communications. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/273/))

Questions presented: whether the forty-one-day Provisional Plan displaced the Old System as the § 5 comparison baseline, and whether Mississippi’s discretionary implementation choices were unprecleared changes from that baseline.

Controlling simulated law before decision: same-term *Lopez v. Monterey County* holds that lack of local discretion does not itself exempt a state-mandated voting change from § 5 and preserves prospective nonuse of unprecleared governmental plans. *Morse v. Republican Party of Virginia* supplies the broader functional coverage and enforcement rules.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The District Court shall identify Mississippi’s covered discretionary changes and enjoin their further administration as appropriate unless and until they receive § 5 preclearance. It shall not decide discriminatory purpose, retrogression, or entitlement to preclearance.

Justice Breyer delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### The operative baseline

Holding: the Provisional Plan did not become the voting practice in force or effect for purposes of the § 5 comparison baseline.

Reasoning: unlawfulness under state law alone would not prevent a longstanding actual practice from becoming the baseline. Here, however, officials expected the plan to become lawful, abandoned it promptly when the legislature failed to act, used it for only forty-one days, obtained implementation by only about one-third of registration officials, and conducted no election under it. Taken together, those circumstances show a temporary and incomplete attempt rather than a settled operative system. The Old System therefore remains the baseline. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/273/))

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not establish a fixed duration or numerical threshold for determining when a practice becomes operative.

##### Discretionary NVRA implementation changes

Holding: discretionary state choices used to implement the NVRA are changes requiring § 5 preclearance when they differ from the lawful baseline, even though the State made them while complying with federal law.

Operative rule: federal or superior legal constraints do not preclear the policy choices left to state or local officials. Change triggers the preclearance sequence regardless of whether its eventual effect is beneficial, harmful, or neutral. The enforcement court identifies coverage and requires nonuse; the designated preclearance forums decide purpose and effect.

Application: Mississippi adopted materially different registration instructions, assistance practices, agency designations, reporting rules, forwarding procedures, forms, and communications. Those choices could affect who registers, what applicants understand, and how officials administer federal and state voting eligibility. They therefore require preclearance. Mississippi’s earlier submission described a materially different unitary system and did not clearly submit the later dual system. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/273/))

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide whether separate federal and state rolls are substantively lawful or whether the identified changes should receive preclearance. The judgment addresses discretionary state implementation. It does not decide whether direct compliance with an express federal command that leaves no state policy choice is itself a state voting change. Same-term *Lopez* remains controlling for changes mandated by a noncovered State.

Legal basis: Voting Rights Act § 5 and the National Voter Registration Act’s non-displacement provisions.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground. He joins the Old System baseline, the coverage of Mississippi’s discretionary implementation choices, and the separation between coverage enforcement and substantive preclearance.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Same-term *Lopez v. Monterey County* is applied and clarified. Its rejection of a general no-discretion exemption remains controlling for state-mandated changes. *Young v. Fordice* adds that federal compliance does not remove discretionary state policy choices from § 5 and identifies the circumstances under which a brief provisional practice fails to replace the prior baseline.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, baseline analysis, and discretionary-change holding match the historical decision. The simulation’s completed *Lopez* decision supplies broader entering law concerning externally mandated changes, but it does not alter the result because Mississippi’s covered practices involved state policy choice. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/273/case.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the three registration systems, baseline facts, discretionary practices, preclearance submission, historical judgment, and opinion authorship. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/273/case.pdf))
