### Chandler v. Miller, 520 U.S. 305 (1997)

Argued January 14, 1997 | Decided April 15, 1997

From: the Eleventh Circuit’s judgment sustaining Georgia’s requirement that candidates for numerous executive, legislative, and judicial offices obtain a certificate showing a negative urinalysis within thirty days before ballot qualification. Candidates could select the testing date and provide the specimen through a personal physician. Georgia established no concrete drug problem among officeholders and no safety-sensitive function common to the covered offices. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520305/usrep520305.pdf))

Question presented: whether the qualification requirement is a reasonable suspicionless search under the Fourth Amendment’s special-needs doctrine.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton* permits suspicionless testing in its confined student-athlete setting, based on reduced privacy, limited procedures, restricted use, and a documented operational problem. It creates no general public-status exception to individualized suspicion.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The District Court shall enter declaratory and prospective relief barring enforcement of the drug-test qualification. Georgia remains free to investigate particularized wrongdoing and impose lawful nonsearch qualifications.

Justice Ginsburg delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Suspicionless testing of candidates

Holding: a State may not require every candidate for designated public office to undergo suspicionless drug testing when it identifies no substantial and concrete special need connected to the actual duties or risks of the covered offices.

Operative rule: reduced intrusiveness cannot substitute for the threshold governmental need. A departure from individualized suspicion requires a substantial operational concern beyond ordinary law enforcement or symbolic reassurance, followed by a context-specific assessment of privacy, efficacy, and fit.

Reasoning: the covered offices share no common immediate safety risk, candidates remain private citizens unless elected, and public candidacy does not waive bodily privacy. Georgia demonstrated no drug crisis among officeholders and no reason ordinary investigation of particularized misconduct would be impracticable. The candidate-selected testing date also makes the program poorly suited to identify or deter drug use. The State’s remaining interest is a symbolic display of commitment to drug control, and symbolic reassurance is not a special need. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520305/usrep520305.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not govern targeted testing based on individualized suspicion, medical fitness examinations, financial-disclosure rules, private-sector testing, or security screening tied to a real and immediate safety risk.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s requirement of a concrete operational need before suspicionless bodily testing may replace individualized suspicion.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Vernonia* is declined extension beyond its documented school-athletics setting. The decision clarifies that a minimally intrusive procedure does not independently justify suspicionless testing when the asserted special need is missing.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 8-1, with the Chief Justice dissenting. Stone rejects that historical position, joins Justice Ginsburg’s opinion, and produces a unanimous simulated judgment. The remaining historical majority alignment is unchanged. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520305/usrep520305.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the statutory offices, testing procedures, absence of a documented problem, historical judgment, author, and dissent. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520305/usrep520305.pdf))

### Blessing v. Freestone, 520 U.S. 329 (1997)

Argued January 6, 1997 | Decided April 21, 1997

From: the Ninth Circuit’s broad holding that five Arizona mothers could use 42 U.S.C. § 1983 to compel systemwide substantial compliance with Title IV-D’s child-support requirements. Their complaint alleged staffing shortages, backlogs, and failures to establish paternity, obtain orders, and collect or distribute support, but neither the complaint nor the lower court parsed the particular statutory provisions said to create particular rights. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/329/))

Questions presented: whether Title IV-D creates an individual right to systemwide substantial compliance, how any specific statutory right must be identified, and whether the federal audit and funding scheme categorically displaces § 1983.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Livadas v. Bradshaw* distinguishes violation of federal law from violation of an individual federal right and treats statutory displacement of § 1983 as a separate inquiry.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The lower courts shall require the plaintiffs to identify each specific statutory right asserted. Only after deciding whether a particular provision creates an individual right may the court address § 1983 displacement, proper defendants, causation, and lawful relief. No undifferentiated order of programwide substantial compliance may issue.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Kennedy | Supports vacatur and remand | Questions whether Spending Clause beneficiaries may sue as third-party beneficiaries under § 1983, while accepting the governing *Wright* and *Wilder* framework for this case |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Identification of enforceable Title IV-D rights

Holding: Title IV-D does not create an individual federal right to compel a State to achieve systemwide substantial compliance with the title as a whole.

Operative rule: a § 1983 claimant must identify a particular statutory provision intended to benefit the claimant, imposing a binding obligation, and defining an interest sufficiently concrete for judicial enforcement. The inquiry proceeds provision by provision. Aggregate program standards, staffing requirements, data systems, service duties, and payment-distribution commands cannot be treated as one indivisible entitlement.

Reasoning: Title IV-D’s substantial-compliance yardstick measures aggregate state performance for federal audit and funding purposes and tolerates nonperformance in some individual cases. It therefore does not promise each eligible family that the entire program will satisfy every statutory and regulatory requirement. Staffing and organizational provisions likewise do not define a manageable individual entitlement. A more specific payment-distribution or service provision may perform different legal work and must be considered separately. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/329/))

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not foreclose a claim under a specific rights-creating provision or determine whether the prior version of § 657 creates a concrete right to prompt distribution of collected payments.

Legal basis: 42 U.S.C. § 1983 and Title IV-D.

##### Statutory displacement of § 1983

Holding: Title IV-D’s general audit, corrective-action, and funding-reduction mechanisms do not categorically foreclose § 1983 actions to enforce every concrete individual right the statute may create.

Operative rule: after a right is identified, § 1983 remains presumptively available unless Congress expressly foreclosed it or established a remedial scheme so comprehensive and incompatible that individual enforcement cannot coexist with the statutory design.

Reasoning: the Secretary’s oversight powers regulate the federal-state funding relationship but provide no general judicial or administrative forum in which an individual family may obtain case-specific relief. That mechanism is materially less comprehensive than the remedial schemes previously found to displace § 1983. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/329/))

Limits and questions left open: the holding creates no statutory right, authorizes no structural remedy, and does not prevent a specific remedial provision from displacing § 1983 for a particular claim.

Legal basis: 42 U.S.C. § 1983 and Title IV-D’s enforcement provisions.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: vacate and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s provision-specific separation of statutory right, § 1983 remedy, defendant, causation, and permissible relief.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Kennedy, agrees that the systemwide substantial-compliance claim fails under controlling precedent. He reserves the more fundamental question whether beneficiaries of federal-state Spending Clause arrangements possess third-party rights enforceable through § 1983. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/329/))

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Livadas v. Bradshaw* is applied and clarified. A complex federal funding statute must be parsed provision by provision, and categorical § 1983 displacement cannot be established merely by pointing to aggregate federal oversight and funding sanctions.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, controlling holdings, and Scalia concurrence joined by Kennedy match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/329/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for the complaint, systemwide claim, provision-specific analysis, preclusion holding, judgment, and separate concurrence. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/329/))

### New Mexico Department of Human Services v. Joseph A. and Josephine A., 69 F.3d 1081

Argued December 3, 1996 | Decided April 21, 1997

From: the Tenth Circuit’s vacatur of an order dissolving a 1983 foster-care consent decree. The decree permitted termination after twelve consecutive months of substantial and continuous compliance. The adopted Special Master’s report stated broad conclusions but did not adequately explain its treatment of low compliance figures, adverse child-specific case studies, disputed data reliability, or the methodology connecting subsidiary facts to the decree’s essential purposes. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/69/1081/609425/))

Questions presented: how the decree’s substantial-and-continuous-compliance condition is construed, and what subsidiary findings and explanation Rule 52(a) requires before the decree may be dissolved.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Missouri v. Jenkins* requires institutional relief to remain connected to the legal obligation being remedied and requires withdrawal of federal control when compliance is complete. *Rufo* and *Dowell* supply the surviving equitable background, but the decree’s own termination clause governs this proceeding.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Tenth Circuit’s remand remains in force. The District Court shall retain the decree while making adequate findings on the existing record or conducting lawful additional proceedings. It shall terminate the decree when the Department establishes the agreed twelve-month condition under the correct standard.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Substantial compliance and Rule 52 findings

Holding: this institutional consent decree may be dissolved only on reviewable findings that the Department maintained substantial and continuous compliance with the decree’s essential purposes for twelve consecutive months. Findings that state ultimate conclusions without explaining material deviations, methodology, and supporting subsidiary facts do not satisfy Rule 52(a).

Operative rule: substantial compliance does not require perfection or literal satisfaction of every numerical criterion. It requires performance equivalent to compliance with the decree’s essential purposes, with only immaterial deviations. The court must identify the governing obligation, explain whether departures from specified criteria materially impair the decree’s objectives, state how it evaluated quantitative and qualitative evidence, and make enough subsidiary findings to permit meaningful appellate review.

Reasoning: the decree’s central purposes include fair, reasonable, and timely decisionmaking and durable procedures for permanent placement. The report acknowledged serious shortfalls and cases in which children remained in temporary care, yet often declared substantial compliance without explaining why the deviations were immaterial. It discounted adverse case studies without showing that they were aberrational, failed to resolve challenges to the reliability of the principal data system, and did not disclose a methodology permitting review. Appellate judges may not reconstruct those missing findings from a voluminous record. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/69/1081/609425/))

Application: the termination order was properly vacated. The District Court must apply the decree’s text and the stated findings rule, but this Court makes no finding whether New Mexico ultimately complied.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not require perfect compliance, make every numerical target indispensable, permit expansion of the decree beyond its terms, or authorize federal control after the termination condition has been established. Termination is neither a reward nor a sanction; it follows from proof of the condition written into the federal judgment.

Legal basis: Federal Rules of Civil Procedure 52(a) and 53, the consent decree, and federal equitable authority over judgments.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the decree-specific substantial-compliance rule, the requirement of reviewable subsidiary findings, and the direction that federal control end once the agreed termination condition is proved.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Missouri v. Jenkins* is applied without alteration. Its violation-remedy nexus and withdrawal principles do not replace a consent decree’s own termination clause. The present decision adds a decree-specific Rule 52 requirement for reviewable findings before systemic relief is dissolved.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulated Court unanimously affirms the historical Tenth Circuit’s vacatur and remand, adopts its essential-purposes understanding of substantial compliance, and preserves its insistence on subsidiary findings adequate for appellate review. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/69/1081/609425/))

Research sources: the Tenth Circuit opinion for the decree, termination clause, Special Master’s methodology, deficiencies in the findings, governing standard, and historical judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/69/1081/609425/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Board of County Commissioners of Bryan County v. Brown, 520 U.S. 397 (1997)

Argued November 5, 1996 | Decided April 28, 1997

From: the Fifth Circuit’s affirmance of a jury judgment holding Bryan County liable under § 1983 for Sheriff Moore’s final-policymaker decision to hire Reserve Deputy Stacy Burns. Burns later used excessive force while arresting Jill Brown. Moore had obtained but not closely reviewed records showing one assault-and-battery conviction, guilty pleas involving resisting arrest and public drunkenness, numerous traffic violations, and other recent lawbreaking. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520397/usrep520397.pdf))

Question presented: whether a facially lawful single hiring decision by a final municipal policymaker reflected the culpability and direct causation required for municipal liability.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Plakas v. Drinski* requires a constitutional injury caused by municipal policy. *Swint v. Chambers County Commission* preserves the distinction between municipal liability and individual-officer immunity. No controlling simulated decision had prescribed the standard for one final-policymaker hiring decision.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The jury’s municipal-liability judgment remains in force. The Court decides neither Sheriff Moore’s dismissed individual liability nor any training theory not included in the Fifth Circuit judgment under review.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Breyer | Stevens and Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Accepts the Court’s governing rule while urging future reconsideration of *Monell*’s broader distinction between municipal policy liability and respondeat superior |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas | Would vacate and remand | Would require a plainly obvious consequence approaching practical inevitability and would hold the record legally insufficient |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Municipal liability for a final policymaker’s single hire

Holding: a final municipal policymaker’s single hiring decision may support § 1983 liability when the policymaker consciously disregards a substantial and highly predictable, applicant-specific risk that conferring the particular governmental authority will produce the kind of constitutional injury that occurs, and the hiring decision directly moves that injury.

Operative rule: the plaintiff must prove an underlying constitutional violation, final policymaking authority in the relevant field, deliberate municipal fault, and a direct causal connection. Negligent screening, favoritism, general unsuitability, or misconduct unrelated to the authority later abused is insufficient. Deliberate indifference does not require practical inevitability, which would collapse the inquiry into intentional authorization, but the risk must be substantially more focused and predictable than the generalized possibility that any employee may misuse official power.

Reasoning: *Pembaur* establishes that one decision by a final policymaker may be municipal policy, while *Canton* requires deliberate indifference and direct causation when the policy is facially lawful. Those principles do not justify either automatic liability from the act of hiring or near-immunity unless the applicant’s later violation was inevitable. The required connection is established when the applicant-specific information corresponds closely to the coercive authority granted and the constitutional injury later inflicted.

Application: Moore obtained records showing a recent pattern of repeated lawbreaking, assaultive misconduct, resistance to official authority, intoxication-related conduct, and numerous driving offenses. He nevertheless granted Burns arrest authority without meaningful review. A rational jury could find that Moore consciously avoided information bearing directly on Burns’s fitness to exercise physical coercion, that the resulting excessive-force risk was substantial and highly predictable, and that the hiring decision was the moving force behind Brown’s injury.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not constitutionalize state hiring qualifications, impose municipal liability for every careless hire, or transfer an officer’s lack of qualified immunity to the municipality. It does not decide liability arising from training, supervision, or retention after later notice.

Legal basis: 42 U.S.C. § 1983.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s applicant-specific deliberate-indifference standard, its rejection of practical inevitability, and its conclusion that the completed jury record permits municipal fault and direct causation to be found.

#### Material Separate Writings

Breyer, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg, accepts the Court’s disposition and rule but argues that the complexity exposed by the case supplies a substantial reason to reconsider *Monell* in a case that squarely presents the question. That broader proposition is noncontrolling.

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, would hold that inadequate scrutiny supports liability only when the particular constitutional violation was the plainly obvious consequence of hiring the applicant. The dissent concludes that Burns’s background showed poor judgment and general unsuitability but did not make excessive force in an arrest sufficiently obvious.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Pembaur v. Cincinnati* and *City of Canton v. Harris* are clarified. A final policymaker’s hiring decision can be municipal policy, but attribution alone does not establish fault or causation. The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for a single final-policymaker hire and preserves *Monell*’s rejection of respondeat superior.

*Plakas* and *Swint* remain unchanged.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court vacated and remanded 5-4, with the historical Chief Justice joining O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. In the simulation, Stone rejects that position and joins the historical judgment-opposing bloc. Ginsburg, who historically joined Breyer’s separate dissent, joins the Court’s rigorous but noninevitability formulation, producing a 5-4 affirmance. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520397/usrep520397.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the hiring record, trial posture, historical judgment, opinion lineup, and competing culpability formulations. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520397/usrep520397.pdf))

### Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno, 76 F.3d 1294

Argued December 4, 1996 | Decided April 28, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date

From: the Third Circuit’s affirmance of dismissal of Pic-A-State’s pre-enforcement action. Pic-A-State accepted Pennsylvania customer orders for interests in other States’ lottery tickets, transmitted procurement information interstate, and used agents to buy and retain the physical tickets in the lottery States. Congress amended 18 U.S.C. § 1301 specifically to prohibit that cross-border procurement mechanism unless the affected States entered an authorizing agreement. Pic-A-State ceased operations upon enactment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/76/1294/519749/))

Questions presented: whether the pre-enforcement controversy is ripe and whether Congress may prohibit the interstate transmission of information used commercially to procure out-of-state lottery interests.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *United States v. Lopez*, *United States v. Harris*, and *United States v. Robertson* preserve congressional authority over interstate channels, instrumentalities, things, and transactions while requiring a genuine constitutional connection rather than a nominal interstate label.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The federal judgment sustaining the Interstate Wagering Amendment remains in force.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Pre-enforcement justiciability

Holding: Pic-A-State presents a ripe Article III controversy because the amendment directly proscribed its existing business, caused immediate cessation, imposed serious criminal consequences on resumption, and was not accompanied by a governmental disavowal of enforcement.

Reasoning: the injury is completed and concrete rather than dependent on a speculative enforcement chain. The challenge is predominantly legal, and requiring Pic-A-State to resume the prohibited conduct before obtaining review would add no useful factual development.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not eliminate ordinary standing or ripeness requirements for a claimant whose planned conduct is uncertain, materially different from the enacted prohibition, or subject only to a remote enforcement possibility.

Legal basis: Article III.

##### Interstate lottery procurement

Holding: the Interstate Wagering Amendment regulates interstate commerce itself because it reaches a knowing interstate transmission made as an operative part of a commercial transaction to procure an interest in another State’s lottery.

Operative rule: Congress need not rely on aggregate substantial effects when the regulated conduct is the cross-border commercial transaction or transmission itself. The offense-specific interstate element must perform genuine work by identifying the interstate procurement mechanism, not merely attach an interstate label to otherwise local gambling.

Reasoning: Pic-A-State’s business used interstate communications to accomplish the economic substance of an out-of-state lottery purchase while leaving the physical ticket with an agent. Congress regulated that interstate procurement service rather than local gambling viewed through generalized effects. The Constitution does not make federal authority depend on whether the commercial interest is represented by a paper ticket physically carried across the border.

Limits and questions left open: the holding does not authorize federal criminalization of purely local gambling through a nominal jurisdictional element or generalized moral concern.

Legal basis: Article I, § 8, clause 3, and 18 U.S.C. § 1301.

##### Interstate-agreement exception

Holding: Congress may unmistakably authorize state restrictions that would otherwise be prohibited only by the dormant Commerce Clause. Section 1301’s federal prohibition and express interstate-agreement exception provide that authorization.

Reasoning: the exception allows the affected States jointly to displace the federal prohibition. Because the dormant Commerce Clause is an implication drawn in the absence of congressional action, an express exercise of Congress’s own commerce power may alter that implication.

Limits and questions left open: congressional authorization cannot permit violation of an independent constitutional prohibition, and the Court does not decide the validity of a materially different state restriction outside § 1301’s agreement structure.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s pre-enforcement ruling, its treatment of the interstate transmission as the regulated commercial transaction itself, and its conditional congressional-authorization holding.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*United States v. Lopez*, *United States v. Harris*, and *United States v. Robertson* are applied without alteration. The decision adds the first controlling simulated rule for a commercial transaction completed through an interstate computer-assisted procurement transmission.

It also confirms that unmistakable congressional authorization may remove a dormant Commerce Clause restriction without altering any independent constitutional limit.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulation affirms the Third Circuit’s judgment but rests principally on regulation of the interstate transaction itself, rather than on a generalized aggregation of gambling’s economic effects. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/76/1294/519749/))

Research sources: the Third Circuit opinion for Pic-A-State’s business model, cessation, ripeness, congressional objectives, and Commerce Clause treatment. ([law.justia.com](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/76/1294/519749/?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [law.justia.com](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/76/1294/519749/?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [law.justia.com](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/76/1294/519749/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

### Richards v. Wisconsin, 520 U.S. 385 (1997)

Argued March 24, 1997 | Decided April 28, 1997

From: the Wisconsin Supreme Court’s affirmance of denial of suppression under a categorical rule permitting unannounced entry whenever officers execute a felony drug warrant. At execution, Richards opened a chained motel-room door, recognized police, abruptly closed it, and retreated. Officers identified themselves while forcing entry and found cocaine and evidence of attempted escape. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520385/usrep520385.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520385/usrep520385.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Question presented: whether the Fourth Amendment permits a blanket felony-drug exception to knock and announce, and whether the particular entry was reasonable.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Wilson v. Arkansas* makes knock and announce part of Fourth Amendment reasonableness and permits exceptions for danger, futility, escape, or destruction of evidence, but left the operative quantum of justification unresolved.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The evidence remains admissible. Wisconsin may not rely on an offense-wide felony-drug exception in future cases.

Justice Stevens delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Case-specific justification for unannounced entry

Holding: the Fourth Amendment permits no categorical no-knock exception for felony drug investigations. Officers may dispense with ordinary announcement when particular facts create reasonable suspicion that announcing would be dangerous or futile, permit escape, or materially inhibit the investigation through destruction of evidence.

Operative rule: reasonable suspicion is less demanding than probable cause but must arise from the circumstances of the actual execution. The disposable character of drugs or the charged offense alone does not satisfy the standard. A magistrate’s advance denial of no-knock authority does not prevent officers from responding to later exigent facts, and advance authorization does not eliminate review of the execution actually undertaken.

Reasoning: an offense-wide exception would make the protection recognized in *Wilson* disappear whenever officials selected a drug charge. Reasonable suspicion accommodates the practical need for prompt decisions while preserving judicial review of a factual basis connected to danger, futility, escape, or evidence loss.

Application: the circumstances materially changed when Richards apparently recognized police, immediately closed and secured the door, and retreated into a room containing readily disposable narcotics. The officers could reasonably suspect that further delay would facilitate destruction or escape. Their brief response was tied to that development rather than to Wisconsin’s categorical rule.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not establish a fixed waiting period after announcement or require officers to disregard developments occurring after a warrant issues.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm while rejecting Wisconsin’s categorical rule.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the reasonable-suspicion threshold, the rejection of an offense-based exception, and the conclusion that Richards’s recognition and immediate retreat supplied case-specific justification.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Wilson v. Arkansas* is clarified. The exceptions identified there now require reasonable suspicion grounded in the execution circumstances. No separate offense category eliminates knock and announce.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, controlling rule, and application match the historical decision. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520385/usrep520385.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520385/usrep520385.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the motel-room entry, Wisconsin’s categorical rule, the reasonable-suspicion standard, judgment, and opinion authorship. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520385/usrep520385.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520385/usrep520385.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

### Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority, 72 F.3d 1133

Argued January 13, 1997 | Decided May 5, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date

From: the Third Circuit’s reversal of a $125,000 jury verdict for a SEPTA employee whose named prescription records revealed medications associated with HIV and AIDS. SEPTA received employee names, medication names, and costs through its self-insured prescription plan. Benefits personnel examined Doe’s prescriptions, researched the medications, inferred his condition, and discussed the information with additional personnel and physicians even though aggregate or coded data could perform the relevant utilization-monitoring function. ([Global Health Rights](https://www.globalhealthrights.org/wp-content/uploads/2013/02/3rd-Cir-1995-Doe-v.-SEPTA.pdf))

Question presented: whether SEPTA’s plan-administration interests justified the identified access, investigation, use, and disclosure as a matter of law under Rule 50.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Sheets v. Salt Lake County* protects against deliberate governmental dissemination of highly intimate information entrusted for a limited purpose when the disclosure serves no connected governmental function. *Hodge v. Jones* permits confidential, accurate, and nonconsequential retention without new investigative use or material dissemination.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The Third Circuit shall reinstate the District Court’s denial of judgment as a matter of law and address any preserved Rule 59 or damages issues it did not reach. The Court decides no ungranted municipal-liability, personal-immunity, or damages question.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm judgment for the defendants | Rejects extension of substantive due process from purposeless public dissemination to internal benefits administration |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Governmental use of identified medical information

Holding: identifiable prescription records, particularly records revealing HIV status, fall within the Fourteenth Amendment’s limited protection against unjustified governmental acquisition, examination, use, and disclosure of highly intimate information.

Operative rule: personally identifying access must remain genuinely and proportionately connected to the actual governmental function asserted. Relevant considerations include the information’s sensitivity, whether the function requires names rather than aggregate or coded data, who receives access, the use made of the information, and the safeguards actually maintained. A legitimate administrative purpose does not create open-ended authority to examine or circulate every identified detail received through the program.

Reasoning: SEPTA had a legitimate interest in monitoring prescription costs, eligibility, and concrete indications of fraud or abuse. That interest justified utilization data and could justify identified review when an individualized concern arose. It did not establish as a matter of law that officials needed named reports for ordinary utilization analysis or could continue researching and communicating an inferred diagnosis after the administrative need had been satisfied.

Application: under Rule 50, the evidence must be viewed in Doe’s favor. A rational jury could find that names were unnecessary for the relevant cost analysis, that the identified prescriptions were deliberately examined after their exceptional sensitivity became apparent, that the inferred diagnosis was communicated beyond those having a demonstrated need, and that no established safeguard confined such use. SEPTA’s later adoption of de-identified reports supplied additional evidence that the asserted function did not require continued named review. ([Global Health Rights](https://www.globalhealthrights.org/wp-content/uploads/2013/02/3rd-Cir-1995-Doe-v.-SEPTA.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not bar aggregate or coded auditing, an identified inquiry prompted by a concrete fraud or eligibility concern, a disclosure required by law, or confidential retention alone. It governs no private employer and decides no damages amount, individual immunity, or municipal-policy question.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment and Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 50.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the proportionate-connection rule, the distinction between legitimate utilization monitoring and unnecessary identified investigation, and the verdict-protective application of Rule 50.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would confine *Sheets* to deliberate public dissemination serving no official function. The dissent would treat SEPTA’s internal administration of a self-insured plan as constitutionally sufficient once a legitimate cost-control purpose existed, leaving misuse of confidential information to statutory and state-law remedies.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Sheets v. Salt Lake County* is extended from purposeless public dissemination to deliberate governmental examination, use, and internal disclosure exceeding the function that justified identified access. The connection between the information and a legitimate function must be genuine and proportionate, not merely conceivable.

*Hodge v. Jones* is distinguished. *Hodge* concerned accurate, confidential retention without new investigative use, material dissemination, or a concrete consequence.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The Third Circuit historically recognized a confidentiality interest but held SEPTA’s interests sufficient as a matter of law. The simulation reverses because simulated *Sheets*, the evidence concerning de-identified alternatives, and Rule 50’s verdict-protective posture permit the jury’s finding. ([Global Health Rights](https://www.globalhealthrights.org/wp-content/uploads/2013/02/3rd-Cir-1995-Doe-v.-SEPTA.pdf))

Research sources: the Third Circuit record for the prescription reports, identified investigation, administrative purposes, jury verdict, Rule 50 disposition, and divided panel treatment. ([Global Health Rights](https://www.globalhealthrights.org/wp-content/uploads/2013/02/3rd-Cir-1995-Doe-v.-SEPTA.pdf))

### Inter-Modal Rail Employees Association v. Atchison, Topeka & Santa Fe Railway Co., 520 U.S. 510 (1997)

Argued March 17, 1997 | Decided May 12, 1997

From: the Ninth Circuit’s dismissal of employees’ claim that a contracting restructuring was intended to interfere with attainment of health and other welfare benefits. The court permitted a pension-interference theory but treated nonvesting welfare benefits as outside ERISA § 510. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520510/usrep520510.pdf))

Question presented: whether § 510’s protection of any right to which a participant may become entitled under a plan excludes welfare benefits because ERISA does not require those benefits to vest.

Controlling simulated law before decision: no prior simulated holding construed § 510’s attainment language. *Varity Corp. v. Howe* and *John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust* require function-specific application of ERISA’s enacted terms.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Ninth Circuit’s affirmance of dismissal of the welfare-benefit theory is reversed. The employees may proceed on that theory, subject to proof of the remaining statutory elements and any preserved defenses.

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Section 510 and nonvesting welfare benefits

Holding: ERISA § 510 reaches discharge or other prohibited action intended to interfere with attainment of rights under an employee welfare benefit plan even though those benefits are not subject to ERISA’s statutory vesting requirements.

Operative rule: a claimant must identify a right under an ERISA plan that the participant may become entitled to and prove the specific intent, prohibited action, causation, proper defendant, and other requirements of § 510. Vesting is not an additional element unless the enacted plan right itself makes vesting relevant.

Reasoning: ERISA expressly defines plan to include both pension and welfare plans. Congress used narrower pension and nonforfeitable terminology elsewhere but did not use it in § 510. The forward-looking phrase may become entitled likewise reaches interference before the participant completes every condition for receipt.

An employer’s authority to amend or terminate a welfare plan through the lawful plan process does not authorize it to avoid that process by discharging participants for a purpose § 510 forbids.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not vest welfare benefits, freeze plan terms, determine whether the employees had already attained a particular right, find prohibited intent, or decide the liability of any defendant that was neither the employer nor a responsible plan actor.

Legal basis: ERISA § 510, 29 U.S.C. § 1140.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: vacate and remand, reversing the welfare-benefit dismissal.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the enacted distinction between plans generally and pension or nonforfeitable benefits specifically.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule concerning § 510 interference with welfare-benefit rights. *Varity* and *John Hancock* are applied through their function-specific treatment of ERISA’s terms.

*Curtiss-Wright Corp. v. Schoonejongen* is distinguished. Employer authority to amend a welfare plan does not authorize a termination undertaken for the purpose prohibited by § 510.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, and controlling statutory interpretation match the historical decision. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520510/usrep520510.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520510/usrep520510.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520510/usrep520510.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the plan structure, welfare-benefit dismissal, statutory definitions, judgment, and opinion authorship. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520510/usrep520510.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520510/usrep520510.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com), [tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520510/usrep520510.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

### United States v. Ortiz, 84 F.3d 977

Argued January 14, 1997 | Decided May 12, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date

From: the Seventh Circuit’s affirmance of Julio Ortiz’s convictions after officers seized a digital pager at arrest and immediately pressed its controls to display stored numerical messages. The court treated the retrieval as a search but upheld it because a pager’s finite memory could be overwritten by incoming pages. The record contained general information about pager memory but no device-specific finding concerning capacity, overwrite behavior, or an imminent incoming message. ([law.justia.com](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/84/977/568395/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Question presented: whether activating the pager was a search distinct from its physical seizure and whether the contemporaneous retrieval was valid incident to arrest.

Controlling simulated law before decision: no simulated holding governed stored pager data. The Fourth Amendment continued to distinguish seizure of an item from examination of concealed contents. Same-term *Richards v. Wisconsin* requires an exception to remain connected to its actual justification but concerns the separate knock-and-announce rule.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4. Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg.

The Seventh Circuit’s judgment as to Ortiz remains in force on the pager-search question. The automobile search, firearm evidence, wristwatch retrieval, Hurtado appeal, and unrelated sentencing matters remain outside the grant.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, and Breyer in full; Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg as to the classification of pager activation as a search | Supports affirmance | Five-Justice search-incident holding; unanimous search classification |
| Dissent in part | Stone | Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg | Would reverse and remand as to Ortiz | Requires a concrete and imminent device-specific overwrite risk and the absence of a nonsearch preservation method |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Activation of stored pager memory

Holding: pressing a pager’s control to display numerical messages concealed in its electronic memory is a Fourth Amendment search distinct from the lawful physical seizure of the device.

Reasoning: physical seizure restrains possession. Activation exposes communicative content that was not otherwise visible. The fact that the device performs the retrieval electronically rather than through opening a mechanical compartment does not eliminate the search.

Limits and questions left open: merely securing a powered device without exposing stored information is not itself the search decided here.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s opinion is joined on this question by all nine Justices and supplies a unanimous holding.

##### Immediate retrieval incident to arrest

Holding: officers may contemporaneously retrieve the short numerical memory of a pager seized from an arrestee when retrieval occurs at the place of arrest and addresses the ordinary risk that incoming pages will overwrite the finite stored sequence.

Operative rule: the authority is confined to an immediate and limited display of the pager’s then-stored numeric messages. Officers need not establish that a particular incoming page is already in transit or prove the device’s exact memory capacity before performing that confined retrieval. The rule does not authorize delayed exploration after the contemporaneous preservation justification has ended or examination of materially broader, durable, or unrelated stores of information.

Reasoning: the categorical search-incident rule allows officers to secure weapons and prevent loss of evidence associated with an arrest without litigating fine factual distinctions during the arrest itself. A pager is designed to receive new transmissions, retains only a finite sequence, and may replace earlier numbers as new pages arrive. The limited contemporaneous retrieval here was closely tied to preserving the evidence in the condition in which officers found it.

Application: the agent activated the pager immediately at the parking lot and displayed the stored numbers without conducting a delayed or expansive examination. The search therefore fell within the confined rule.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment search-incident-to-arrest doctrine.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s opinion, joined on this question by O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, and Breyer, supplies a five-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse the judgment as to Ortiz and remand.

Public role: joins the Court’s conclusion that pager activation is a search and dissents from the search-incident holding and judgment.

Announced ground: Stone concludes that search incident to arrest does not categorically open secured electronic memory. Immediate retrieval should be permitted only when the known operation of the particular device and the actual circumstances establish a concrete, imminent risk of overwrite before a warrant can reasonably be obtained, and no nonsearch means will preserve the information. The general observations in this record do not satisfy that showing.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for immediate retrieval of stored pager messages incident to arrest.

Same-term *Richards v. Wisconsin* is distinguished. *Richards* rejects an offense-based exception detached from the circumstances of entry. The present majority relies on the distinct categorical search-incident authority governing an item seized from the arrestee and confines the electronic retrieval to the contemporaneous preservation function.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulation affirms the Seventh Circuit’s pager ruling but expressly classifies activation as a search and confines the exception to immediate retrieval of short, volatile numeric memory. Stone and three Justices would require a device-specific preservation showing. ([law.justia.com](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/84/977/568395/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Research sources: the Seventh Circuit opinion for the arrest, physical seizure, pager activation, finite-memory rationale, judgment, and issues outside the simulated grant. ([law.justia.com](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/84/977/568395/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

### Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board, 520 U.S. 471 (1997)

Argued December 9, 1996 | Decided May 12, 1997

From: direct appeals from a three-judge District Court judgment preclearing Bossier Parish School Board’s twelve-district plan. The plan created no majority-Black district, was stipulated not to be retrogressive from the existing school-board plan, and was adopted instead of an NAACP alternative containing two majority-Black districts. The lower court held that an alleged § 2 violation was not an independent reason to deny § 5 preclearance and appeared to treat dilution evidence as categorically irrelevant to purpose. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520471/usrep520471.pdf))

Questions presented: whether § 5’s effect prong incorporates § 2’s results standard and whether evidence also supporting a dilution theory may be considered in the § 5 purpose inquiry.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Beer v. United States* makes retrogression from the operative baseline the § 5 effect inquiry. Simulated *Johnson v. De Grandy* separately governs § 2 totality and nonmaximization. Same-term *Lopez v. Monterey County* and *Young v. Fordice* govern § 5 coverage, baseline, and enforcement sequence but do not determine substantive effect or the full purpose inquiry.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Whether a § 2 violation independently establishes prohibited § 5 effect | District Court’s legal conclusion sustained, 7-2 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens, Souter | Section 5 effect remains a comparison with the operative baseline. |
| Purpose-evidence treatment and preclearance judgment | Vacated and remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The District Court shall reconsider purpose without categorically excluding evidence merely because it also bears on dilution. |

##### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The judgments in both consolidated appeals are vacated and the cases remanded. The three-judge court shall keep § 5 effect separate from § 2 liability, consider all relevant purpose evidence under the governing evidentiary framework, and make any necessary findings. It shall not treat preclearance as immunity from a separate § 2 action.

The Court does not decide whether the Board acted with prohibited purpose, whether the plan violates § 2, whether § 5 purpose extends beyond retrogressive intent, or whether a same-decision burden or defense applies.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas in full; Ginsburg and Breyer except insofar as the purpose-scope reservation conflicts with Breyer’s concurrence | Supports vacatur and remand | Seven-Justice § 2 and effect holding; all joined propositions at the stated scope |
| Concurrence | Thomas | None | Supports vacatur and remand | Emphasizes the distinct statutory text, benchmarks, and procedures of §§ 2 and 5 |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Breyer | Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand | Agrees on § 5 effect but would hold that the purpose prong reaches an intent to impose unconstitutional vote dilution even without retrogression |
| Dissent in part and concurrence in part | Stevens | Souter | Supports the remand but opposes the § 2 and effect ruling | Would permit denial of preclearance for a clear § 2 violation and would define prohibited purpose more broadly |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Relationship between § 5 effect and § 2

Holding: a violation of § 2 does not independently establish prohibited effect under § 5. Section 5 effect compares the proposed change with the jurisdiction’s lawful operative baseline and asks whether the change is retrogressive.

Operative rule: § 2 and § 5 remain distinct. Section 2 uses the statutory totality inquiry and an undiluted benchmark. Section 5 effect uses the existing lawful practice as its comparison point. Preclearance does not decide a separate § 2 claim, and a possible § 2 violation does not substitute for the § 5 effect inquiry.

Reasoning: Congress assigned the provisions different geographic scopes, burdens, benchmarks, and procedures. Treating § 2 liability as automatic § 5 effect would replace the enacted retrogression comparison with an undiluted hypothetical and erase those differences.

Limits and questions left open: the Court neither decides whether the Board’s plan violates § 2 nor requires proportional representation or maximization.

Legal basis: Voting Rights Act §§ 2 and 5.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion, joined on this issue by Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a seven-Justice majority.

##### Dilution evidence in the purpose inquiry

Holding: evidence of dilutive impact, rejected alternatives, historical context, decision sequence, procedural departures, official statements, and asserted pretext may be relevant to why a covered jurisdiction selected a plan. It may not be categorically excluded merely because the same evidence also supports a § 2 theory.

Operative rule: evidentiary relevance does not transfer a legal conclusion from one statute to the other. Dilutive effect and the availability of a less dilutive alternative are evidence rather than automatic proof of prohibited § 5 purpose. The covered jurisdiction retains the statutory burden governing preclearance, and the factfinder must evaluate purpose through the complete circumstances.

Reasoning: one fact may bear on multiple legal questions without making those questions identical. A plan’s concentrated racial impact, the rejection of feasible alternatives, departures from ordinary procedures, or a weak asserted explanation can make a discriminatory purpose more probable even though none independently establishes the ultimate statutory conclusion.

Application: portions of the District Court’s opinion suggest that it excluded dilution evidence because it was associated with § 2. Because that legal error may have distorted the purpose inquiry, the preclearance judgment cannot stand.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not determine whether Bossier Parish acted with prohibited purpose, define the complete scope of § 5 purpose, or establish a same-decision rule.

Legal basis: Voting Rights Act § 5 and the received *Arlington Heights* evidentiary framework.

Authority of the proposition: all nine Justices agree that dilution-related evidence may not be categorically excluded from the purpose inquiry and that remand is required.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: sustain the District Court’s separation of § 5 effect from § 2; vacate the preclearance judgments and remand on purpose.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinct statutory benchmarks, the relevance of dilution evidence to purpose, and the reservation of the full purpose boundary and any same-decision issue.

#### Material Separate Writings

Breyer, joined by Ginsburg, would hold that § 5 purpose reaches an intent to cause vote dilution prohibited by the Constitution even when the plan is not retrogressive. That proposition is not necessary to the judgment and is noncontrolling.

Stevens, joined by Souter, agrees that the case must be remanded but would also permit denial of preclearance when the proposed change clearly violates § 2. They reject the Court’s strict separation of the effect inquiries.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Beer v. United States* is applied and clarified. Its retrogression benchmark remains the controlling measure of § 5 effect.

Simulated *Johnson v. De Grandy* remains the separate rule governing § 2 totality, proportionality, and nonmaximization. Same-term *Lopez v. Monterey County* and *Young v. Fordice* remain controlling on coverage, baseline, and the preclearance sequence but are not extended into the substantive effect inquiry.

The decision creates a controlling purpose-evidence rule: facts relevant to dilution may also be relevant to discriminatory purpose, while the ultimate legal inquiries remain distinct.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated issue alignments and opinion structure match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the position of the historical Chief Justice. The formal historical judgment, like the simulated judgment, was vacatur and remand with Stevens and Souter concurring in the remand. The 7-2 division concerns the § 2 and § 5 effect issue. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520471/usrep520471.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the competing plans, stipulated nonretrogression, § 2 objection, historical opinion topology, purpose-evidence treatment, and separate writings. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520471/usrep520471.pdf))

### Camps Newfound/Owatonna, Inc. v. Town of Harrison, 520 U.S. 564 (1997)

Argued October 9, 1996 | Decided May 19, 1997

From: the Maine Supreme Judicial Court’s judgment sustaining denial of a charitable property-tax exemption to a nonprofit summer camp because approximately 95 percent of its campers came from outside Maine and it charged more than the statutory weekly threshold. Comparable charitable institutions principally serving Maine residents remained eligible for the exemption. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520564/usrep520564.pdf))

Question presented: whether conditioning the tax exemption on the residence of the institution’s principal beneficiaries discriminates against interstate commerce, and whether the tax treatment can be sustained as a subsidy or market-participant measure.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Oregon Waste Systems* applies demanding review to origin-based fiscal discrimination. *C & A Carbone* condemns reservation of a local market for favored local activity. *West Lynn Creamery* requires integrated review of linked fiscal treatment.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

The discriminatory residency condition may not be enforced against the camp. The Maine courts shall provide the exemption and any refund or prospective relief available under state remedial law.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas and Ginsburg | Would affirm | Treats the exemption as a permissible State decision to subsidize charities benefiting residents |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would affirm | Questions the dormant Commerce Clause’s foundations and would not extend it to this tax exemption |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Residency-based charitable tax treatment

Holding: Maine violates the dormant Commerce Clause by denying a charitable property-tax exemption because the institution principally serves nonresidents while granting the exemption to comparable charities serving residents.

Reasoning: the camp markets and sells a recreational and charitable service nationally and brings customers across state lines to receive it in Maine. The tax classification increases the cost of that interstate service precisely because the beneficiaries are nonresidents. Discrimination against the provider according to the origin of its customers is discrimination against interstate commerce even though the provider itself is incorporated in Maine.

The exemption is part of Maine’s tax system, not a direct public purchase or participation in the market. Calling favorable tax treatment a subsidy does not remove the discriminatory condition. Maine may assist residents through direct public services, resident benefits, general-revenue grants, or neutral exemption criteria without penalizing a charity that attracts interstate users.

Application: the camp’s predominantly nonresident enrollment triggered more than $20,000 in annual property tax that a comparable resident-serving charity would not bear. Maine identified no objective that could not be served through reasonable nondiscriminatory alternatives.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not require States to subsidize charitable activity everywhere, invalidate direct resident public benefits, or prevent neutral criteria based on nonprofit status, property use, finances, or regulatory compliance.

Legal basis: dormant Commerce Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s treatment of the residency condition as facial fiscal discrimination, its rejection of the subsidy and market-participant defenses, and its preservation of nondiscriminatory charitable policies.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas and Ginsburg, characterizes the exemption as a State’s decision to devote its own resources to institutions relieving resident needs. Thomas, joined by Scalia, separately questions the legitimacy of extending the dormant Commerce Clause beyond historically established restrictions. Neither position controls.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Oregon Waste Systems*, *C & A Carbone*, and *West Lynn Creamery* are applied and extended to a tax exemption conditioned on the interstate identity of a charity’s beneficiaries.

Same-term *Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno* is distinguished. *Pic-A-State* involved unmistakable congressional authorization to alter dormant Commerce Clause restrictions. Maine identifies no comparable federal authorization here.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 5-4. Stone rejects the historical Chief Justice’s dissenting position and joins Justice Stevens’s opinion, producing a 6-3 simulated judgment. The remaining historical alignments are unchanged. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520564/usrep520564.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the camp’s operation, residency condition, tax treatment, market-participant and subsidy arguments, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520564/usrep520564.pdf))

### Edwards v. Balisok, 520 U.S. 641 (1997)

Argued November 13, 1996 | Decided May 19, 1997

From: the Ninth Circuit’s judgment permitting a prisoner’s § 1983 challenge to procedures used in a disciplinary hearing that resulted in loss of thirty days of earned good-time credit. Balisok alleged that the hearing officer concealed exculpatory statements, refused requested questions, failed to explain the finding, and acted with deceit and bias. He sought damages, declaratory relief, and prospective date-stamping relief while reserving restoration of credits for habeas. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520641/usrep520641.pdf))

Question presented: whether *Heck v. Humphrey* applies to procedural claims whose success would necessarily invalidate prison discipline affecting the duration of custody, and how separable prospective claims should proceed.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Heck* temporarily channels a § 1983 claim when success would necessarily establish the invalidity of an outstanding conviction or sentence and habeas remains available. Simulated *Sandin v. Conner* governs the existence of prison liberty interests but does not determine the proper remedial channel once earned credits affecting release have been revoked.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The deceit-and-bias damages and declaratory claims shall be dismissed without prejudice while the disciplinary judgment remains outstanding. The lower courts shall separately consider prospective or other claims whose success would not necessarily invalidate the loss of good-time credits.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | Souter and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes that each alleged procedural defect must be classified according to whether success would necessarily imply invalidity |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Deceit, bias, and the outstanding disciplinary judgment

Holding: a prisoner may not pursue § 1983 damages or declaratory relief based on alleged deceit and bias in a disciplinary hearing when success would necessarily imply the invalidity of an outstanding deprivation of good-time credits.

Operative rule: the consequence of the claim, not its procedural label or requested remedy alone, controls. A challenge to procedure is channeled when the asserted defect would necessarily vitiate the disciplinary judgment. A genuinely separable procedural claim that could succeed without implying invalidity remains immediately cognizable.

Reasoning: a ruling that the decisionmaker dishonestly concealed exculpatory evidence and adjudicated the matter with unconstitutional bias would invalidate the disciplinary judgment regardless of whether some evidence supported the charge. Balisok cannot avoid *Heck* by requesting damages for the process while reserving restoration of credits for another proceeding.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not bar every procedural challenge to discipline, require invalidation before a claim concerning conditions unrelated to custody duration, or determine whether Balisok can obtain habeas relief.

Legal basis: 42 U.S.C. § 1983, the habeas statutes, and *Heck v. Humphrey*.

##### Prospective and separable relief

Holding: prospective relief that regulates future receipt or date-stamping of witness statements does not ordinarily imply the invalidity of an earlier credit loss and may proceed under § 1983 if Article III standing and ordinary equitable requirements are satisfied.

Application: neither lower court adjudicated the prospective claim, and its factual and equitable predicates were not briefed in the Supreme Court. It is remanded without a merits determination.

Limits and questions left open: a nominally prospective request remains channeled if its necessary legal premise would invalidate the outstanding discipline.

##### Dismissal rather than exhaustion stay

Holding: a claim unavailable under *Heck* must be dismissed without prejudice rather than stayed merely to require exhaustion of state remedies.

Reasoning: § 1983 contains no freestanding judicial exhaustion requirement. A claim either is immediately cognizable or cannot yet proceed because the outstanding judgment has not been invalidated. A federal court may not transform the latter into a pending action through an unauthorized exhaustion stay.

Limits and questions left open: a stay may be available for a different independently lawful reason.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the claim-specific necessary-invalidity inquiry, the preservation of genuinely separable prospective relief, and the dismissal requirement.

#### Material Separate Writings

Ginsburg, joined by Souter and Breyer, emphasizes that Balisok also alleged failure to provide a statement identifying the evidence and reasons supporting guilt. A claim based solely on that defect might not necessarily invalidate the credit deprivation and therefore may be immediately cognizable. The concurrence reinforces the Court’s claim-by-claim rule.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Heck v. Humphrey* is extended from criminal convictions and sentences to prison disciplinary judgments revoking good-time credits when the challenged judgment affects custody duration.

Simulated *Sandin v. Conner* remains unchanged. *Sandin* governs whether prison law and the deprivation create a protected liberty interest; *Edwards* governs the procedural channel for a claim that would invalidate an outstanding credit judgment.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, controlling rule, remand treatment, and Ginsburg concurrence joined by Souter and Breyer match the historical decision. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520641/usrep520641.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the disciplinary allegations, good-time loss, requested relief, *Heck* analysis, disposition, and separate concurrence. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep520/usrep520641/usrep520641.pdf))
