### United States v. Thomas, 74 F.3d 701

Argued January 15, 1997 | Decided May 19, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date

From: the Sixth Circuit’s affirmance of obscenity convictions arising from the Thomases’ operation of a password-controlled California bulletin board. Paid members submitted addresses and telephone numbers, were screened, and received individual passwords. A Memphis postal inspector applied, spoke with the operators, was admitted as a member, and downloaded charged GIF files in Tennessee. The operators knew the subscriber’s location and retained control over whether to authorize access. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/74/701/594242/))

Questions presented: whether 18 U.S.C. § 1465 reaches obscene image files transmitted electronically, whether venue lies in the known receiving district, and whether that receiving community’s standards govern a targeted controlled distribution.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Miller v. California* and surviving obscenity doctrine permit regulation of obscenity under the standards of the community in which material is knowingly distributed, subject to the nationally framed reasonable-person inquiry concerning serious value. Simulated *United States v. X-Citement Video* requires knowledge of the operative facts distinguishing lawful from criminal conduct.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The convictions remain in force on the questions within the grant. The Court does not decide separate child-pornography counts, evidentiary issues, sentencing issues, or any matter concerning an open or anonymous network.

Justice Ginsburg delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Section 1465 and electronic image files

Holding: § 1465 reaches obscene image files knowingly transported for sale or distribution through an interstate computer-and-telephone system.

Operative rule: a generally worded statute covering interstate transport or distribution of obscene pictures, images, and electrical transcriptions applies to a later-developed transmission method when the conduct falls within the enacted terms. The court applies the statute to the new mechanism; it does not add an offense by analogy.

Reasoning: the statute is not confined to paper, film reels, or another closed list of physical containers. GIF files contain pictures and images, and the operators used interstate computer and telephone facilities to deliver those images to paying members. The statutory terms gave adequate notice that the interstate commercial transmission was covered.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide a statute whose text is confined to a specifically enumerated physical medium or authorize conviction without the required knowledge and obscenity findings.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. § 1465.

##### Venue and receiving-community standards

Holding: when an operator screens a subscriber, knows the subscriber’s district, approves access, and knowingly transmits obscene files there, venue is proper in the receiving district and that receiving community supplies *Miller*’s geographic community standards.

Reasoning: the distribution was a controlled interstate transaction rather than passive publication to an unknown audience. Memphis was the place where the files were received and the charged distribution was completed. Because the operators knew the destination and could refuse or restrict access, applying the receiving community’s standards did not impose liability based on an accidental or unknowable geographic contact.

The serious-literary, artistic, political, or scientific-value inquiry remains governed by the nationally framed reasonable-person component of *Miller*. The receiving community does not control that distinct question.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not determine the governing standard for an open website, anonymous access, unavoidable routing, or a publisher unable to identify or control the recipient’s location. It establishes no national cyberspace obscenity standard.

Legal basis: First Amendment, Article III venue provisions, Sixth Amendment, and federal venue statutes.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm on the questions within the grant.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the enacted-text application to electronic images, the receiving-district venue holding, and the narrow reliance on the operators’ actual knowledge and control over the Memphis distribution.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule applying § 1465 to interstate electronic transmission of obscene image files and the first rule identifying venue and community standards for a controlled, targeted digital distribution.

*Miller v. California* and *Hamling v. United States* are applied without alteration. Simulated *X-Citement Video* remains the governing scienter rule.

Same-term *Pic-A-State Pennsylvania, Inc. v. Reno* is consistent with the result because both cases treat an interstate computer-assisted commercial transmission as the operative interstate transaction. *Thomas*, however, rests independently on § 1465’s enacted terms and the completed delivery to a known district.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulation unanimously affirms the Sixth Circuit’s treatment of the controlled-access bulletin board, targeted Memphis distribution, venue, and receiving-community standards, while expressly reserving open and anonymous Internet publication. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/74/701/594242/))

Research sources: the Sixth Circuit opinion for the membership system, known destination, electronic downloads, statutory construction, venue, community standards, judgment, and reserved technological setting. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/74/701/594242/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Church on the Rock v. City of Albuquerque, 84 F.3d 1273

Argued February 18, 1997 | Decided May 27, 1997

From: the Tenth Circuit’s reversal of summary judgment for Albuquerque and entry of an injunction preventing the City from excluding the privately sponsored film *Jesus* and voluntary distribution of giant-print New Testaments from its senior centers. The centers hosted broad privately organized programming, including nondevotional presentations about the Bible, religion, literature, history, and philosophy. Attendance was voluntary, and users did not reside at the centers. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/84/1273/568760/))

Question presented: whether the City may exclude an otherwise eligible private presentation because its treatment of a permitted subject is devotional, affirmatively religious, or evangelistic.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Good News/Good Sports Club v. School District of Ladue* prohibits exclusion of a religious viewpoint from a government access program opened to the underlying subject. *Capitol Square v. Pinette*, *Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller*, and *Rosenberger* distinguish neutral access for private religious expression from governmental authorship or financing. Same-term *Brown v. Polk County* and *Robinson v. City of Edmond* further distinguish privately attributed expression from religion selected or presented as the government’s own.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The injunction and appellate fee award remain in force. The district court shall determine reasonable fee amounts and permit Albuquerque to administer neutral scheduling, capacity, safety, sponsorship-identification, and conduct rules.

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Religious viewpoint exclusion from the senior-center program

Holding: Albuquerque violated the First Amendment by excluding the privately sponsored film and New Testaments because they presented an affirmative religious viewpoint on subjects the senior-center access program otherwise permitted.

Reasoning: the City allowed private speakers to discuss the Bible, religion, history, literature, morality, and philosophy, but treated affirmation of religious belief as the forbidden perspective. That line turns on viewpoint rather than the subject admitted to the forum. Equal access did not make the film or literature the City’s message. Church on the Rock selected and supplied the material, adults voluntarily chose whether to attend, participants remained free to leave, and no public funds produced the religious content.

The Older Americans Act funding condition does not supply authority for the as-applied exclusion. Albuquerque may administer that condition to prevent governmental or center-sponsored sectarian instruction and worship. It may not treat neutral private access as governmental sponsorship and thereby suppress one viewpoint within an otherwise open subject.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not require the City to sponsor worship, finance devotional materials, allow disruption, relinquish control of facility hours, or admit expression outside the defined access program. Albuquerque may adopt a genuinely subject-neutral rule excluding all comparable private instruction or enforce neutral rules against obstruction, coercion, or interference with other users.

Legal basis: Free Speech Clause, informed by the Establishment Clause’s attribution boundary.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s application of the religious-viewpoint rule, its private-sponsorship analysis, and its conclusion that voluntary neutral access creates no governmental worship.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Good News/Good Sports Club* is applied beyond the school-access setting to a voluntary adult program. *Pinette*, *Chabad-Lubavitch*, and *Rosenberger* are applied without alteration. Same-term *Brown v. Polk County* and *Robinson v. City of Edmond* are clarified by contrast: privately sponsored religious expression remains private, while a symbol or message selected and used by government as its own remains governmental.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulation unanimously affirms the historical Tenth Circuit’s central holding that Albuquerque engaged in religious-viewpoint discrimination and that neither Establishment Clause concerns nor the federal funding condition justified the exclusion. The simulation adds an express saving construction preserving neutral administration of the funding condition. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/84/1273/568760/))

Research sources: the reported Tenth Circuit opinion for the program, speakers, requested film and literature, City policy, federal funding condition, judgment, and panel. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/84/1273/568760/))

### Clinton v. Jones, 520 U.S. 681 (1997)

Argued January 13, 1997 | Decided May 27, 1997

From: the Eighth Circuit’s affirmance of the denial of President Clinton’s asserted temporary immunity and its reversal of a categorical postponement of trial until the end of his Presidency. Jones’s action concerned alleged unofficial conduct occurring while Clinton was Governor of Arkansas, before he assumed the Presidency. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/681/))

Questions presented: whether a sitting President possesses temporary constitutional immunity from federal civil litigation based on unofficial pre-office conduct, and whether separation of powers requires a term-long stay.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Nixon v. Fitzgerald* protects official acts within the outer perimeter of presidential responsibility. *United States v. Nixon* and *Youngstown* preserve Article III authority to adjudicate legal disputes affecting the President. No controlling simulated decision extends official-function immunity to private conduct.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The case may proceed under ordinary federal procedural law. The district court shall accommodate concrete Article II responsibilities through scheduling, sequencing, protective orders, and other proportionate measures when warranted.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Breyer | None | Supports affirmance | Emphasizes the court’s duty to respond to a concrete showing of material impairment of presidential functions |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Temporary immunity for unofficial conduct

Holding: a sitting President has no constitutional temporary immunity from a federal civil action based on unofficial conduct alleged to have occurred before taking office.

Reasoning: presidential immunity protects the independence of official decisionmaking and is defined by the function performed, not by the officeholder’s status alone. The alleged conduct was neither presidential nor undertaken through governmental authority. Adjudicating it does not review, limit, or transfer an Article II power. Generalized concern about distraction cannot create a constitutional privilege unsupported by text, history, or the functional basis of official-act immunity.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide a comparable action in state court, criminal process against a sitting President, or the validity of a particular order requiring the President’s attendance at a specific time and place.

Legal basis: Articles II and III and separation of powers.

##### Categorical postponement and case-specific accommodation

Holding: separation of powers does not require a categorical stay through the end of the presidential term. Particular proceedings may be postponed or modified upon a concrete showing that they would materially interfere with presidential responsibilities.

Operative rule: the party seeking delay must identify the asserted official burden, and the court must select an accommodation proportionate to that burden. Ordinary case-management authority may not be converted into status-based immunity through automatic delay.

Reasoning: the term-long stay imposed risks of lost evidence and substantial delay without connecting those costs to an identified presidential duty. Scheduling, sequencing, written discovery, protective orders, and reconsideration of particular demands allow a federal court to respect the Presidency while preserving timely adjudication.

Limits and questions left open: no ruling is made on any particular deposition, subpoena, trial date, or attendance order.

Legal basis: Articles II and III, separation of powers, and the district court’s ordinary procedural authority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the functional boundary of presidential immunity and the distinction between categorical postponement and proportionate accommodation of a demonstrated Article II burden.

#### Material Separate Writings

Breyer concurs in the judgment and emphasizes that a district court must remain prepared to postpone or alter a particular proceeding if a concrete record shows that it would materially impair the President’s performance of a constitutional responsibility. That proposition is consistent with, but does not enlarge, the Court’s case-specific accommodation rule.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Nixon v. Fitzgerald* is declined extension beyond official presidential functions. The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for federal civil litigation against a sitting President based on unofficial pre-office conduct. Ordinary docket-management authority remains available but cannot operate as categorical temporary immunity.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, author, controlling rule, principal joins, and Breyer concurrence match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/681/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for the allegations, procedural history, questions reserved, judgment, opinion lineup, and Breyer’s separate position. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/681/))

---

### De Buono v. NYSA-ILA Medical and Clinical Services Fund, 520 U.S. 806 (1997)

Argued February 24, 1997 | Decided June 2, 1997

From: the Second Circuit’s reinstatement of an injunction against New York’s Health Facility Assessment as applied to treatment centers owned by an ERISA welfare plan. The assessment imposed a 0.6 percent charge on gross patient-service receipts of licensed facilities and entered New York’s general revenues; most facilities subject to it were not owned by ERISA plans. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/806/))

Question presented: whether ERISA preempts the generally applicable assessment because the Fund owns the taxed facilities and therefore remits the charge directly from plan assets.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *New York State Conference of Blue Cross & Blue Shield Plans v. Travelers* holds that ordinary economic influence on plan choices is not enough for preemption. State law remains preempted when it refers specifically to ERISA plans, binds benefit structure or administration, creates an alternative enforcement mechanism, or effectively dictates plan choices through coercive economic force.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The injunction shall be dissolved. The lower courts shall resolve any remaining refund, state-law, or remedial issue consistently with the conclusion that ERISA does not preempt this application of the assessment.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Opposes merits disposition | Would require briefing and resolution of the Tax Injunction Act issue before reaching ERISA preemption |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Generally applicable facility taxation

Holding: ERISA does not preempt New York’s Health Facility Assessment as applied to treatment centers owned and operated by the Fund.

Operative rule: a generally applicable state tax does not relate to an ERISA plan merely because the plan directly owns the taxed business or property and therefore pays the charge itself. Preemption turns on regulation of plan benefits, administration, enforcement, or choices with force equivalent to direct command, not on the accounting route by which an ordinary cost reaches the plan.

Reasoning: the assessment neither refers to ERISA plans nor conditions benefits, provider selection, plan procedure, fiduciary conduct, or remedies. Had the Fund purchased treatment from an independent facility, the same assessment could have reached the Fund indirectly through prices. Ownership changes the route of payment, not the tax’s legal character. Treating every direct plan expense as preempted would displace property taxes, utility charges, and other ordinary state law without evidence that Congress intended such a result.

Limits and questions left open: a tax that singles out ERISA plans, operates as an alternative enforcement device, or is structured to compel a particular benefit or provider arrangement may present a different case.

Legal basis: ERISA § 514(a), 29 U.S.C. § 1144(a).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s application of *Travelers* and its rejection of a direct-versus-indirect-incidence distinction for ordinary state taxation.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would not reach the merits without resolving whether the Tax Injunction Act deprived the federal courts of jurisdiction. The dissent would order briefing and argument on that threshold question. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/806/))

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Travelers* is applied and clarified. Its economic-influence rule does not turn on whether an ERISA plan purchases taxed services or owns the facility and pays the assessment directly. The existing boundaries concerning ERISA-specific reference, plan administration, alternative enforcement, and coercive dictation remain unchanged.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated merits vote, author, lineup, and controlling rule match the historical decision. The simulation adds a limited remand for any residual refund or state-law remedial issue rather than ending with a bare reversal. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/806/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for the assessment, the Fund’s ownership, the direct-versus-indirect analysis, judgment, opinion lineup, and jurisdictional dissent. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/806/))

---

### Bracy v. Gramley, 520 U.S. 899 (1997)

Argued April 14, 1997 | Decided June 9, 1997

From: the Seventh Circuit’s affirmance of the denial of federal habeas discovery. Bracy was tried and sentenced to death by Judge Thomas Maloney, who was later convicted of accepting bribes to fix criminal cases. Bracy alleged that Maloney treated unbribed defendants harshly to conceal the corrupt acquittals and identified several facts connecting that theory to his own trial. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/899/))

Question presented: whether the established corruption record and Bracy’s case-specific allegations established good cause under Habeas Corpus Rule 6 for focused discovery into actual judicial bias.

Controlling simulated law before decision: due process requires a neutral judge and prohibits adjudication by a judge with an actual disqualifying interest or bias. Habeas Corpus Rule 6 permits discovery for good cause. *United States v. Armstrong* confirms more generally that a discovery threshold must correspond to the elements of the underlying constitutional theory.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The district court shall authorize focused discovery reasonably directed to whether Maloney was actually biased in Bracy’s case. The Court makes no finding of bias and grants no new trial.

Chief Justice Stone delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Good cause for judicial-bias discovery

Holding: Bracy established good cause for focused discovery into whether Maloney’s corruption produced compensatory bias in Bracy’s capital trial.

Operative rule: Rule 6 does not require a habeas petitioner to prove the concealed constitutional fact before receiving discovery reasonably necessary to investigate it. The petitioner must identify a legally sufficient claim and specific facts providing reason to believe that properly developed evidence may establish entitlement to relief. The district court retains authority to confine discovery to that connection.

Reasoning: Maloney’s proved corruption does not itself establish bias in every unbribed case. Bracy, however, added case-specific allegations concerning counsel’s relationship with Maloney, unusually compressed preparation and penalty proceedings, and the placement of his trial among murder cases identified in the federal corruption materials as fixed. Requiring completed proof of Maloney’s hidden motive would make the discovery standard circular.

Application: discovery may address the asserted counsel relationship, scheduling, relevant corruption evidence, and other evidence reasonably bearing on Maloney’s treatment of Bracy’s case. The district court may sequence and limit depositions and document production.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not authorize discovery whenever a judge committed unrelated misconduct, presume bias from appearance, determine procedural default or prejudice, or decide whether Bracy ultimately merits habeas relief.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process Clause and Rule 6 of the Rules Governing Section 2254 Cases.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone states the Court’s claim-specific good-cause rule and directs focused discovery without finding actual bias or entitlement to a new trial.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The neutral-decisionmaker requirement remains unchanged. The decision creates the first controlling simulated application of Habeas Corpus Rule 6 to a concealed judicial-bias claim and clarifies that discovery good cause requires a concrete basis for investigation, not completed proof of the ultimate fact.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, controlling rule, and remand match the historical decision. Stone, occupying the Chief Justice position, authors the opinion delivered historically by Chief Justice Rehnquist. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/899/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for Maloney’s corruption, Bracy’s case-specific allegations, the Rule 6 holding, and the historical judgment; the Seventh Circuit opinion for the corrected lower-court decision date. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/899/))

---

### Gilbert v. Homar, 520 U.S. 924 (1997)

Argued March 24, 1997 | Decided June 9, 1997

From: the Third Circuit’s reversal of summary judgment for university officials. Homar, a tenured university police officer, was suspended without pay immediately after an independent felony arrest. The charges were dismissed six days later, but the unpaid suspension continued, and he received his first opportunity to respond twenty-three days after the suspension. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/924/))

Questions presented: whether due process required a pre-suspension opportunity to respond before both removal and nonpayment, and whether the later process was sufficiently prompt.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Mathews v. Eldridge* governs the timing and form of process. *Loudermill* ordinarily requires notice and an opportunity to respond at a useful time. Existing law permits immediate provisional action when genuine urgency and sufficiently reliable independent information reduce the value of predeprivation process.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1. Supporting: Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone.

The lower courts shall determine whether Homar received a sufficiently prompt and effective post-suspension opportunity to respond. Damages, qualified immunity, and the validity of the eventual demotion remain open.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone | None | Opposes reversal on the immediate nonpayment issue; agrees that immediate removal was permissible and that post-suspension promptness requires adjudication | Distinguishes removal from cessation of pay |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Immediate suspension without pay after an independent felony arrest

Holding: due process did not require a pre-suspension hearing before the University temporarily suspended Homar without pay following his arrest and formal felony charge.

Operative rule: when an independent arrest and charge establish reasonable grounds concerning the conduct bearing on a sensitive public position, the government may take immediate temporary action without first providing an opportunity to respond, provided constitutionally prompt corrective process follows.

Reasoning: the State had a strong interest in immediately removing a police officer facing a serious drug charge from a position of public trust. The independent arrest and charge substantially reduced the initial risk that the suspension rested on a baseless accusation and performed the principal screening function a short pre-suspension response opportunity would otherwise serve. The Constitution did not require the University to continue salary payments while that provisional decision was made.

Limits and questions left open: the holding does not authorize indefinite suspension, permit reliance on an arrest after the employer obtains contrary information, or decide a case lacking an independent objective basis for immediate action.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process Clause.

Authority of the proposition: Scalia’s opinion, joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies an eight-Justice majority.

##### Prompt post-suspension process

Holding: the employee must receive a prompt and meaningful opportunity to contest the continuing employment consequence after immediate action is taken.

Reasoning: the independent arrest supports provisional action, not an indefinite deprivation. The dismissal of the criminal charge and the University’s development of its own employment allegations materially affect the timing inquiry. The lower courts must determine what information was available, why earlier process was impracticable, and whether the later meetings could effectively correct the continuing deprivation.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not require a full evidentiary trial before provisional action or decide the validity of Homar’s later demotion.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process Clause.

Authority of the proposition: the Court opinion and Stone’s separate writing support remand for adjudication of post-suspension promptness. All nine Justices agree on that consequence.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm the Third Circuit’s reversal of summary judgment and remand.

Public role: concurs in part and dissents in part.

Announced ground: Stone agrees that the independent felony arrest justified immediate removal from sensitive police duties. He concludes, however, that stopping salary was a distinct property deprivation requiring a separately demonstrated urgency. Because no such justification was established as a matter of law, Stone would leave the Third Circuit’s summary-judgment disposition intact while remanding the post-suspension timing issue.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Mathews* and *Loudermill* are clarified by the eight-Justice majority. A sufficiently reliable independent arrest may permit immediate unpaid suspension from a sensitive public position without predeprivation process, but prompt corrective review remains mandatory. Stone’s proposed separation of removal and nonpayment is noncontrolling.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed and remanded unanimously in an opinion by Justice Scalia. Stone’s controlled distinction between removal and nonpayment produces an 8-1 simulated judgment while leaving the historical majority’s rule and authorship otherwise intact. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/924/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for the arrest, unpaid suspension, later proceedings, historical judgment, and opinion authorship. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/924/))

### Thomasson v. Perry, 80 F.3d 915

Argued February 19, 1997 | Decided June 9, 1997

From: the en banc Fourth Circuit’s affirmance of Lieutenant Paul Thomasson’s honorable discharge under 10 U.S.C. § 654 and its implementing directive. Thomasson had ten years of highly rated Navy service. His statement that he was gay triggered a presumption of prohibited conduct, propensity, or intent, but the Navy identified no past prohibited act, performance failure, security breach, or operational impairment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/80/915/627632/))

Questions presented: how the statutory statements provision operates, whether the statement-triggered presumption classifies by sexual orientation, and whether the discharge satisfies equal protection under the same-term standard established in *Walmer v. Department of Defense*.

Controlling simulated law before decision: same-term *Walmer* holds that a military rule mandating separation specifically for homosexual acts classifies by sexual orientation and receives intermediate scrutiny. The Government must establish an actual important military objective and a substantial, nonstereotyped relationship between the classification and that objective. Supported professional military judgments receive substantial evidentiary weight, but they do not displace the standard or shift the constitutional burden.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

Thomasson’s discharge based on his identity statement and the resulting unsupported presumption shall be set aside. The lower court shall order appropriate reinstatement and correction of his military records, subject to any independent lawful ground not adjudicated here.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Breyer in full; O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas as to construction of the statements provision | Supports reversal and remand | Five-Justice equal-protection holding; unanimous statutory construction |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Kennedy | O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas | Would affirm the discharge | Accepts the rebuttable-presumption construction but concludes that the statutory process and military record satisfy constitutional review |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Construction of the statements provision

Holding: § 654’s statements provision creates a rebuttable presumption concerning conduct, propensity, or intent. It does not establish automatic discharge for homosexual identity alone.

Operative rule: the member may present all evidence relevant to actual conduct, propensity, and intent, including the statement’s context, credibility, past conduct, testimony from others, service history insofar as probative, and a commitment to obey valid conduct rules. The member need not retract or deny homosexual identity to invoke the rebuttal process.

Reasoning: the statute and directive expressly identify a presumption rather than direct status-based separation. Treating denial of identity as the only effective rebuttal would make the presumption irrebuttable in practice and would disregard the categories of contrary evidence the directive itself makes relevant.

Limits and questions left open: a statement actually admitting prohibited conduct or intent may be used according to its content. The Court does not eliminate the statutory presumption in every application.

Legal basis: 10 U.S.C. § 654 and DoD Directive 1332.30.

Authority of the proposition: Ginsburg’s opinion and Kennedy’s separate writing support the same construction. The proposition is unanimous.

##### Equal protection and the statement-triggered discharge

Holding: § 654(b)(2)’s use of a homosexual-identity statement as the trigger for presumed conduct, propensity, or intent classifies by sexual orientation and receives intermediate scrutiny. As applied to Thomasson without independent evidence of prohibited conduct, propensity, or intent, the discharge is not shown substantially related to the military’s important readiness and discipline interests.

Reasoning: unit cohesion, discipline, privacy, readiness, and operational effectiveness are important military objectives, and supported contemporaneous professional judgments receive substantial weight. The Government nevertheless must establish the required relationship rather than rely on a self-validating inference from identity to conduct. Thomasson’s service record was exemplary, and the Navy identified no operational impairment or independent conduct evidence. On this record, the identity-based presumption supplied the sole decisive bridge to discharge.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not invalidate neutral rules against specified sexual conduct, fraternization, coercion, security violations, or operational misconduct. It does not decide due process, free speech, or substantive liberty.

Legal basis: Fifth Amendment equal protection.

Authority of the proposition: Ginsburg’s opinion, joined on this issue by Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Breyer, supplies a five-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand for as-applied relief.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the genuine rebuttal construction and the application of *Walmer* to a discharge in which the identity statement and resulting presumption, rather than individualized evidence, performed the decisive constitutional work.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas, accepts the Court’s construction of the statutory presumption but would affirm. The dissent concludes that the member carried the regulatory burden of rebuttal, that the military could rely on the unrebutted inference together with Congress’s institutional findings, and that *Walmer* should not be extended to invalidate this application.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Same-term *Walmer v. Department of Defense* is applied and extended from a rule directed to homosexual acts to a statement-triggered presumption that uses homosexual identity as evidence of predicted conduct. Military judgment remains weighty evidence within intermediate scrutiny, but an identity-to-conduct inference unsupported by individualized evidence does not itself establish substantial fit.

The decision also creates the first controlling simulated construction of § 654’s statements provision.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The en banc Fourth Circuit upheld the statute, directive, and discharge under deferential review. The simulation reverses because same-term *Walmer* displaced that rational-basis premise before Thomasson’s decision and required a nonstereotyped, evidence-supported fit that the present record does not establish. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/80/915/627632/))

Research sources: the en banc Fourth Circuit opinion for the statutory and regulatory language, Thomasson’s statement, service record, rebuttal evidence, Board proceedings, and historical disposition. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/80/915/627632/))

### Associates Commercial Corp. v. Rash, 520 U.S. 953 (1997)

Argued April 16, 1997 | Decided June 16, 1997

From: the en banc Fifth Circuit’s affirmance of a Chapter 13 plan valuing a retained Kenworth tractor truck by estimated net foreclosure proceeds. The debtors proposed to keep and use the truck in their freight-hauling business rather than surrender it to the secured creditor. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/953/))

Question presented: whether § 506(a) values retained collateral by replacement value for the debtor’s proposed use or by the creditor’s hypothetical net foreclosure recovery.

Controlling simulated law before decision: no prior simulated holding construes § 506(a). The enacted text directs valuation in light of the valuation’s purpose and the proposed disposition or use of the collateral.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

The bankruptcy court shall value the truck under the replacement-value standard and reconsider confirmation and the allowed secured claim.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer in full; Scalia except footnote 4 | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion at the stated joins |
| Dissent | Stevens | None | Would affirm | Would value the secured claim by the creditor’s net foreclosure recovery |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Valuation of retained collateral

Holding: when a Chapter 13 debtor retains and uses collateral under the cram-down option, § 506(a) values the secured claim by the cost of obtaining like property for the same proposed use, adjusted for the collateral’s age and condition, rather than by net foreclosure proceeds.

Operative rule: replacement value is not invariably retail value and does not mean the cost of a new item. The valuation must exclude warranties, services, accessories, or other components a comparable replacement would provide but the retained collateral does not.

Reasoning: § 506(a) makes proposed disposition or use legally relevant. The plan’s chosen disposition is retention and continued business use, not foreclosure. A foreclosure measure would price a transaction the plan prevents and transfer the entire use-value advantage to the debtors. Replacement value preserves the statutory distinction between surrender and retention while leaving the relevant market and adjustments to the bankruptcy court.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not prescribe one pricing source for every asset or decide valuation when the debtor surrenders, abandons, or proposes a materially different use.

Legal basis: 11 U.S.C. §§ 506(a) and 1325(a)(5)(B).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the statute’s proposed-use command and the requirement that replacement value reflect comparable property rather than a new or enhanced asset.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule for valuation of collateral retained through a Chapter 13 cram down. *BFP v. Resolution Trust Corp.* remains confined to completed foreclosure and does not supply the measure for prospective retained use under § 506(a).

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, 8-1 vote, author, partial Scalia join, dissent, and controlling valuation rule match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/953/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for the competing valuations, retained-use posture, judgment, opinion lineup, and replacement-value rule. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/953/))

### Hughes Aircraft Co. v. United States ex rel. Schumer, 520 U.S. 939 (1997)

Argued February 25, 1997 | Decided June 16, 1997

From: the Ninth Circuit’s application of the 1986 False Claims Act amendments to alleged false claims submitted from 1982 through 1984. Before the amendment, a relator action based on information already in the Government’s possession was barred; the amendment narrowed that bar and allowed Schumer’s later-filed action to proceed. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/939/))

Question presented: whether the amendment may be applied to completed pre-enactment conduct when doing so revives a private action barred by the prior government-possession rule.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Landgraf v. USI Film Products* requires a clear congressional temporal command or, absent one, asks whether application would attach new legal consequences to completed conduct. *Rivers v. Roadway Express* applies the same sequence to expanded civil coverage.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded with instructions to dismiss, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Ninth Circuit shall direct dismissal of Schumer’s qui tam action under the pre-1986 government-possession bar.

Justice Thomas delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by every participating Justice.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Retroactive application of the 1986 qui tam amendment

Holding: the 1986 qui tam amendment does not govern alleged false claims submitted before enactment when applying it would revive a relator action barred by the prior government-possession rule.

Reasoning: the amendment does more than assign a tribunal or regulate the secondary conduct of litigation. It removes a complete defense, permits a private relator with a financial interest to maintain an action that could not previously be brought, and exposes completed transactions to treble-damages litigation initiated through a newly available enforcement route. Congress did not clearly prescribe that retroactive operation.

Calling the former bar jurisdictional does not change the analysis. The amendment determines whether this plaintiff may maintain the action at all, not merely which court hears an otherwise existing claim.

Application: the Government possessed the material information within the former bar before Schumer filed. Pre-amendment law therefore controls and requires dismissal.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not govern post-1986 conduct, an action filed by the Government, the amended public-disclosure rule in a prospective case, or a purely forum-allocating jurisdictional amendment that leaves substantive exposure unchanged.

Legal basis: False Claims Act and the retroactivity rule of *Landgraf*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand with instructions to dismiss.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s treatment of the newly available relator action and eliminated complete bar as new legal consequences attached to completed conduct.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Landgraf* and *Rivers* are applied and clarified. A provision’s jurisdictional label does not remove it from retroactivity analysis when it changes whether a private action exists rather than merely reallocating an existing action among tribunals.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated unanimity, author, controlling rule, and dismissal consequence match the historical decision. The historical formal judgment was vacatur and remand; the simulation uses Stone’s controlled reversal-and-remand formulation while directing the same dismissal under pre-1986 law. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/939/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for the pre-1986 bar, alleged conduct dates, statutory change, judgment, authorship, and retroactivity holding. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/520/939/))

### Abrams v. Johnson, 521 U.S. 74; United States v. Johnson, No. 95-1460

Argued December 9, 1996 | Decided June 19, 1997

From: direct appeals from a three-judge District Court’s remedial congressional plan following simulated *Miller v. Johnson*. After finding the prior Eleventh and Second Districts race predominant and unsupported, and after the Georgia legislature failed to enact a replacement, the court adopted an eleven-district plan containing one majority-Black district. It emphasized county lines, district cores, communities of interest, compactness, and population equality while considering race without making it predominant. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/521/74/))

Questions presented: whether the court exceeded its remedial authority by failing to preserve additional majority-Black districts, whether the plan violated Voting Rights Act §§ 2 or 5, and whether its slight population deviation violated Article I, § 2.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Miller* defines racial predominance and establishes two scrutiny lanes only after predominance is found. *Holder v. Hall* and simulated *Johnson v. De Grandy* govern § 2 comparator, totality, and nonmaximization. Same-term *Lopez v. Monterey County*, *Young v. Fordice*, and *Bossier Parish* govern judicial plans, lawful baselines, retrogression, and the separation of §§ 2 and 5.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Abrams v. Johnson*, No. 95-1425 | Affirmed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The District Court’s remedial plan remains in force. |
| *United States v. Johnson*, No. 95-1460 | Affirmed, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | The District Court’s remedial plan remains in force. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas | Supports affirmance in both appeals | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg | Would reverse and require reconsideration of a lawful two-majority-Black-district alternative | Rejects the Court’s remedial, § 2, and population-equality conclusions |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Remedial authority and racial predominance

Holding: the District Court acted within its remedial authority in adopting a statewide plan after multiple districts required correction and the legislature failed to enact a lawful replacement. It was not required to retain prior race-driven features that would reproduce the constitutional violation.

Operative rule: a federal redistricting court should respect lawful and workable state districting policies, but it may not preserve a policy inseparable from the violation being remedied. Simulated *Miller*’s scrutiny lanes are activated only if race predominates in the remedial plan.

Reasoning: the invalid districts affected separated parts of the statewide map, making broader adjustment unavoidable. The court returned first to lawful traditional criteria, including county integrity, district cores, communities of interest, compactness, and population. Its awareness of racial consequences did not materially subordinate those criteria. Because race did not predominate in the court plan, neither of *Miller*’s postpredominance scrutiny lanes is activated.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not authorize a court to disregard a lawful legislative policy unrelated to the violation or to use a remedial label to shield race-predominant line drawing.

Legal basis: Equal Protection Clause and federal equitable authority.

##### Voting Rights Act § 2

Holding: the record does not establish that § 2 required creation of a second majority-Black district.

Reasoning: the appellants did not establish a sufficiently compact additional community capable of forming the required district under lawful traditional criteria, and the bloc-voting findings did not show that white voting ordinarily defeated minority-preferred candidates in the manner necessary for the asserted remedy. Statewide electoral figures and the ability to imagine a more race-conscious district do not establish vote dilution. Section 2 does not require maximization of majority-minority districts.

Limits and questions left open: statewide electoral success or rough proportionality is not a safe harbor. A compact, cohesive community facing legally sufficient bloc voting could require a different result.

Legal basis: Voting Rights Act § 2.

##### Voting Rights Act § 5

Holding: the wholly court-developed plan need not itself be submitted for preclearance, and it does not produce prohibited retrogression when compared with Georgia’s last lawful operative benchmark, the 1982 plan.

Reasoning: the unprecleared 1991 proposal never became legally operative, and an unconstitutional racial feature of the 1992 plan cannot be frozen into the baseline. The 1982 plan contained one majority-Black district. The court plan preserves a genuine opportunity district and does not reduce protected voters’ electoral opportunity from that lawful benchmark. Nor does the record establish a racially discriminatory purpose in the court’s remedial design.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not define the full boundary of § 5’s purpose prong reserved in same-term *Bossier Parish*. A judicial plan that reduced actual opportunity from the lawful baseline or embodied prohibited purpose would not receive protection from its remedial source.

Legal basis: Voting Rights Act § 5 and federal remedial authority.

##### Population equality

Holding: the slight population deviation in the court plan does not violate Article I, § 2 because it is supported by consistently applied legitimate state and remedial criteria and is lower than the deviation in any otherwise lawful alternative presented.

Reasoning: population equality remains the paramount objective. The residual deviation resulted from the court’s consistent effort to preserve county and precinct boundaries, district cores, and communities of interest while repairing multiple invalid districts. The challengers identified no materially more equal alternative that avoided independent constitutional defects.

Limits and questions left open: the decision creates no numerical safe harbor and does not excuse a deviation adopted for convenience, favoritism, or an unexplained policy.

Legal basis: Article I, § 2.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm in both appeals.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the conclusion that the court plan returned to lawful districting criteria without racial predominance, did not violate §§ 2 or 5, and justified its small population deviation through consistently applied state and remedial considerations.

#### Material Separate Writings

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg, would require the District Court to reconsider a two-majority-Black-district alternative. The dissent concludes that Georgia had manifested a lawful policy favoring two such districts and that the court departed too far from that policy when a constitutionally permissible two-district plan remained possible. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/521/74/))

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Miller v. Johnson* is applied without alteration. Racial awareness does not establish predominance, and the two scrutiny lanes are not activated when traditional criteria remain controlling.

Same-term *Lopez v. Monterey County* is applied to a wholly court-formulated necessary remedy. *Young v. Fordice* and *Bossier Parish* are applied to the lawful baseline and retrogression inquiry. Simulated *De Grandy* remains the governing nonmaximization rule under § 2.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, 5-4 vote, author, principal lineup, and result match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the position held by the historical Chief Justice. The simulation reaches that result through the altered *Miller* predominance and scrutiny framework and the completed same-term Voting Rights Act decisions. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/521/74/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for the remedial history, issues, § 5 benchmark analysis, population-equality treatment, judgment, and separate dissent. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/521/74/))

### Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp., 521 U.S. 179 (1997)

Argued April 21, 1997 | Decided June 19, 1997

From: the Eighth Circuit’s affirmance of dismissal of the Klehrs’ civil RICO action as untimely. The Klehrs purchased a Harvestore silo in 1974, alleged injury beginning in the 1970s, filed suit in August 1993, and relied on continuing predicate acts and alleged concealment to avoid the four-year limitations bar. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/521/179/))

Questions presented: whether a last-predicate-act rule permits recovery for old injuries whenever a later predicate occurs, what remains open concerning the universal civil RICO accrual trigger, and whether fraudulent-concealment tolling requires reasonable diligence.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Agency Holding Corp. v. Malley-Duff* supplies the four-year limitations period. *NOW v. Scheidler* preserves RICO’s enacted injury and causation requirements. No controlling simulated decision selects among injury, injury-discovery, and injury-and-pattern-discovery as the universal accrual rule.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1. Supporting: Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg in full; Stone as to the no-revival and reasonable-diligence holdings; Scalia and Thomas as to the reasonable-diligence holding | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for six Justices; partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Would adopt the Clayton Act injury-accrual rule rather than leave the universal trigger unresolved |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone | None | Opposes affirmance | Joins the no-revival and diligence rules but would remand for injury-specific application |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Separate accrual and no revival of stale injuries

Holding: a later predicate act does not revive damages for an older time-barred injury. A later act causing a genuinely new and independent injury may support a separately accruing claim limited to that injury and its resulting damages.

Operative rule: each asserted injury must be connected to the act causing it. A timely new injury does not reset the limitations period for completed older harm merely because both acts form part of one alleged pattern.

Reasoning: a revival rule would permit the limitations period to continue indefinitely whenever a pattern remained active and would defeat the repose supplied by the borrowed four-year period. RICO’s pattern requirement defines prohibited conduct; it does not convert every later predicate into a new accrual date for every earlier injury.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not choose among injury, injury-discovery, and injury-and-pattern-discovery as the universal civil RICO accrual trigger.

Authority of the proposition: Breyer’s opinion is joined on the no-revival rule by Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Stone. Scalia and Thomas independently support the same no-revival proposition through their broader injury-accrual rule. All nine Justices reject revival, while seven Justices support leaving the universal trigger unresolved.

##### Fraudulent concealment

Holding: a civil RICO plaintiff relying on fraudulent concealment must establish reasonable diligence in attempting to discover the cause of action.

Operative rule: concealment by the defendant does not toll the limitations period when reasonable investigation would have disclosed the claim. The claimant bears the obligation to establish both concealment and diligence under the governing equitable doctrine.

Reasoning: private civil RICO actions serve compensatory and enforcement functions analogous to the Clayton Act. Tolling therefore cannot depend solely on the defendant’s behavior while disregarding whether the claimant reasonably pursued available indications of injury.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not prescribe a universal form of investigation or decide an injury whose concealment could not have been overcome through reasonable diligence.

Authority of the proposition: every participating Justice supports the reasonable-diligence requirement.

##### Application and judgment

Holding: the Eighth Circuit’s judgment is affirmed because the last-predicate-act rule is unavailable, and the Klehrs’ fact-specific challenge to the lower court’s diligence determination falls outside the scope of the writ.

Reasoning: under the most plaintiff-favorable remaining theory considered below, the Klehrs should have discovered the existence and source of the asserted injury before the four-year period preceding suit. Their request that the Supreme Court reweigh the evidentiary record concerning diligence presents a question not included in the grant.

Authority of the proposition: Breyer’s full opinion and the Scalia-Thomas concurrence support affirmance, producing an eight-Justice judgment coalition. Stone dissents from this application.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: vacate and remand.

Public role: concurs in part and dissents in part.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s no-revival rule, its preservation of separate accrual for a genuinely new injury, and its reasonable-diligence requirement. He concludes that the record does not adequately identify each alleged injury, the act causing it, discoverability, or the corresponding tolling evidence. Stone would remand for injury-by-injury application without selecting a universal accrual trigger.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would adopt the Clayton Act injury-accrual rule immediately. Under that rule, the limitations period begins when the plaintiff suffers the RICO injury, without waiting for discovery of a pattern. The broader proposition is noncontrolling. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/521/179/))

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated civil RICO rules governing separate accrual, nonrevival of stale injuries, and reasonable diligence for fraudulent-concealment tolling. *Agency Holding* continues to supply the four-year period, and *NOW v. Scheidler* continues to require statutory injury and causation. The universal accrual trigger remains unresolved.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed unanimously. Stone’s controlled conclusion that the record requires injury-specific remand produces an 8-1 simulated judgment. The historical author, the principal opinion structure, the no-revival rule, the diligence requirement, and the Scalia-Thomas preference for immediate adoption of the Clayton Act injury rule otherwise remain intact. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/521/179/))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports text for the alleged injuries, competing accrual rules, no-revival holding, diligence requirement, judgment, and separate concurrence.

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Metropolitan Stevedore Co. v. Rambo, 521 U.S. 121 (1997)

Argued March 17, 1997 | Decided June 19, 1997

From: the Ninth Circuit’s reversal of an order discontinuing Rambo’s disability compensation. Rambo’s permanent work injury remained, but his earnings as a crane operator had risen to approximately three times his preinjury wages. The court of appeals directed entry of a nominal award to preserve the possibility of later modification if the injury impaired his future earning capacity. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521121/usrep521121.pdf))

Questions presented: whether the LHWCA authorizes nominal compensation when no present wage loss exists but the injury creates a significant potential for future loss, and whether an appellate court may direct the award without the administrative law judge making the necessary findings.

Controlling simulated law before decision: the prior decision in this compensation proceeding treats statutory disability as economic loss of wage-earning capacity and permits modification when that capacity changes, but it does not resolve nominal compensation or the allocation of proof concerning future risk.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas.

The Ninth Circuit shall remand through the Benefits Review Board to the administrative law judge. A nominal award may be entered only after the administrative law judge finds that Rambo proved the required significant future potential.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia and Thomas | Would reverse and sustain termination without a nominal award | Rejects nominal compensation in the absence of present economic disability |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Nominal compensation and future earning capacity

Holding: the LHWCA permits a nominal compensation award when a permanent work injury causes no present loss of wage-earning capacity but the claimant proves a significant, nonconjectural potential that the injury will reduce future capacity.

Operative rule: the employer seeking termination of actual benefits first bears the burden of proving the change in conditions supporting termination, including that current earnings fairly represent present earning capacity. Once that showing is made, the claimant seeking nominal compensation bears persuasion under 5 U.S.C. § 556(d) and must prove by a preponderance that the injury creates a significant potential for later economic loss. Permanent impairment alone, an abstract possibility of unemployment, or administrative convenience is insufficient.

Reasoning: § 8(c)(21) compensates economic disability rather than physical impairment standing alone, but § 8(h) expressly directs attention to the future effects of disability. An outright denial whenever current earnings equal or exceed preinjury wages could allow § 22’s modification period to expire before a foreseeable injury-related loss materializes. A nominal award reconciles those provisions without paying present compensation for a loss that has not yet occurred.

Application: Metropolitan established grounds to terminate Rambo’s actual benefits because his current wages showed no present economic loss. That evidence did not answer whether his permanent injury, together with reasonably foreseeable labor-market conditions, created a significant potential for future loss.

Limits and questions left open: nominal compensation is not available for every permanent injury. The decision neither presumes future unemployment nor authorizes an appellate tribunal to determine probability or magnitude in the first instance.

Legal basis: 33 U.S.C. §§ 902(10), 908(c)(21), 908(h), and 922; 5 U.S.C. § 556(d).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: vacate and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s statutory reconciliation, burden allocation, and insistence that the administrative law judge make the future-risk finding.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The prior simulated *Rambo* decision is clarified. Present disability remains an economic concept, but a proved significant potential for future injury-related wage loss may support nominal compensation preserving the statutory modification route.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, 6-3 vote, author, and opinion lineup match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the supporting position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521121/usrep521121.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the compensation record, statutory analysis, burden allocation, judgment, and historical lineup. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521121/usrep521121.pdf))

---

### O’Dell v. Netherland, 521 U.S. 151 (1997)

Argued March 18, 1997 | Decided June 19, 1997

From: the en banc Fourth Circuit’s reversal of federal habeas relief. At O’Dell’s capital sentencing, Virginia argued future dangerousness, but the court refused an accurate instruction that a life sentence made O’Dell legally ineligible for parole. His sentence became final in 1988, six years before *Simmons v. South Carolina*. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521151/usrep521151.pdf))

Question presented: whether *Simmons* announced a new rule under *Teague v. Lane* and, if so, whether that rule applies retroactively to O’Dell’s final capital sentence.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Simmons* requires an accurate parole-ineligibility instruction when the prosecution places future dangerousness at issue and the sole noncapital sentence legally forecloses parole. *Allridge v. Scott* declines to extend that rule when parole remains legally possible. No simulated decision alters *Teague*’s new-rule sequence or recognized exceptions.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

##### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The Fourth Circuit shall reinstate the District Court’s conditional penalty-phase relief. O’Dell’s conviction remains undisturbed. Virginia may conduct a new sentencing proceeding complying with *Simmons* or impose the lawful noncapital sentence.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment and Opinion of the Court by issue | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas as to the new-rule classification; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer as to the watershed exception and remedy | Supports reversal and remand | Different five-Justice coalitions as stated |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Would classify *Simmons* as dictated by prior law, but joins the alternative watershed holding and remedy |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia, and Kennedy | Would affirm | Joins the conclusion that *Simmons* was new but rejects retroactivity under either exception |

#### Decision of the Court

##### New-rule classification

Holding: *Simmons* announced a new procedural rule for *Teague* purposes.

Reasoning: the earlier due-process decisions established a right to answer materially misleading sentencing premises but did not dictate the particular parole-ineligibility instruction later required by *Simmons*. The disagreement among reasonable jurists and the division within *Simmons* itself confirm that its precise result was not compelled when O’Dell’s sentence became final.

Limits and questions left open: the classification does not narrow *Simmons* on direct review or alter the distinction between legal parole ineligibility and predictions about practical release.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion, joined on this issue by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, supplies a five-Justice majority.

##### The watershed exception in the precise Simmons setting

Holding: when the State seeks death through future dangerousness, the only lawful alternative is life without parole, and the court refuses an accurate instruction stating that fixed legal fact, the *Simmons* safeguard falls within *Teague*’s watershed exception.

Operative rule: the exception is confined to the conjunction of a future-dangerousness prosecution theory, legal impossibility of parole under the only noncapital sentence, and refusal of an accurate instruction necessary to correct the jury’s false release premise.

Reasoning: the sentencing jury’s sole task was to choose between death and a life sentence that legally excluded parole. The State invited death based on future danger while the court prevented O’Dell from correcting the premise that he might return to society. In that narrow setting, the omitted safeguard directly compromises the reliability and fundamental fairness of the choice between the only lawful penalties.

Limits and questions left open: the holding does not assign watershed status to capital-sentencing rules generally. It does not govern cases lacking a future-dangerousness argument, cases in which parole remains legally possible, disputes concerning practical release estimates, or cases in which the jury received an accurate instruction.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion, joined on this issue by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies a five-Justice majority.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand to reinstate conditional sentencing relief.

Public role: authors the opinion announcing the judgment and the issue-specific Opinions of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone classifies *Simmons* as new and procedural but holds that the precisely defined false-release-premise setting satisfies *Teague*’s existing watershed exception.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, would additionally hold that *Simmons* was dictated by the longstanding right to answer the State’s materially misleading capital-sentencing case.

Thomas, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Kennedy, agrees that *Simmons* was new but concludes that its limited right of rebuttal does not meet the exceptional watershed threshold. That proposition does not control the judgment.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated application of *Teague*’s watershed exception. The exception remains narrow and is activated only by the specific alignment stated above. *Allridge* remains unchanged because parole there remained legally possible.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed 5-4. The simulation reverses 5-4 because Stone agrees with the historical majority that *Simmons* was new but agrees with the historical dissenters that, even on that assumption, the rule is retroactive in this exact capital-sentencing setting. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521151/usrep521151.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the sentencing record, *Teague* analysis, historical judgment, and the historical dissent’s alternative watershed position. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521151/usrep521151.pdf))
