### Agostini v. Felton, 521 U.S. 203 (1997)

Argued April 15, 1997 | Decided June 23, 1997

From: the Second Circuit’s affirmance of an order refusing relief from the permanent injunction implementing *Aguilar v. Felton*. The injunction required New York City to provide Title I remedial services to religious-school students through off-site arrangements rather than public teachers working inside the schools. The teachers, curriculum, materials, and supervision remained public, and the record contained no finding of religious diversion or control. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521203/usrep521203.pdf))

Questions presented: whether Rule 60(b)(5) requires relief from the continuing injunction after intervening doctrinal change, and whether publicly controlled secular Title I instruction on religious-school premises violates the Establishment Clause.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Aguilar* directly supports the injunction, but later simulated decisions make attribution depend on actual control, sponsorship, financing, content, and program operation rather than religious premises alone. They also distinguish equal public services from financing devotional activity.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded in both consolidated dockets, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The lower courts shall dissolve the permanent injunction. New York City may provide on-premises Title I services under the program and safeguards described by the Court.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens and Ginsburg in full; Breyer as to the Establishment Clause merits | Would affirm | Defends *Aguilar* and *Ball* against reconsideration on the merits |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Stevens, Souter, and Breyer | Would affirm | Rejects use of Rule 60(b)(5) to reopen the continuing injunction in this posture |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Relief from the prospective injunction

Holding: Rule 60(b)(5) requires dissolution of a prospective injunction when the controlling legal rule supporting its continued operation has been overruled.

Reasoning: the lower courts correctly treated *Aguilar* as binding until this Court reconsidered it. The injunction, however, regulates future public administration and cannot remain in force after its sole constitutional premise has been rejected. The continuing expense and reduced practical access reinforce the inequity of prospective enforcement but do not substitute for the necessary legal change.

Limits and questions left open: lower courts may not anticipate this Court’s overruling of its own precedent, and completed liability judgments are not reopened merely because later decisions create doctrinal tension.

Legal basis: Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 60(b)(5).

##### Publicly controlled secular instruction on religious premises

Holding: New York City’s Title I program does not violate the Establishment Clause merely because public employees deliver secular remedial instruction on religious-school premises.

Operative rule: the service is constitutional when eligibility is independent of religion, government retains control of personnel, curriculum, materials, and assessment, no public funds or discretionary educational authority pass to the religious institution, the instruction is secular and operationally separable from worship, and practical safeguards prevent diversion without requiring intrusive religious surveillance.

Reasoning: children receive the service because of educational need, not religious affiliation. Public teachers answer to public supervisors and use public materials. Religious officials neither choose the beneficiaries nor direct the instruction. Ordinary professional supervision creates less church-state entanglement than the elaborate off-site system mandated by the injunction.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not authorize financing of worship, devotional instruction, theological formation, clergy training, or programs in which religious officials control public personnel or content. Diversion, coercion, and governmental attribution remain subject to challenge.

Legal basis: Establishment Clause and Title I.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand in both dockets.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s use of Rule 60(b)(5), its control-and-attribution analysis, and its express rejection of presumptions based solely on location and public employment.

#### Material Separate Writings

Souter, joined as stated, would retain the line drawn in *Aguilar* and *Ball*, treating on-premises public instruction as direct support for the religious schools’ educational function.

Ginsburg, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Breyer, concludes that the motion improperly uses Rule 60(b)(5) as a substitute for rehearing this Court’s earlier judgment. These propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Aguilar v. Felton* and the corresponding portion of *School District of Grand Rapids v. Ball* are overruled insofar as they presume indoctrination, governmental attribution, or excessive entanglement from the religious location and public employment alone.

The simulated distinctions among neutral public access, direct public service, and public financing of devotional activity remain intact. The decision does not disturb the latter prohibition.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, 5-4 vote, author, principal lineup, and overruling of the relevant portions of *Aguilar* and *Ball* match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521203/usrep521203.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the program, injunction, Rule 60(b)(5) posture, judgment, and historical opinion structure. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep521/usrep521203/usrep521203.pdf))

---

### Cohen v. Brown University, 101 F.3d 155

Argued May 19, 1997 | Decided June 23, 1997, on a simulation-assigned date

From: the First Circuit’s affirmance of Title IX liability and a continuing compliance injunction, together with its vacatur of an order directing Brown to elevate four identified women’s teams to university-funded varsity status. The trial record showed material underrepresentation, no continuing practice of responsive expansion, and competitively viable unmet interests among female students. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/101/155/596492/))

Questions presented: whether Title IX’s three alternative effective-accommodation routes are valid, how the third route measures unmet interests and abilities, whether its sex-conscious operation satisfies equal protection, and whether the district court could prescribe particular teams before Brown received another opportunity to select a lawful compliance method.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois* recognizes program-wide equality and three alternative compliance routes. Substantial proportionality is a safe harbor, not a quota or exclusive command.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Title IX liability and continuing compliance obligation | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | Brown remains liable and must submit an effective compliance plan. |
| Vacatur of the four-team remedial order | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | Brown receives a further opportunity to choose among lawful compliance methods. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full; Scalia and Thomas as to the three-route framework and remedial sequence | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for seven Justices; partial joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Supports the remedial disposition but opposes the liability affirmance | Accepts alternative routes and institutional choice; rejects the Court’s third-route interpretation and equal-protection application |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Alternative compliance routes and the third route

Holding: Title IX permits an institution to demonstrate effective accommodation through substantial proportionality, a continuing practice of responsive expansion, or full accommodation of the underrepresented sex’s demonstrated interests and abilities.

Operative rule: the third route asks whether actual, competitively viable unmet interests and abilities of the underrepresented sex are fully and effectively accommodated. Aggregate comparisons between male and female interest levels do not excuse proved unmet demand. Numerical imbalance alone establishes neither liability nor a mandatory quota.

Reasoning: proportionality is one safe harbor rather than the statutory command itself. The third route evaluates the opportunities actually available and the interests and abilities actually shown. An institution cannot preserve unequal opportunity by invoking aggregate interest differences that the existing distribution of opportunities may itself have produced.

Application: Brown materially underrepresented women, did not establish a continuing practice of responsive expansion, and left demonstrated viable interests unaccommodated. Liability rests on the program’s operation and the unmet demand, not enrollment arithmetic alone.

Limits and questions left open: the holding preserves no particular team and requires no unsupported, noncompetitive, or infeasible program.

Authority of the proposition: the three-route structure and nonquota rule are unanimous. The third-route construction and liability application are supported by O’Connor’s opinion joined by Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing a seven-Justice majority.

##### Equal protection

Holding: the third-route enforcement framework satisfies intermediate scrutiny as applied to Brown.

Reasoning: correcting demonstrated program-wide inequality in athletic opportunities is an important governmental objective. Responding to proved, competitively viable unmet interests of the underrepresented sex is substantially related to that objective and does not rest on stereotypes about what men or women ordinarily prefer. The classification ends when effective accommodation is achieved.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not approve every future sex-conscious remedial order or dispense with review of the particular means selected.

Legal basis: Fifth Amendment equal protection, Title IX, and 34 C.F.R. § 106.41.

Authority of the proposition: seven Justices.

##### Remedial sequence

Holding: the First Circuit properly vacated the order elevating four particular teams and preserved Brown’s initial authority to choose an effective lawful method of compliance.

Operative rule: after liability is established, the institution ordinarily receives a fair opportunity to select among lawful compliance routes. A court may prescribe more specific relief if supported findings show that institutional choice has failed or will not cure the violation.

Authority of the proposition: unanimous.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm the First Circuit’s judgment in full.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the three alternative routes, the demonstrated-unmet-interest interpretation, the intermediate-scrutiny holding, and the institution-first remedial sequence.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would not permit third-route liability to turn on sex-specific unmet interests without a more individualized showing that the institution denied opportunities because of sex. They nevertheless agree that proportionality is not an exclusive quota and that the district court prematurely selected particular teams.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Kelley* is applied and clarified. Its three alternative routes remain intact. The present decision defines full accommodation under the third route and confirms that numerical disparity is evidence relevant to the program-wide inquiry, not an independent violation.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulation affirms the First Circuit’s liability and remedial judgment while making explicit the equal-protection basis and the distinction between demonstrated unmet demand and numerical imbalance alone. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/101/155/596492/))

Research sources: the First Circuit opinion for Brown’s program, participation record, three-part framework, liability judgment, and remedial disposition. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/101/155/596492/))

### Idaho v. Coeur d’Alene Tribe of Idaho, 521 U.S. 261 (1997)

Argued October 16, 1996 | Decided June 23, 1997

From: the Ninth Circuit’s ruling that the Eleventh Amendment barred the Tribe’s claims against Idaho and its agencies and its direct quiet-title claim, but permitted prospective declaratory and injunctive claims against responsible state officials. The Tribe alleged that those officials continued to regulate submerged lands and connected waters contrary to federal tribal property rights. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-261.pdf))

Question presented: whether *Ex parte Young* permits prospective relief against responsible Idaho officials when determining the alleged ongoing federal violation requires resolution of a disputed federal title question.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Seminole Tribe* left the independent availability of the *Young* officer channel unresolved. No simulated holding creates a property-specific or sovereign-lands exception to the ordinary distinction between prospective officer relief and relief against the State or its treasury.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The Ninth Circuit’s remand proceeds. Idaho, its agencies, and claims seeking retrospective or direct quiet-title relief against the State remain dismissed. The prospective claims against responsible officials may proceed.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas | Would reverse the officer-suit ruling | Treats the requested relief as the functional equivalent of quiet title against the State |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Prospective enforcement of federal tribal property rights

Holding: the Eleventh Amendment does not bar prospective declaratory and injunctive relief against responsible state officials to end an alleged ongoing violation of federal tribal property rights, even when the federal court must resolve a disputed title question to determine whether the officials’ conduct is unlawful.

Operative rule: the complaint must allege an ongoing violation of federal law, identify officials with a genuine enforcement connection, and seek relief directed to their future conduct. The officer action may not award damages, retrospective compensation, an accounting payable by the State, execution against state property, or a decree requiring the State itself to convey title.

Reasoning: the Tribe alleges that officials presently exercise regulatory authority over lands federal law reserves to the Tribe. Deciding ownership or jurisdiction as an antecedent to prospective relief does not convert the officers into the State. Making federal enforceability depend on the importance of the State’s claimed property interest would create an exception that *Ex parte Young* does not supply.

Application: the district court shall determine the federal rights asserted, identify any ongoing violation, and tailor any injunction to ending unlawful future official interference.

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide ownership of the submerged lands or authorize direct quiet-title relief against Idaho.

Legal basis: Supremacy Clause, Article III, Eleventh Amendment, and *Ex parte Young*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s refusal to create a sovereign-lands exception and its strict preservation of the distinction between prospective officer relief and relief against the State itself.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, concludes that the relief would effectively eliminate Idaho’s sovereign control over uniquely important submerged lands and therefore operates as a quiet-title judgment against the State despite its nominal officer form.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The open *Ex parte Young* question reserved in simulated *Seminole Tribe* is resolved for an ordinary ongoing federal-law claim lacking a congressionally exclusive remedial sequence. The State, its agencies, its treasury, and retrospective relief remain protected.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 5-4 and treated the relief as the functional equivalent of quiet title against Idaho. In the simulation, Stone joins the historical dissenters, producing a 5-4 affirmance and rejecting a property-specific exception to *Ex parte Young*. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-261.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the submerged-land claim, requested relief, historical judgment, and fractured historical opinion structure. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-261.pdf))

### Kansas v. Hendricks; Hendricks v. Kansas, 521 U.S. 346 (1997)

Argued December 10, 1996 | Decided June 23, 1997

From: consolidated cross-petitions concerning a Kansas Supreme Court judgment invalidating the State’s Sexually Violent Predator Act. Hendricks had been found beyond a reasonable doubt to be dangerous because of pedophilia and impaired control. The statute authorized post-sentence confinement, treatment, annual review, and release when the qualifying condition and danger ended. The state court did not resolve the preserved double-jeopardy and ex-post-facto claims. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-346.pdf))

Questions presented: whether the statutory commitment criteria satisfy substantive due process and whether Kansas’s application of the Act after completion of Hendricks’s criminal sentence is punitive.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *United States v. Ursery* requires deference to a civil designation unless objective design and operation supply the clearest proof of punishment. Same-term *United States v. Sahhar* permits continuing civil custody only while current illness-caused dangerousness, meaningful review, release mechanisms, and the statute’s civil administration remain genuine.

#### Judgment

The judgment of the Kansas Supreme Court is affirmed in both consolidated matters, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

Hendricks may not remain confined under this application of the Act for conduct predating its enactment. The judgment does not foreclose another independently lawful civil-commitment route.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete as-applied Ex Post Facto opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia, and Kennedy | Would reverse | Would sustain the commitment as civil and nonpunitive |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Punitive operation and retrospective confinement

Holding: Kansas’s Act supplies the clearest proof of punishment as applied to Hendricks on this record, and imposing that post-sentence confinement for preenactment conduct violates the Ex Post Facto Clause.

Operative rule: no single feature controls punitive classification. A civil designation may be overcome when the statute’s combined design and actual operation demonstrate that the custody functions as added punishment rather than a genuinely administered civil treatment-and-release system.

Reasoning: the Act selected its class through prior criminal conduct and initiated commitment only at the end of the criminal sentence. Hendricks remained in prisonlike custody, meaningful treatment had been delayed or inadequately administered, less restrictive civil alternatives were not genuinely considered, and the record did not establish an operational treatment-and-release structure sufficient to distinguish the added confinement from punishment. Those features together satisfy the demanding *Ursery* standard.

Limits and questions left open: prior crime, post-sentence timing, confinement, deterrent effect, or imperfect treatment alone does not establish punishment. The holding is record specific and does not facially invalidate every prospective application of Kansas’s statute or comparable civil-commitment legislation.

Legal basis: Article I, § 10, Ex Post Facto Clause.

##### Questions not reached

The Court does not endorse the Kansas Supreme Court’s conclusion that the term mental abnormality is categorically inadequate under substantive due process. It also does not decide the separate Double Jeopardy claim because the Ex Post Facto ground fully sustains the judgment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm the Kansas Supreme Court’s judgment on the alternative as-applied Ex Post Facto ground.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s total-operation analysis and its record-specific conclusion that the statutory civil label is overcome here.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Kennedy, would treat the statutory treatment obligation, annual review, release criteria, and dangerousness finding as sufficient to preserve the Act’s civil character. The dissent concludes that operational shortcomings do not supply the clearest proof required to negate legislative designation.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Ursery* is applied and clarified through an as-applied punitive classification. Same-term *Sahhar* is distinguished: the federal scheme there retained a current illness-and-dangerousness predicate, meaningful review and release routes, and an operative placement structure. The present record does not establish equivalent civil administration.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 5-4 and sustained the Act as civil. The simulation affirms 5-4 because Stone joins the historical dissenting coalition and the Court confines its ruling to the record-specific punitive application. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-346.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the commitment criteria, procedural history, treatment record, historical judgment, and competing punitive classifications. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-346.pdf))

### Lindh v. Murphy, 521 U.S. 320 (1997)

Argued April 14, 1997 | Decided June 23, 1997

From: the en banc Seventh Circuit’s application of AEDPA’s amended chapter 153 standards to Lindh’s noncapital habeas case. Lindh filed his federal application in 1992, and the appeal was pending when AEDPA became effective on April 24, 1996. The Act expressly addressed pending cases in chapter 154 but included no comparable pending-case instruction for chapter 153. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-320.pdf))

Question presented: whether amended § 2254(d) and the related chapter 153 provisions govern a noncapital habeas application filed before AEDPA’s effective date.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Landgraf* supplies the general temporal sequence. *Felker v. Turpin* applied AEDPA’s expressly directed gatekeeping provisions but did not decide the ordinary chapter 153 amendments.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The Seventh Circuit shall adjudicate Lindh’s appeal under the habeas law governing when his application was filed. The Court expresses no view on the underlying Confrontation Clause claim.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy and Thomas | Would affirm | Would apply the amended law to all cases pending on the effective date absent an express contrary command |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Temporal reach of chapter 153

Holding: AEDPA’s chapter 153 amendments do not govern a noncapital federal habeas application filed before April 24, 1996.

Operative rule: the relevant boundary is the filing of the federal application. Chapter 153 governs applications filed after enactment, while chapter 154 follows its own express pending-case instruction.

Reasoning: Congress enacted the two chapters together and expressly made chapter 154 applicable to pending cases. The omission of comparable language from chapter 153 is meaningful and shows that Congress considered temporal reach but selected a different operation. The application-based boundary also avoids altering the legal standards governing a proceeding invoked years before enactment.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not govern post-AEDPA applications, chapter 154 cases within its express clause, successive applications governed by *Felker*, or a provision carrying a different enacted temporal command.

Legal basis: AEDPA §§ 101 through 107 and 28 U.S.C. chapter 153.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the enacted structural contrast and the application-based temporal boundary.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Landgraf* is applied through AEDPA’s more specific structure. *Felker* remains confined to provisions whose text and procedural setting supplied a different temporal answer.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 5-4. Stone rejects the historical Chief Justice’s dissenting position and joins Justice Souter’s opinion, producing a 6-3 simulated judgment. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-320.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the filing chronology, statutory contrast, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-320.pdf))

### Richardson v. McKnight, 521 U.S. 399 (1997)

Argued March 19, 1997 | Decided June 23, 1997

From: the Sixth Circuit’s affirmance of the denial of qualified immunity to two guards employed by a private, for-profit company operating a Tennessee prison. The guards were treated as acting under color of state law, but their employer operated through ordinary market, employment, insurance, and contractual arrangements rather than the State’s civil-service and indemnification systems. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-399.pdf))

Question presented: whether privately employed prison guards may invoke the qualified immunity available to comparable public correctional officers.

Controlling simulated law before decision: the existing qualified-immunity cases govern eligible officials and collateral-order review but do not decide categorical eligibility for privately employed prison personnel. *Wyatt v. Cole* rejected immunity for private replevin actors while leaving materially different functions open.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4. Supporting: Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The denial of qualified immunity remains in force. The underlying constitutional claim, causation, damages, corporate liability, and any distinct good-faith defense remain for further proceedings.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, and Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy and Thomas | Would reverse | Would extend public-officer qualified immunity to private guards performing the same custodial function |
| Dissent | Stone | None | Would reverse and remand | Rejects categorical ineligibility but requires an individualized conduct-based immunity inquiry |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Qualified immunity for guards employed by a for-profit prison firm

Holding: guards employed by a private, for-profit prison-management company are not entitled to qualified immunity from § 1983 damages actions in the circumstances presented.

Operative rule: the holding applies where the private firm is systematically organized to perform the custodial function for profit, operates in a competitive market, maintains ordinary insurance and employment arrangements, and is not integrated into the State’s civil-service, indemnification, or closely supervised public-officer structure.

Reasoning: the historical record does not establish a firmly rooted immunity for privately employed jailers of this kind. Market competition, contractual monitoring, insurance, replacement of personnel, and ordinary employment discipline provide incentives against timidity and unlawful conduct that perform much of the work attributed to public-officer immunity.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not govern a private person briefly assisting government, a nonprofit or uniquely public institution, a contractor subject to direct day-to-day governmental control, or any separate good-faith defense. State action remains distinct from immunity.

Legal basis: 42 U.S.C. § 1983 and the federal common law of qualified immunity.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: dissents.

Announced ground: Stone concludes that private payroll status alone neither grants nor categorically defeats qualified immunity. He would require the lower court first to decide whether a constitutional violation occurred and then determine whether each guard earned individual protection through objectively reasonable conduct under the law and information then available.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas, would grant the guards the same categorical eligibility afforded comparable public correctional officers because the delegated coercive function and risk of overdeterrence are materially identical.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule denying qualified immunity to employees of a competitive, for-profit prison-management company under the stated conditions. *Elder*, *Johnson v. Jones*, and *Behrens* remain unchanged because they concern the content and appellate treatment of immunity once the actor is eligible.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated 5-4 affirmance and Court-opinion lineup match the historical decision. Stone does not reproduce the historical Chief Justice’s complete join in Scalia’s dissent, instead preserving a narrower individualized approach in a separate writing. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-399.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the private-prison structure, immunity question, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-399.pdf))

### Amchem Products, Inc. v. Windsor, 521 U.S. 591 (1997)

Argued February 18, 1997 | Decided June 25, 1997

From: the Third Circuit’s vacatur of certification, settlement approval, and a classwide injunction covering a proposed global asbestos settlement. The class combined currently injured claimants, exposure-only claimants, and family members under varying state laws, without separately represented subclasses. The agreement imposed disease categories, payment ranges, annual caps, and restrictions affecting present and future claimants differently. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-591.pdf))

Questions presented: whether settlement-only certification relaxes Rule 23’s predominance and adequate-representation requirements, and whether this proposed class satisfies them.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Matsushita* requires constitutionally adequate class process before absent members may be bound. *Richards v. Jefferson County* requires genuinely aligned interests, accountable representation, and adequate procedure. Neither creates a settlement exception to Rule 23.

Participation: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Nonparticipation: Justice O’Connor, basis not publicly stated. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-591.pdf))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-2. Supporting: Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg. Opposing: Stevens and Breyer.

The certification, settlement approval, and classwide injunction remain vacated. The parties remain free to pursue a materially different settlement consistent with Rule 23.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | Stevens | Would remand for renewed certification analysis | Agrees that all Rule 23 requirements apply but would give greater weight to settlement in the predominance and adequacy inquiries |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Rule 23 in a settlement-only class

Holding: a settlement-only class must satisfy every applicable requirement of Rule 23. Settlement may eliminate trial-management difficulties, but it does not relax predominance, typicality, commonality, or adequate representation.

Reasoning: Rule 23(e)’s fairness review supplements rather than replaces the certification requirements. A court may account for the absence of a trial when assessing manageability, but the settlement cannot manufacture common issues or cure conflicting representative incentives.

Legal basis: Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 23(a), 23(b)(3), and 23(e).

##### Predominance and adequate representation

Holding: the proposed asbestos class fails Rule 23 because individual and state-law questions overwhelm the common issues and one undivided set of representatives cannot adequately protect the conflicting interests of currently injured and future claimants.

Reasoning: common exposure to asbestos does not answer product identification, latency, causation, injury, limitations, defenses, or damages across numerous jurisdictions. The agreement itself demonstrates structural conflict. Present claimants value immediate fixed payments, while exposure-only claimants bear future diagnostic uncertainty, inflation, annual caps, and the surrender of claims that may not yet exist. General notice and opt-out rights do not replace representatives whose incentives correspond to each group.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not make mass-tort settlement classes categorically impossible or prescribe one mandatory subclass structure. Separately represented subclasses, narrower claim groupings, and individual settlements remain possible.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s insistence that settlement does not dilute Rule 23 and its conclusion that the present-future claimant conflict requires accountable separate representation.

#### Material Separate Writings

Breyer, joined by Stevens, agrees that settlement-only classes remain subject to Rule 23 but would remand for a more settlement-sensitive assessment rather than affirm decertification on the existing record.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Matsushita* and *Richards* are applied and clarified. Constitutionally adequate class process requires compliance with Rule 23’s structural protections before a federal settlement may bind absent claimants, particularly persons whose injuries and claims have not yet matured.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated participation, 6-2 judgment, author, principal lineup, and disposition match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the supporting position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-591.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the proposed class, settlement structure, representation conflicts, participation, judgment, and historical lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-591.pdf))

### City of Boerne v. Flores, 521 U.S. 507 (1997)

Argued February 19, 1997 | Decided June 25, 1997

From: the Fifth Circuit’s reversal of an interlocutory district-court ruling that Congress lacked authority to apply RFRA to Boerne’s historic-preservation permitting decision. RFRA imposed compelling-interest and least-restrictive-means review on every state and local rule substantially burdening religious exercise, including neutral and generally applicable rules governed constitutionally by *Employment Division v. Smith*. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-507.pdf))

Question presented: whether Congress validly subjected States and local governments to RFRA through § 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Church of Scientology* and *Swanner* applied RFRA to state or local conduct but did not separately decide Congress’s constitutional authority. *Smith* remains the judicially enforceable Free Exercise rule for neutral, generally applicable state laws.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded with instructions to dismiss the RFRA claim against the City, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Ginsburg. Opposing: O’Connor, Souter, and Breyer.

Preserved constitutional, property, speech, state-law, or other claims may proceed on their own elements.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, Thomas, and Ginsburg in full; Scalia except the historical Free Exercise subsection | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion at the stated joins |
| Concurrence | Stevens | None | Supports reversal | Adds an Establishment Clause concern |
| Concurrence in part | Scalia | Stevens | Supports reversal | Defends the historical foundation of *Smith* |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Breyer except as to the opening portion | Would affirm | Would reconsider *Smith* before invalidating RFRA |
| Dissent | Souter | None | Would affirm | Questions the historical premise underlying *Smith* |
| Dissent | Breyer | None | Would affirm | Would avoid the broad § 5 holding while reconsidering the constitutional baseline |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Section 5 enforcement power

Holding: § 5 authorizes preventive and remedial legislation congruent and proportional to Fourteenth Amendment violations Congress may address, but RFRA’s universal application to state and local government exceeds that authority by replacing the governing constitutional rule.

Operative rule: Congress may regulate some otherwise constitutional state conduct when the measure is fitted to a demonstrated pattern or serious threat of constitutional violations. The scope and burden of the remedy must remain congruent and proportional to the injury to be prevented or corrected. Section 5 does not authorize Congress to redefine the substance of the Fourteenth Amendment.

Reasoning: RFRA applies across every state and local law, subject, official, and level of government, without a jurisdictional, subject-matter, temporal, or problem-specific limit tied to identified Free Exercise violations. Its compelling-interest and least-restrictive-means rule displaces *Smith* even where the Constitution itself permits the challenged state action. That is substantive replacement rather than fitted enforcement.

Application: because Boerne is a local government and the RFRA claim depends solely on the Act’s universal state-and-local command, the claim must be dismissed.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not reconsider *Smith*, determine RFRA’s validity as applied to the Federal Government, decide Congress’s authority under another enumerated power or a lawful spending condition, or restrict States from providing broader religious-exercise protection under state law.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment § 5.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand with instructions to dismiss the RFRA claim against the City.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s distinction between enforcement and constitutional replacement, its congruence-and-proportionality requirement, and its refusal to decide RFRA’s federal application or reconsider *Smith*.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor, joined in part by Breyer, would reconsider *Smith* and concludes that Congress may have legislated against an unduly narrow constitutional baseline. Souter separately questions *Smith*’s historical foundation. Stevens and Scalia state distinct supporting grounds, but neither enlarges the controlling § 5 holding.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Simulated *Church of Scientology* and *Swanner* are overruled insofar as they treat RFRA as universally enforceable against state and local governments under § 5. Their final judgments remain intact because the antecedent power question was not decided there.

Their provision-specific substantial-burden, compelling-interest, and least-restrictive-means formulations continue to govern any RFRA application resting on a valid source of federal authority. RFRA’s application to the Federal Government is not adjudicated.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated 6-3 judgment, author, principal lineup, partial Scalia join, and separate writings match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the position held by the historical Chief Justice. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-507.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the permitting dispute, RFRA’s scope, § 5 analysis, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-507.pdf))

# OCTOBER TERM 1996, CHUNK 7 OF 7

This chunk adds *United States v. O’Hagan* to the uncoordinated June 25 decision group already rendered in Chunk 6; *O’Hagan*, *Amchem Products*, and *City of Boerne* therefore share the June 24 entering-law baseline, while the three June 26 decisions likewise use one common baseline and do not influence one another.

The approved Chunk 7 brief controls the case records and Stone’s positions. `OT_1996CHUNK7.md` The Sim Engine and Render Contract govern adjudication and presentation. `SUPREME_COURT_TERM_SIMULATOR_ENGINE.md` `JUDICIAL_TURN_OUTPUT_RENDER_CONTRACT.md` The controlled composition, Standards and Tests, Holdings Register, and Standing State establish the roster, term-opening law, and procedural baseline. `Supreme Court Composition — Simulation (OT1993–OT2026).txt` `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md` `STANDING_STATE_.md`

Historical-comparator correction: the attached *O’Hagan* brief describes the historical mail-fraud holding as 7-2. The official opinion shows that Scalia joined the Court’s mail-fraud section and that Thomas, joined by the historical Chief Justice, separately concurred in reinstatement of those counts. The historical mail-fraud disposition was therefore unanimous, although the misappropriation and Rule 14e-3 questions had narrower coalitions. Supp. 1248 is the October 25, 1995 denial of reconsideration, which itself describes the underlying order as dated July 27. The citation correction does not alter the judgment under review or Stone’s position. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/district-courts/FSupp/894/1360/2094899/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. O’Hagan, 521 U.S. 642 (1997)

Argued April 16, 1997 | Decided June 25, 1997

From: the Eighth Circuit’s reversal of O’Hagan’s securities-fraud, tender-offer, mail-fraud, and money-laundering convictions. O’Hagan obtained confidential information through his law firm’s representation of a contemplated tender offeror, acquired Pillsbury securities before public announcement, and realized more than $4.3 million after the offer became public. The United States sought review of the securities and related mail-fraud rulings, but not the money-laundering disposition. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-642.pdf))

Questions presented: whether deceptive misappropriation from an information source falls within § 10(b) and Rule 10b-5 when securities trading monetizes the breach, and whether Rule 14e-3(a) lies within the preventive rulemaking authority granted by § 14(e).

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Central Bank of Denver* preserves a distinction between limited secondary-assistance liability and a defendant’s own primary deception. *MCI Telecommunications* requires agency power to arise from enacted authority rather than administrative assertion. No prior simulated holding resolves source-directed deceptive trading or Rule 14e-3(a).

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Section 10(b) and Rule 10b-5 convictions | Reversed and remanded, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The Eighth Circuit shall address any preserved instructional, sufficiency, or harmless-error issue not resolved by the governing legal rule. |
| Section 14(e) and Rule 14e-3(a) convictions | Reversed and remanded, 8-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Thomas | Preserved alternative objections to the rule’s application remain available on remand. |
| Mail-fraud convictions | Reversed and remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No opposing Justice | Alternate challenges to those convictions not reached below remain open. |

##### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The Eighth Circuit’s judgment is reversed as to the securities-fraud, tender-offer, and mail-fraud counts. It shall reinstate those convictions to the extent supported by valid instructions and sufficient evidence or resolve any preserved alternative ground left undecided.

The United States did not seek review of the separate money-laundering ruling. That portion of the Eighth Circuit’s judgment remains undisturbed.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer in full; Scalia as to Rule 14e-3(a) and mail fraud | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for seven Justices; partial join as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports reversal on Rule 14e-3(a) and mail fraud; opposes the § 10(b) ruling | Rejects the misappropriation construction of § 10(b) |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports reversal on mail fraud; opposes both securities rulings | Treats the mail-fraud counts as independently sustainable under *Carpenter* |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Primary § 10(b) liability for deceptive misappropriation

Holding: a trader commits primary fraud under § 10(b) when, with scienter, the trader secretly uses material nonpublic information entrusted by a source participating in an actual or contemplated securities transaction, breaches a defined duty of fidelity or confidentiality to that source, and trades securities whose value is materially affected by the source’s transaction. The trading must consummate or monetize the concealed breach.

Operative rule: liability requires a defined source duty, deceptive nondisclosure of the planned use, material nonpublic information connected to the source’s securities activity, scienter, and transaction-linked trading. Disclosure of the intended use to the source before trading removes the deception required by this theory.

Reasoning: § 10(b) reaches a deceptive device used in connection with a securities purchase or sale and does not confine the deceived party to the buyer or seller. O’Hagan allegedly feigned loyalty to the law firm and client that entrusted the tender-offer information, then used the precise securities transaction made valuable by that information to produce his gain. The source relationship, contemplated tender offer, target securities, and trading profit form one connected fraudulent course rather than an unrelated breach followed by a coincidental market transaction. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-642.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: the decision creates no general parity-of-information rule, reaches no innocent receipt of information, and does not federalize every misuse of confidential material followed by securities trading. It reserves relationships involving family, friendship, government service, ordinary employment, or commerce when the source is not participating in the securities transaction or the information is unrelated to that transaction.

Legal basis: Exchange Act § 10(b) and SEC Rule 10b-5.

Authority of the proposition: Ginsburg’s opinion, joined on this issue by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer, supplies a seven-Justice majority.

##### Rule 14e-3(a) and the preventive tender-offer delegation

Holding: § 14(e) authorizes Rule 14e-3(a)’s bounded prohibition on trading while possessing material nonpublic tender-offer information obtained from the offeror, issuer, or persons acting for them, when the trader knows or has reason to know the information came from one of those sources.

Operative rule: § 14(e)’s express authority to define and prescribe means reasonably designed to prevent fraudulent, deceptive, or manipulative tender-offer practices permits a prophylactic rule extending beyond proof of completed common-law fraud. The rule must remain fitted to the tender-offer domain and the source, information, and knowledge conditions enacted in the regulation.

Reasoning: tender offers depend on compressed timing and unusual secrecy. Trading before announcement may exploit information obtained through deception even when proof of the underlying source breach is difficult after the fact. Congress expressly granted preventive authority in that defined transactional field. The Court therefore sustains the rule through independent construction of § 14(e), without relying on a general power to equalize market information or on deference to create authority the statute does not contain. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-642.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not authorize regulation of all confidential-information trading, decide every application involving source-approved trading or warehousing, or dispense with Rule 14e-3(a)’s source and knowledge limitations.

Legal basis: Exchange Act § 14(e) and Rule 14e-3(a).

Authority of the proposition: Ginsburg’s opinion, joined on this issue by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer, supplies an eight-Justice majority.

##### Mail fraud

Holding: the Eighth Circuit may not sustain reversal of the mail-fraud convictions on the premise that the securities theories are legally invalid.

Reasoning: eight Justices conclude that the Court’s securities rulings remove the basis on which the Eighth Circuit reversed the mail-fraud counts. Thomas reaches the same judgment on the separate ground that the indictment and instructions independently describe fraudulent conversion of entrusted information under *Carpenter*. Any other preserved objection remains for the court of appeals. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-642.pdf))

Authority of the proposition: the judgment is unanimous. The securities-dependent rationale commands eight votes; Thomas supplies an independent judgment-supporting ground.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand as to the securities-fraud, tender-offer, and mail-fraud counts; leave the money-laundering ruling outside the mandate.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the source-bound primary-fraud rule, the independent statutory validation of Rule 14e-3(a), and the remand preserving unaddressed record and instruction questions.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia agrees that § 14(e) contains sufficient preventive authority and that the mail-fraud judgment must be reversed, but concludes that § 10(b) does not state the misappropriation theory with sufficient clarity.

Thomas would reject both securities theories. He nevertheless concludes that the alleged secret conversion of the firm’s and client’s confidential property independently supports the mail-fraud counts under *Carpenter*.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Central Bank of Denver* is clarified, not altered. Its distinct rule governing limited private secondary assistance does not displace primary liability for a defendant’s own deception.

*MCI Telecommunications* is applied without modification. The SEC’s authority here arises from § 14(e)’s enacted preventive delegation; agency interpretation alone cannot supply the power.

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rules for source-directed misappropriation under § 10(b) and for Rule 14e-3(a).

#### Historical Comparison

The historical misappropriation holding was 6-3. Stone joins Justice Ginsburg’s position rather than the historical Chief Justice’s dissent, producing a 7-2 simulated majority. The Rule 14e-3 holding becomes 8-1 for the same reason. The mail-fraud disposition remains unanimous after correcting the attached brief’s historical vote description. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-642.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the transaction record, statutory holdings, judgment, opinion lineup, mail-fraud treatment, and unreviewed money-laundering ruling. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-642.pdf))

### Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union, 521 U.S. 844 (1997)

Argued March 19, 1997 | Decided June 26, 1997

From: direct appeal from a three-judge District Court order preliminarily enjoining the Communications Decency Act’s criminal restrictions on transmitting or displaying “indecent” or “patently offensive” material in a manner available to minors. The injunction preserved enforcement against obscenity and child sexual-abuse material under independently operative provisions. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-844.pdf))

Question presented: whether the challenged provisions are facially invalid because their content-based, vague, and substantially overbroad terms suppress protected adult Internet speech without narrow tailoring.

Controlling simulated law before decision: simulated *Denver Area* requires realistic adult access when government channels material to protect children. Same-term *Turner Broadcasting II* preserves independent review of speech burdens and does not import broadcast scarcity into another medium. Same-term *United States v. Thomas* concerns controlled transmission of unprotected obscenity to a known recipient and expressly reserves open or anonymous Internet communication.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor.

The preliminary injunction remains in force against the challenged indecency and patently-offensive restrictions. Severable obscenity prohibitions and independently valid laws governing unprotected material remain available.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | No other Justice | Would preserve narrow applications to knowing one-to-one adult-to-minor communications | Agrees that the display provision and broad audience applications are unconstitutional |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Content-based criminal suppression of protected Internet speech

Holding: the CDA’s indecency-transmission and patently-offensive-display provisions are facially invalid because their vague and substantially overbroad content categories suppress a large amount of protected adult speech, and the Government has not established narrow tailoring in light of practical, materially less speech-restrictive means of limiting minors’ access.

Operative rule: a content-based criminal restriction on expression through a general-purpose communications network must define the prohibited content with adequate precision and preserve realistic adult access. Severe penalties, uncertain content categories, nationwide distribution, community-standard uncertainty, and speaker-side access controls that exclude lawful adults all enter the facial inquiry. Scienter concerning transmission does not cure uncertainty about what speech the law makes criminal.

Reasoning: “indecent” and “patently offensive” reach serious discussion of health, art, sexuality, politics, and personal experience. A speaker addressing adults cannot reliably know which minors may obtain access or which community standard will govern. Credit-card and password verification were economically impracticable for many noncommercial speakers, discouraged lawful adult access, and excluded adults lacking the required credentials. The record also identified user-controlled filtering and more targeted regulation of unprotected material and direct communication with identified minors. The Government did not show why those alternatives would be materially less effective. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-844.pdf))

The Internet’s open architecture supplies none of the spectrum-scarcity or pervasive-reception considerations historically associated with diminished broadcast protection. The medium therefore receives ordinary full First Amendment protection. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-844.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not protect obscenity, child sexual-abuse material, true threats, fraud, unlawful solicitation, or direct harmful contact with minors. It does not classify every Internet service under one constitutional category or decide a future, precisely drawn rule using genuinely effective and nonexclusionary age-assurance technology.

Legal basis: First Amendment.

##### Severability and the injunction

Holding: the words “or indecent” are severed from § 223(a)(1)(B), leaving its separately stated obscenity prohibition in force. Section 223(d)’s patently-offensive restriction remains enjoined because the text supplies no constitutionally administrable narrowing line.

Reasoning: obscenity is separately identified and may be prohibited. The open-ended protected-speech restrictions cannot be rewritten application by application without the Court assuming legislative work. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-844.pdf))

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm the District Court’s judgment.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s content-based classification, practical adult-access analysis, vagueness and overbreadth conclusions, and confined severability ruling.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor would construe and sustain the transmission provisions when an adult knowingly communicates indecent material only to one or more identified minors. She agrees that the provisions cannot govern mixed adult-minor audiences or open displays under the present technological conditions. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-844.pdf))

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Denver Area* is extended from cable channeling to a general-purpose communications network. Its practical adult-access principle applies with greater force where speaker-side compliance would exclude lawful adults or silence noncommercial speakers.

Same-term *Turner Broadcasting II* is distinguished. Cable’s intermediate-scrutiny framework does not govern these content-based criminal restrictions, and broadcast scarcity supplies no lesser Internet standard.

Same-term *United States v. Thomas* remains confined to targeted, controlled distribution of unprotected obscenity to a known recipient. It does not support suppression of protected indecent speech across an open network.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed 7-2, with the historical Chief Justice joining O’Connor’s partial dissent. Stone instead joins Justice Stevens’s complete opinion, producing an 8-1 simulated judgment. The controlling First Amendment rule and the remaining O’Connor position otherwise track the historical decision. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-844.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the statutory provisions, District Court injunction, factual findings concerning verification costs, First Amendment analysis, severability, and historical lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-844.pdf))

---

### Vacco v. Quill, 521 U.S. 793 (1997)

Argued January 8, 1997 | Decided June 26, 1997

From: the en banc Second Circuit’s judgment invalidating New York’s distinction between a competent patient’s refusal of life-sustaining treatment and intentional assistance in suicide. New York prohibited aiding suicide while recognizing treatment refusal, advance directives, and good-faith palliation. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-793.pdf))

Question presented: whether that conduct distinction violates equal protection.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Cruzan* protects competent refusal of unwanted bodily intervention. Simulated *Romer v. Evans* invalidates a status-based withdrawal of ordinary legal protection when independent legitimate purpose cannot explain the disability. No simulated holding treats assisted suicide as a protected status or equates treatment refusal with intentional assistance in death.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Second Circuit shall direct rejection of the equal-protection challenge. Independent rights to refuse unwanted treatment, enforce valid directives, and receive lawful pain relief or good-faith palliative care remain undisturbed.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | Ginsburg and Breyer in part | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes lawful pain relief and reserves materially different end-of-life applications |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Reserves exceptional applications |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Souter | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Relies on the State’s present regulatory judgment |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Incorporates her stated reservations concerning individual circumstances |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes the distinction between a right to die and freedom from intolerable suffering |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Treatment refusal and intentional assistance in suicide

Holding: a State does not violate equal protection by permitting a competent person to refuse unwanted medical treatment while prohibiting intentional assistance in suicide.

Operative rule: ordinary rational-basis review governs when the law applies without a suspect or status-based classification and distinguishes conduct through bodily consent, legal causation, intent, and professional role. Equal protection does not require government to collapse different acts merely because death may follow both.

Reasoning: treatment refusal directs that an unwanted bodily intervention stop or not begin, after which the underlying condition takes its course. Assisted suicide requires another actor intentionally to provide or administer the means of death. New York may also distinguish a physician who respects bodily noninterference or provides symptom relief from one who makes death the intended means or object. Those distinctions rationally serve interests in preserving life, preventing suicide, protecting vulnerable patients, and maintaining the medical profession’s caregiving role. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-793.pdf))

The prohibition applies across persons and does not withdraw ordinary civic protection from terminally ill or disabled people. The record supplies no basis for treating hostility or civic subordination as the law’s remaining explanation.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not authorize forced treatment, override a valid advance directive, permit discriminatory denial of pain care, or make good-faith palliation unlawful merely because death is foreseen. A materially different record of discriminatory administration remains subject to ordinary constitutional review.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Equal Protection Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone distinguishes bodily noninterference from affirmative lethal assistance and applies ordinary rational-basis review to New York’s conduct-based line.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor, joined in part by Ginsburg and Breyer, emphasizes that the judgment assumes continued practical access to lawful pain relief and palliative care. The concurrence reserves a case in which the State uses its suicide law to prevent medically appropriate alleviation of suffering.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Cruzan* is applied and distinguished. Its protection of treatment refusal does not create an affirmative entitlement to another person’s assistance in causing death.

*Romer v. Evans* is declined extension. New York’s law is neither a status-based political disability nor an unexplained withdrawal of ordinary legal protection.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated and historical judgments are unanimous. Stone authors the principal opinion in the institutional position occupied historically by the Chief Justice, while preserving the historical structure of a five-Justice Court opinion and narrower judgment concurrences. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-793.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for New York’s laws, the causation and intent distinction, legitimate state interests, judgment, and historical opinion structure. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-793.pdf))

---

### Washington v. Glucksberg, 521 U.S. 702 (1997)

Argued January 8, 1997 | Decided June 26, 1997

From: the en banc Ninth Circuit’s judgment recognizing a constitutional right for competent, terminally ill adults to obtain physician assistance in suicide. Washington prohibited knowingly causing or aiding suicide while preserving treatment refusal, advance directives, and good-faith palliative care. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-702.pdf))

Question presented: whether the Due Process Clause protects an affirmative right to obtain intentional physician assistance in suicide.

Controlling simulated law before decision: no simulated holding recognizes the asserted right. *Cruzan* protects competent refusal of unwanted medical treatment, and the ordinary substantive-due-process sequence requires the claimed liberty to be identified at a legally meaningful level before its constitutional status is assessed.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Ninth Circuit shall direct entry of judgment for Washington on the asserted substantive-due-process claim. Claims involving forced treatment, valid treatment refusal, lawful pain relief, good-faith palliation, or discriminatory administration remain outside the disposition.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | Ginsburg and Breyer in part | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes the availability of palliative care and reserves narrower applications |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Reserves exceptional individual claims |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Souter | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Would employ a more searching assessment of the State’s reasons in a narrower case |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Incorporates her separate end-of-life reservations |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Frames the protected concern as avoidance of severe suffering rather than a general right to assisted suicide |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Asserted fundamental right to physician-assisted suicide

Holding: the Due Process Clause does not protect a fundamental right to obtain intentional physician assistance in suicide, and Washington’s prohibition survives rational-basis review.

Operative rule: the asserted liberty must be identified as the legal entitlement actually sought rather than as autonomy at the highest level of generality. The Court examines constitutional text and structure, precedent, the function of the claimed protection, and relevant legal tradition. When those sources do not establish a fundamental right, the law receives rational-basis review.

Reasoning: treatment refusal protects bodily integrity by permitting a person to decline intervention. Physician-assisted suicide asks the Constitution to immunize another actor’s affirmative provision of lethal means from state regulation. Neither *Cruzan* nor general statements about personal autonomy establish that entitlement, and the relevant legal tradition has treated assistance in suicide as subject to prohibition. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-702.pdf))

Washington may rationally account for diagnostic error, coercion, depression, unequal access to care, pressure on vulnerable patients, medical ethics, and difficulty policing the line between voluntary assistance and abuse. Rational review does not require certainty that every feared harm will occur. The prohibition also leaves Washington and other States free to adopt a different policy through legislation. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-702.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not permit forced treatment, override competent refusal or valid advance directives, criminalize good-faith pain relief merely because death is foreseen, or authorize use of the assisted-suicide prohibition to deny lawful palliative care. It does not decide a materially narrower application in which another independently protected interest is established.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand.

Public role: authors the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone precisely identifies the claimed affirmative entitlement, distinguishes it from bodily noninterference, declines to recognize it as fundamental, and sustains Washington’s law under rational-basis review.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor, joined in part by Ginsburg and Breyer, emphasizes that a dying patient retains a constitutionally significant interest in effective pain relief. Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer reserve narrower applications while agreeing that the broad right recognized below cannot stand.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Cruzan* is applied and distinguished. Its treatment-refusal principle remains fully operative but does not create a constitutional right to obtain another person’s intentional assistance in causing death.

The decision creates the first controlling simulated rule concerning physician-assisted suicide.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, unanimity, principal five-Justice lineup, and separate-writing structure match the historical decision. Stone authors the Court opinion in place of the historical Chief Justice and uses the controlled text, structure, precedent, function, and relevant-tradition formulation. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-702.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for Washington’s law, the claim asserted, treatment-refusal distinction, legitimate state interests, judgment, and historical lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-702.pdf))
