### Printz v. United States; Mack v. United States, 521 U.S. 898 (1997)

Argued December 3, 1996 | Decided June 27, 1997

From: consolidated review of Ninth Circuit judgments sustaining the Brady Act’s interim commands to state and local chief law-enforcement officers. The Act required those officers to receive purchaser forms, make reasonable efforts to determine whether handgun receipt or possession would violate law, and perform related notice and record-handling functions until the federal background-check system became operational. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-898.pdf))

Question presented: whether Congress may compel state and local executive officers, because of their state offices, to administer an interim federal regulatory program and which portions of the scheme remain operative after the compulsory commands are removed.

Controlling simulated law before decision: surviving *New York v. United States* prohibits federal commands that States enact or administer federal policy. Simulated *Voting Rights Coalition v. Wilson* permits federal-election registration duties through state offices only because the Elections Clause expressly assigns Congress authority over federal-election procedure.

#### Judgment

| Docket | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Printz v. United States*, No. 95-1478 | Reversed and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Enjoin enforcement of the compulsory state-officer commands within the lawful scope of the action. |
| *Mack v. United States*, No. 95-1503 | Reversed and remanded, 5-4 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Enjoin enforcement of the materially identical commands within the lawful scope of the action. |

##### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The background-check command and the associated duty to accept completed purchaser forms may not be enforced against unwilling state or local officers. Duties whose operation presupposes mandatory officer participation, including destruction of forms and provision of reasons following an officer determination, are inoperative as to nonparticipating officers.

The invalid commands are severable from federal administration, regulation directed to private dealers and purchasers, information sharing, and voluntary state cooperation. The Court expresses no view on every application of the independent private-party provisions because no dealer or purchaser is before it.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas | Supports reversal and remand in both dockets | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes voluntary state participation and lawful cooperative programs |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Identifies additional questions concerning the substantive scope of federal firearms authority |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Would affirm | Concludes that the Necessary and Proper Clause permits limited use of state officers to implement valid federal regulation |
| Dissent | Souter | No other Justice | Would affirm | Relies on founding-era materials supporting federal use of state officers |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens | Would affirm | Relies in part on comparative federal practice |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Mandatory state-executive administration

Holding: outside a constitutionally assigned source-specific exception, Congress may not command state or local executive officers, as officers of their States, to administer or enforce a federal regulatory program.

Operative rule: Congress may regulate private actors directly, preempt conflicting state law, create federal offices, enact generally applicable rules that reach state employees as persons, contract with willing States, obtain voluntary assistance, and attach lawful conditions to federal funds. It may not transfer federal program administration to unwilling state executives by direct command.

Reasoning: the Brady provisions do not regulate Printz and Mack as private persons and do not displace a conflicting state rule. They direct state offices to receive federal forms, investigate federal disqualifications, and communicate results for a federal transaction regime. The distinction between policymaking and ministerial implementation does not cure the defect. Either form compels execution of federal law, compromises state autonomy, and obscures political accountability. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-898.pdf))

The importance, efficiency, or temporary character of the federal objective cannot overcome the source limitation. A measure structurally inconsistent with state sovereignty is not made “proper” merely because it assists execution of the commerce power. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-898.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: the holding does not affect state-court obligations under the Supremacy Clause, valid federal preemption, voluntary agreements, information sharing, lawful spending conditions, or duties expressly authorized by another constitutional provision.

Legal basis: constitutional structure, Articles I and II, the Necessary and Proper Clause, and the Tenth Amendment.

##### Severability and remaining federal administration

Holding: the compulsory state-officer commands are severable from federal administration and voluntary cooperation.

Reasoning: removing the commands leaves independent federal objectives and lawful mechanisms capable of operating without transforming state officers into mandatory federal administrators. Dealer and purchaser obligations not imposed on the present plaintiffs are not invalidated through an advisory ruling. The dependent officer duties simply have no operation when an officer does not voluntarily participate. The historical statute likewise separated the temporary officer role from the planned federal background-check system. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-898.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide every future application of the remaining Act or require any State to supply voluntary assistance.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: reverse and remand in both dockets.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the structural distinction between direct federal regulation and compulsory state administration, the rejection of a ministerial-task exception, and the confined severability ruling.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor emphasizes that States remain free to participate voluntarily and that cooperative federal-state law enforcement remains constitutionally available.

Thomas agrees with the anti-commandeering holding and separately questions whether other federal firearms regulations might exceed the commerce power in a properly presented case. That broader proposition is noncontrolling.

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, would hold that Congress may require limited assistance from state officials when the underlying federal regulation is valid and the burden is modest.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*New York v. United States* is applied and clarified. Its prohibition on requiring States to enact or administer federal policy extends to direct commands imposed on state and local executive officers and does not depend on whether the assigned task is characterized as policymaking or ministerial.

Simulated *Voting Rights Coalition v. Wilson* is distinguished. Its result rests on the Elections Clause’s express assignment of authority over federal-election procedure and creates no general power to command state executives.

The decision creates the first controlling simulated general rule against federal commandeering of state executive officers.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated judgment, 5-4 division, author, principal lineup, and separate writings match the historical decision, with Stone occupying the position held by the historical Chief Justice. The simulation expressly states the severability of federal administration and voluntary cooperation while preserving the historical Article III limit concerning independent dealer and purchaser provisions. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-898.pdf))

Research sources: official U.S. Reports for the Brady duties, consolidated posture, anti-commandeering rule, dependent provisions, judgment, and historical opinion lineup. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-521/pdf/USREPORTS-521-898.pdf))

### United States v. Mussari, 95 F.3d 787

Argued May 27, 1997 | Decided June 30, 1997

From: the Ninth Circuit’s reversal of an order dismissing an indictment under the Child Support Recovery Act of 1992. The indictment alleged that Mussari, while residing outside Arizona, willfully failed to pay a state-court support obligation exceeding $5,000 and unpaid for more than one year for children residing in Arizona. The charging period began before enactment of § 228. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/95/787/547814/))

Questions presented: whether Congress may criminalize willful default on a legally fixed support obligation owed to a child residing in another State, whether such direct regulation invades state domestic-relations authority, and what temporal limit governs an indictment spanning pre-enactment conduct.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *United States v. Lopez* rejects federalization of noneconomic local conduct through generalized downstream effects. Simulated *United States v. Harris* permits direct protection of an interstate instrumentality or thing when the offense-specific interstate element performs real constitutional work. Same-term *Printz* distinguishes direct regulation of private persons from commands requiring state officers to administer federal law. Same-term *Pic-A-State Pennsylvania* confirms that Congress may regulate an interstate transaction itself without relying on aggregate effects.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

The Ninth Circuit’s remand for trial proceeds. Any prosecution must be confined to willful failures occurring after § 228 became effective. The Court makes no finding of guilt and does not alter the predicate state support order.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would reverse and direct dismissal | Rejects characterization of passive nonpayment of a state-created family obligation as regulation of a thing in interstate commerce |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Commerce power over an interstate support-payment obligation

Holding: Congress may criminalize the willful post-enactment failure to satisfy a legally fixed child-support obligation owed to a child residing in another State because § 228 directly protects an interstate payment obligation and requires a case-specific interstate residence connection that performs constitutional work.

Operative rule: the statute must operate on an existing support obligation fixed through lawful state process, require different-state residence of obligor and child, and require proof of willful default. The interstate element cannot be a remote historical fact; it must identify an obligation whose satisfaction in legal and ordinary operation calls for value to pass between States.

Reasoning: the offense is not sustained through a chain running from family hardship to public assistance, productivity, insurance, or other generalized national consequences. The object Congress protects is the interstate payment obligation itself. State law creates and defines the debt. Once obligor and child reside in different States, satisfaction ordinarily requires interstate transmission of money or credit, and deliberate frustration operates directly on that interstate obligation. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/95/787/547814/))

The statutory different-state element therefore performs work materially different from the missing commerce element in *Lopez*. Congress need not additionally prove that a particular payment would have traveled by mail or wire when the obligation’s legally required performance is interstate in character.

Application: the indictment alleges that Mussari resided in a different State from his children and willfully failed to satisfy an Arizona support order meeting § 228’s duration and amount requirements. Those allegations are sufficient to proceed to trial. Ability to pay, willfulness, the continuing force of the order, and every other offense element remain for adjudication.

Limits and questions left open: the decision does not create a federal domestic-relations code, reach purely intrastate nonpayment, dispense with the statutory mental state, or permit federal revision of custody or support terms.

Legal basis: Commerce Clause and 18 U.S.C. § 228.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s opinion, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, supplies an eight-Justice majority.

##### Tenth Amendment and domestic-relations authority

Holding: § 228’s direct regulation of the private obligor neither commandeers a State nor transfers domestic-relations adjudication to the federal court.

Reasoning: the statute commands no state legislature, court, or executive officer to administer a federal program. Arizona creates and may modify the support obligation through its own law. The federal tribunal takes the operative state order as the predicate and determines only the federal statutory elements. The domestic-relations limitation associated with diversity jurisdiction does not withdraw an otherwise valid congressional power. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/95/787/547814/))

Limits and questions left open: a federal court may not determine custody, parentage, or the amount of support, disregard a lawful state modification, or manufacture a predicate obligation that state law does not supply.

Legal basis: Commerce Clause, Tenth Amendment, and 18 U.S.C. § 228.

##### Pre-enactment failures

Holding: § 228 may punish only willful failures occurring after its October 25, 1992 effective date.

Operative rule: earlier arrears may establish the existence, age, or amount of the predicate obligation and may supply otherwise admissible background. They cannot themselves constitute offense conduct or support punishment.

Reasoning: the indictment’s allegation beginning in 1988 cannot enlarge a federal offense that did not yet exist. The remand must therefore separate post-enactment willful nonpayment from the pre-enactment history. Section 228 was added by the Child Support Recovery Act on October 25, 1992. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/link/uscode/18/228?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Limits and questions left open: the Court does not decide evidentiary treatment of particular earlier defaults or whether the Government can prove every element during the lawful charging period.

Legal basis: Ex Post Facto Clause, due-process notice principles, and § 228’s effective date.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the direct-interstate-obligation rationale, the distinction between private regulation and commandeering, the preservation of state control over family status, and the post-enactment limitation.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas concludes that a state-created support debt does not become a thing in interstate commerce merely because creditor and debtor reside in different States. In his view, passive nonpayment remains local noneconomic conduct, and the residence element supplies only a jurisdictional label rather than the direct connection required by *Lopez*.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*United States v. Lopez* is applied and clarified. Generalized social and economic consequences remain insufficient, but a statute may directly protect a legally operative interstate payment obligation through an element identifying the parties’ different-state relationship.

*United States v. Harris* is extended from direct protection of a transportation instrumentality to a legally fixed obligation whose required performance is itself interstate. The decision preserves *Harris*’s requirement that the offense-specific interstate element perform real constitutional work.

Same-term *Printz* is distinguished. Section 228 regulates the private obligor and commands no state officer to administer federal law.

Same-term *Pic-A-State Pennsylvania* is consistent with the result because both decisions sustain regulation of the interstate transaction itself rather than relying on attenuated aggregate effects.

#### Historical Comparison

There was no historical Supreme Court merits decision. The simulation affirms the Ninth Circuit’s judgment but narrows its reasoning by rejecting generalized economic consequences and by requiring the statutory different-state element to identify an interstate payment obligation in legal operation. It also makes the post-enactment charging limit express. The Ninth Circuit historically reversed and remanded in a unanimous opinion by Judge Noonan. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/95/787/547814/))

Research sources: the Ninth Circuit opinion for the indictment, statutory text, Commerce Clause rationale, domestic-relations objection, panel, and judgment; the district-court opinions for the corrected dismissal and reconsideration history; and the United States Code for § 228’s enactment date. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/95/787/547814/))
